DIALOGUES 


PROPHECY. | 


VOL, IIl. 


LONDON : 


JAMES NISBET, BERNERS STREET. 


MDCCC XXVIII. 


PREFACE. 


(Extract from a MS.) 
a 


I po not know what feelings may be awakened 
in the minds of others, or what lessons others 
may be disposed to learn, or what interest of any 
kind others may take in these enquiries which 
we make into the purposes of God in His deal- 
ings with His church: but on my own mind, the 
most powerful feeling is that of shame, blushing 
and burning shame, that I should so long have 
remained ignorant, and am still so ignorant, of the 
great mystery of the providence of God over His 
church in times past, present, and tocome. It 
seems to me as if I-were awaking out of a state 
of total indifference, yea, of practical atheism, 
as to what God is doing and has been doing 
with His church. Have we not been all busy, 
looking at the mighty events which He has been 
doing around us? and what He hath been doing 
for us and in the midst of us? speculating like 
politicians, arguing like selfish and ungodly 
lookers-on, without feeling that we have any 
care, or that our God hath any care or concern 
in the whole matter? Whereas it is His doing, and 
B 2 


4 
it is all done by Him for the sake of His church. 
And how has this indifference to His working 
in the world for the church crept upon us, if 
it be not from undervaluing the study of His 
former doings in the world and in the church, 
which have been all written and interpreted by 
the Holy Ghost for our learning, that we, through 
patience and comfort of the Scriptures, might 
have hope? And what has been the conse- 
quence of such indifference to the outward ob- 
servation of the way in which God rules among 
the nations, and how He judges in the great 
congregation? This has been the consequence, 
that we have almost forgotten that there is a 
church at all ; that we have lost the faculty of dis- 
tinguishing it from the apostacy. Christ, and the 
believer; that is all; Christ in the flesh, and Christ 
in us—this is the whole substance of our theo- 
logy, theoretical and practical. These are all the 
practical ideas, if I may call them ideas, but they _ 
are, the former a selfish interest—the latter the 
spirit, the bustling spirit of worldly business, 
= which filleth, possesseth, and ruleth over all 
things. But is that: all? No: there lacketh 
the Holy Spirit; and the ‘church, which is 
his temple; the. election according to grace, 
which is the body of Christ; and the out- 
ward visible church, against which wrath is 
heaped up, and horrid judgments written. The — 
communion of saints and the holy catholic church 


5 


are grown to be dead letters in the creed ; their 
meaning is understood, their comfort felt,no more. 
And will churches ever thus be built? will the 
sweet influence of love thus descend like dew on 
Hermon? will a national church ever thus be re- 
formed, or a foul apostacy overthrown? Never, 
while the world lasts; never, while the word of 
God is faithful; never, while the Holy Spirit 
worketh through the appointed ordinance and 
fellowship of a catholic church. Now I feel no less 
a zeal stirring up my soul to search into God’s 
mystery of Providence by His church and in the 
world, than the desire of redeeming the mi- 
nistry of the word from a deep blot, which I feel 
it to underlie, and you from a state of igno- 
rance, in which I make no doubt you are 
willing to confess yourselves to be; and I 
believe that the Lord will eminently bless our 
labours herein, if we labour together in the Lord, 
waiting for His Holy Spirit, and watching after 
the signs of HIS COMING. | 
Hence, also, arises our little profit and 
small progress in the present work of the 
Holy Spirit, because, until we can discern the 
outward, we never shall discern the inward; 
there must be an outward before there is an 
inward, a visible before there is a spiritual. 
There. was a world created before Adam was 
formed to.see it all reflected in his own under- 
standing: the Saviour was revealed in promise, 


6 


in type, in priesthood, in royalty, in flesh, before 
the Spirit could work any fruit of righteousness 
in the soul: and it is in the act of faithfully 
beholding, and deeply meditating, and religiously 
observing these in the ordinances of the visible 
temple, that we are transformed into the like- 
ness of Him after whom, and to shew forth 
whom they all are fashioned. So until we study, 
and understand, and can give some account of 
the Lord’s dealings with His church, His choosing 
her of His own free grace, His leading and guid- 
ing her in righteousness, after she has been 
fairly set out by His mighty power, His after- 
wards leading her into temptation, in order to 
reveal the wickedness that she hath contracted 
by her unbelief and disobedience, His chasten- 
ing her with the rod of His fatherly wrath, His 
hedging up the way that she might not utterly 
run into wild apostacy,-His rejection of her, His 
cutting of her wholly off from being His church, 
and, after very long ages, Hisreturning to her with 
the warmth of His -first love, that none of His 
promises to His chosen ones might fail; I say, 
until all these His dealings with the outward 
visible- church be understood, meditated on, 
treated of, and delightfully entertained in dis- 
course, it will never happen that we shall be 
able to learn His dealings with us as individuals, 
of which the former is but the outward lesson, 
written large and legible, that the wayfaring 


7 


man, though a fool, may not err therein. If not 
able to read this, how think we to read the events 
of our own experience? if not able to distinguish 
the dealings of God by His church and the world, 
how think to be able to descry the dealings of 
His Holy Spirit with the spiritual and the natu- 
ral man within? we do utterly err, not knowing 
the Scriptures nor the ways of God. Hence 
such dark-lantern stumblings upon the wild 
mountains of fancy, when they undertake to 
discourse of experience; or, I should say, hence 
the rudeness of the attempt they make, to ex- 
plain the work of the Spirit ; their frames, their 
feelings, their sensation, and all their selfish 
furniture of the churches. How can it be other- 
wise? They know not Babylon from Jerusalem, 
nor care to know. They know not the mystery 
of His dealings with His church, how can they 
know His dealings with an individual of the 
church? They cannot with the telescope of His 
word discern the stars, how shall they expect to 
pry into the pores of their own skin? In good 
truth, the days have grown sick, and we are grown 
sick in the days; the atmosphere is unwholesome, 
and we are meagre skeletons of Christians. 
But it was not always so: it was never so in 
the Protestant Churches till within these latter 
years of what they burlesque by calling it a 
revival. The fathers who preached to our fa- 
thers- were great men, very great in their kind. 


8 


‘They could take up a soul, and lead him into 
Egypt, and shew his deliverance thence, his 
wilderness condition, his way by the delectable 
mountain into the Holy Land; they could 
shut him up in Babylon, and deliver him by 
the conquering Lord; and shew him the signs © 
of his calling and election. throughout, shew - 
him the wisdom, the necessity of the discipline. 
Witness, as an example of this, the Pilgrim’s 
Progress, by no means the perfection of that 
kind, which was carried much higher among the 
powerful preachers of the pure times of the Re- 
formed church. But now they have lost the very 
ken of a reprobation in the church, and almost of 
an election; and so they have lost the knowledge 
within themselves of a flesh to be crucified, be- 
cause it is cursed in Adam; and of a Spirit to 
be glorified, because it is quickened by Christ : 
and they talk and prattle about faith, and simple 
faith, like the merest children, seeing not, nor 
caring to see, the glorious Trinity and Their 
offices in salvation. And shall these things con- 
tinue in the church? shall they sanctify their 
_ darkness and call it light? shall they enumerate 
their abominations, and call them holiness? The 
Lord forbid: the Head of the Church forbid: 
the Holy Spirit forbid: ye ministers of the word 
forbid : ye saints of God forbid. We should there- 
fore labour most diligently to understand the out- 
ward that we may understand the inward, fer we 


9 


never shall be able to discourse of personal ex- 
perience, until we are able to discourse of the 
experience of the church; yea, and you shall 
never be able to bear to hear of, nor to under- 
stand the one, until you are able to bear to hear 
of, and to understand the other. Let the church, 
therefore, bear with these enquiries at the divine 
oracles, and be not impatient of our researches, 
as are the violent excitable religious world; but 
as men who desire to drink deep and largely into 
the purposes of God, I pray you to search 
the word for yourselves. 

Especially, I entreat you devoutly to con- 
sider the mystery of the cause of the Lord’s 
rejection of His chosen people: and mark how 
the ten tribes undervalued His royal predeces- 
sors of David’s line, in whose loins He then was; 
consider this, I say, lest you make light of His 
royal function, and, cherishing the notion of a 
kingdom in the spiritual Gentile dispensation, 
should commit the same offence. For know 
assuredly He is as jealous of His honour to 
come from this time forward, as He was of His 
honour to come from that time forward; and 
that the Gentile church shall be cut off as 
Israel. was, for their rejection of the pro- 
mise of Christ to come in His kingdom. I 
would save as many as will hear me, from 
the awful indifference into which the church 
has fallen to the Advent of the Lord as 


10 


king. I would save my own soul. Oh! I 
would be jealous for the abused prerogative of 
my King. Oh! I would be jealous for the 
honour of the promises of God. I would 
arouse the church from its stupefaction. I 
would shew her the fearful judgment of God. 
in the time past, upon His beloved people. I 
would shew her also His grace, His long-suffer- 
ing, in order that we may forsake our papal 
adulteries with the kings of the world, and with 
the plebeians of the world, and keep our affec- 
tions for Him that is to come; and I labour 
in hope, assured the Lord will bless the la- 
bour ; yea, I will serve the Lord, and the Lord 
will save us in the judgment. I will take 
pledges of him. I will preach, as Noah did, 
his wrath to be poured out; and as for Noah’s 
sake He saved Noah’s family, so, for the sake 
of the preacher's faithful testimony, He may save 
the preacher’s flock. He will, if you reject not : 
but, oh! and if you reject having been warned, 
having been instructed, then it will be more 
tolerable for others than for you. The Lord 
open our eyes; the Lord open our ears; the 
Lord fill us all with the spirit of watchfulness, 
for BEHOLD—HE COMETH—AS A THIEF. 


DIALOGUES 


ON 


Prophecy. 


CONVERSATION THE EIGHTH. 


What is revealed concerning the Order of Events connected with 
the Second Coming of the Lord. 


Philalethes.—In one of the conversations which 
we held last year upon the present hopes and 
expectations of the Christian church, I asked 
you at what point of prophetic time you thought 
we had now arrived, and you replied that you 
considered the period described by the sixth 
vial to be that under which we are now living : 
give me leave to enquire whether. your subse- 
quent observations have tended to strengthen or 
to weaken that impression ? 

Anastasius.—Instead of answering that ques- 
tion directly, let me call to your recollection the 
simple facts which have occurred since that con- 
versation was held, and do you draw what infe- 
rence from them you please, bearing in mind, at 
the same time, the explanation then given. One 
of the most upright and conscientious ministers 


12 


this country ever possessed, virtually no more. 
The Tory Administration saute en Tair. Mr. 
Canning dead. An administration of liberals 
formed under Mr. Robinson. An English army 
on the Continent. A war with Turkey brought 
about in violation of the laws of nations, of pub- 
lic faith, and justice, by an act of this country, 
which has ever before been the grand asserter 
and defender of that international code. A vic- 
- torious Russian army penetrated to the heart of 
Persia. Whoever had ventured to predict any 
one of these events, last year, much less all, 
would have been thought, with justice, to be 
little better than a madman. 

Philalethes.—What event do you next antici- 
pate? | | 

Anastasius.—“ I am no prophet, neither a 
prophet’s son,” Amos vil. 14, but I endeavour 
to search the word of God, and the result of 
those researches I have no objection to lay 
before you. This will not give us much insight 
into the minutie of politics, but will afford us 
` all information necessary to our individual esta- 
blishment in the truth. Mede said the sixth 
vial would destroy the Turkish power, p. 529. 
Mr. Faber, in A.D. 1815, said, “the next pri- 
mary event in prophecy will be the downfall 
of the Ottoman empire, which will begin to be 
dismembered as soon as the sixth vial shall be- 
gin to flow.” Mr. Frere says, in the same year, 
“ The sixth vial foretells the fall of the Ottoman 
empire.” Sophron said to me, above a year ago, 
“I shall lay on my oars, till I see what the Lord 


13 


will do with Turkey ; for if I am right in my 
conjectures, the sixth vial must describe a rup- 
ture in that quarter.” 

Philalethes.—But surely there must be some 
definite order in the events which are indicated 
as to take place before the final consummation 
of all things. | 

Anastasius.—Undoubtedly there is: but before 
we can attempt to arrange the events revealed in 
the order in which they are to occur, we must 
distinguish the different parties interested in the 
coming of the Lord. These are four at the least. 
i. The Elect Spiritual Church ; ii. The Apostate 
Christian Church ; iii. The Jews; iv. The Hea- 
then. The discursive prophecies speak some- 
times of circumstances which are common to 
all, and sometimes of such as are applicable to 
one only. | 

Philalethes.—As the first is the most import- 
ant, I wish you would tell me what you think is 
the next event which is to be anticipated for the 
church. 

Anastasius.— Assuming, first, that we are 
living under the sixth vial, and, secondly, in the 
words of Mede, though not for his reasons, 
p. 585, that “ the seventh vial is coincident 
with the seventh trumpet,” you will observe, 
in Rev. xvi. 15, after the sixth vial is men- 
tioned, and before the seventh vial is named, 
there occurs this remarkable notice, “ Behold ! 
—I come—as a thief.” Here we have first a 
call to look around us; Ið! secondly, an an- 
nunciation that-the object to be looked for is 


14 


the coming of the Lord; thirdly, a description 
of the mode of his coming, viz. as a thief; un- 
suspected, unobserved, at a time, in a manner, 
and at a place that he is not expected. The 
Apostle, however, explains to the Thessalonians, 
i. v.2—8, that the Lord only comes as a thief 
in the night to them who are in darkness re- 
specting his coming, but not in this manner to 
them who are of the day. The figure of coming 
as a thief is mentioned as the character of our 
Lord’s personal advent, both by Peter, and by 
himself in Luke xu. 39, 40, and it would be 
reasonable to conclude, from these circumstances 
alone, that the coming of the Lord occurs at the 
period when the notice is given, namely, be- 
tween the sixth and seventh vials. 

Philalethes.— Although Mede is very good au- 
thority, yet I should be glad to have your own 
reason for adopting the idea that the last trumpet 
and the last vial are synchronous. 

Anastasius.—My reason for thinking Mede is 
right may be expressed very shortly, thus : 

The sixth seal opens, and there is a great 
earthquake, followed by the great day of the 
wrath of the Lamb, Rev. vi. 12—17. 

The seventh trumpet sounds, ushering in the 
‘day of wrath, and is preceded by a great earth- 
quake, Rev. xi. 13—18. Hence the sixth trum- 
pet contains the earthquake, which is followed 
by the day of wrath. But the sixth seal also 
contains this earthquake. Ergo, the sixth seal 

and sixth trumpet synchronize. - 
= The seventh trumpet reaches down to the 


15 


grand consummation, Rev. xi. 15. Hence the 
seventh seal must fall within this period. Ergo, 
seventh seal and seventh trumpet synchronize. 

Under the seventh trumpet there is an earth- 
quake and great hail, Rev. xi. 19. 

Under the seventh vial there is an savtlquale 
and great hail, Rev. xvi. 18. 21. 

Ergo, seventh trumpet and seventh vial syn- 
chronize. Ergo, all the three sevens — 
nize. 

Isocrates.—It is ‘revealed in 1 Cor. xv. 25, that 
“ we shall be changed at the last Trump.” In 
1 Thess. iv. 16, we are told, that “ the Lord 
himself shall descend from heaven with a shout, 
with the voice of the Archangel, and with the 
TRUMP of God; and the dead in Christ shall 
rise first: then we which are alive, and remain, 
shall be caught up together with them in the 
clouds to meet the Lord in the air.” That the 
trumpets in these two passages are identical 
will not be disputed ; but the word /as¢ must have 
reference to some series of trumpets: no such 
series, however, occurs in the New Testament, 
except the seven enumerated in the Apocalypse, 
the last of which has been considered by most 
commentators as synchronous with the last vial. 
It is probable, therefore, that the indication in 
Rev. xvi. is given before the seventh vial, to 
point out that the taking up of the elect occurs 
at the commencement of that vial, and not at 
the end of it. | 

Anastasius.—Under the sixth vial we are like- 
wise informed that three unclean spirits go 


16 


forth to gather the kings of the whole world to 
the war of the great day of God Almighty, which 
war must take place during the seventh vial. In 
the three great judgments with which mankind 
have already been visited, and which are referred 
to as types of that which is to destroy the Gen- 
tile apostacy at the. end of the times of the 
Gentiles, namely, those on the world by the 
deluge, on Sodom by fire, and on Jerusalem by 
the Roman armies; the Lord, faithful to his 
‘promises of preservation to his people, provided 
an ark, a Zoar, and a Pella respectively, for their 
reception. It has therefore very naturally been 
a subject of anxious enquiry, where is the spot 
now to which the church must resort, to escape 
the impending judgments. No person, however, 
has been able to find it; -but as it is not analo- 
gous to the Lord’s former dealings, nor to his 
gracious promises at all times to his church, that 
she should not be preserved, the not being able 
to find a refuge appointed for her on the earth 
seems to be an additional confirmation of the 
idea, that all his chosen ones will be saved by. 
being caught up to meet him. 

© Philalethes.—Can any argument be fairly | 
grounded upon the circumstance of no commen- 
tator having hitherto been able to ascertain a 
Pella? 

Anastasius. —Certainly not aiiiar as at 
the best it is but a negative argument; but 
coupled with others, it is. one to some 
weight. = | 
Sophron. —The events. which typify, seem to 


17 


hold the same language as the passages which 
predict, the second Advent. Our Lord comes as a 
thief in the night; that is, when the whole house 
is at rest and quiet. The world shall be saying 
peace and safety, 1 Thess. v.3. The buying, sel- 
ling, planting, marrying, &c. until the day when 
the Son of Man is revealed, are images of peace, 
and not of warfare. In like manner the sleep- 
ing and slumbering of the virgins denote rest 
and quiet, not alarm and discord. The types 
also indicate the previous safety of the elect ; 
it was not till after Noah was secure in the ark 
that the antediluvian atmosphere was overcast, 
and the fountains of the great deep broken up. 
Lot must get into the little city before the angel 
of Jehovah could do any thing; and the sun 
was risen on the earth with all the beauty and 
serenity of an eastern summer, when Lot entered 
Zoar. The disciples left Jerusalem before the 
assault was made upon it. There seems also 
to be a striking analogy in this respect between 
the first and second advent. Before our Lord’s 
nativity, the Roman earth had been torn and 
agitated by civil wars, proscriptions, &c. and 
just before Christ was born, the temple of Janus 
was shut. In like manner, now, all Europe has 
been agonized to its centre by the wars of the 
French Revolution, and peace has for twelve 
years succeeded those wars. The midnight cry 
which awakens the virgins, can be nothing but 
a company of preachers, proclaiming the coming 
of the Bridegroom, and that cry has been 
made. | 
VOL, II. c 


18 


-Philalethes—How can it be said that that cry 
has been made by a company of preachers when 
so few of them believe it? 

Sophron.—I could name, nevertheless, above. 
one hundred pulpits in London, and various 
parts of England, in which the cry has been 
clearly and loudly made ; and there is scarcely. 
an individual in the kingdom that has not heard 
of it. Scotland too has been warned, and some 
of the boldest, most dutiful, and most truly. 
affectionate of her sons have ventured to say, 
“ Scotland is asleep,” notwithstanding her motto, 
Nemo me impune lacessit. 

Philalethes.—How can Paul, in writing to the 
Corinthians and Thessalonians, allude to a series 
of trumpets in the Apocalypse, which book was 
not written until after his death? l 

Anastasius.—Y our objection assumes as proved, 
a question which is adhuc sub judice. There are 
many allusions in the New Testament to things 
described in the Apocalypse, and on this internal 
evidence, as well as for some other reasons, Sir 
I. Newton, Michaelis, and Grotius, contend that 
it was written before the Epistles; their argu- 
ments have been very powerfully stated by Mr. 
Tulloch ; but it is not necessary to our present 
purpose to enter into them. Upon the ordinary 
supposition, however, that the Apocalypse was 
written last, the difficulty of reconciling the al- 
lusions made to it in books composed prior to its 
publication, is very great: for example, in the 
Epistle to the Hebrews, Paul speaks of the Pa- 
triarchs who looked for ruv rsç Oeusdise exsoay row, 


19 


the city having the foundations, xi. 10, which ei- 
ther must refer to the city with the twelve foun- 
dations of Rev. xxi. 2, Tne TWoAEwC EXOV Oeuedts¢ Swoexa, 
or there is no clue to the meaning of the expres- 
Sion to be found. The same may be said of 
“« Jerusalem which is above” of Gal. iv. 26, and 
Several other passages. As many allusions 
are made in the earlier prophets to subjects 
which cannot be well understood without refe- 
rence to later writers, who have discoursed more 
fully upon the same, so the Holy Spirit may 
have over-ruled a writer in the earlier part of the 
New Testament to make allusions to topics which 
were to be more completely developed in a sub- 
sequent portion of the Divine Record. In ar- 
guing on this matter we are too apt to lose sight 
of the Holy Spirit, who is the real writer, and 
consider the penmen rather than Him who sug- 
gested, controlled, and repressed all that they 
said, and all that they withheld. 
Philalethes.—But is it not said in the parable 
of the tares and the wheat, “gather first the 
tares, and bind them in bundles to burn.” 
Anastasius.—This parable deserves accurate 
examination. It is very clear, from many pas- 
sages, that there are two judgments, one under 
the figure of harvest, and the second under that 
of vintage. This parable relates to the time of 
the ovvreàsia rs awvoc, the completion of the Gen- 
tile dispensation. It is evident from Daniel 
vii. 8, 9. 25, 26, that the Ancient of Days begins 
to sit at the end of the papal period, or 1260 
days. Rev. xiv. 14, seems to describe the same 
c2 


20 

sitting as Daniel; and, after this, immediately 
follows the harvest, from which it would appear, 
that the harvest begins to be reaped at the end 
of the 1260 days. If this be correct, the burn- 
ing of the tares has been going on during the 
last thirty years, and is still going on in the 
Peninsula: as soon as it is completed, the 
wheat will be gathered into the garner, and the 
earth left for the unmingled judgment of the 
vintage. The gathering of the wheat is ex- 
plained by our Lord to be, the righteous shining 
forth as the sun in the kingdom of their Father, 
verse 43, which can be nothing but the. first 
resurrection, and changing of the saints. Com- 
pare also Dan. xii. 2, 3, and Matt. xvii. 2. 
Thus the six first vials are partial judgments, 
applying to that part of the process of the har- 
vest which consists of burning the tares: after 
the sixth, ‘‘ Behold, I come: blessed,” &c. which 
is the gathering of the wheat, and then the 
seventh, which is universal, that is, the vintage. 
. Philalethes—But if the elect were caught up 
to heaven at the commencement of those trou- 
bles, there would be no room for the exercise of 
that caution which ought ‘to be induced by the 
warming given respecting Lot’s wife. 

- Anastasius.—We are not told what occasioned 
her looking back: it seems, however, that it 
was more than a mere turning round of the 
body, and that her regrets were for something 
that she had left behind; and when it is re- 
membered that these regrets may have been 

occasioned _by. sympathy and affection for her 


21 


sons-in-law, who had mocked at the warnings 
of her husband, and for other kindred who were 
left in the devoted city, there is surely abundant

visible sign” of the invisible resurrection of the

church was gathered together in one nation, and

visible was confined to one nation, and one spot 
also. Now that the visible is scattered over many 
nations, and the Jews. dispersed, existing only 
as individuals, and not as communities, the 
invisible church is dispersed also, existing only 
as individuals taken here and there, but not in 
any great number at any one place. But if the 
resurrection of the saints do not take place at 
the time of the restoration, or national resurrec-

dow it forth.

Anastasius.—The saints are called “ the salt of 
the earth;” that is, the preservative by which. 
alone the corruption and dissolution of the 
whole political body is prevented. As soon as 
they shall have been caught up to meet the 
Lord in the air, there seems to be no further 
reason for the preservation of the present frame- 
work of human society, which is only kept in 
order to preserve the church, so that the very 
elements which compose it shall be dissolved, 
as well as the powers which rule it melt away, 
2 Pet. iii. 10. Thus mankind, being left to 
themselves, without the restraining grace of the 
Holy Spirit, will quickly give way to all their 
bad passions, which being then uncontrolled, 
will produce the greatest misery, and end in 
their mutually destroying one another. In 
Rev. xvii. which gives the details of the judg- 
ment on Babylon, the saints, “called, chosen, 


27 


and faithful,” appear, in ver. 14, already with

when Christ, having appeared to take to Him- 
self His kingdom, to shew Himself as God of 
War, and having finished the work of Saviour, 
that all within the confines of Christendom, who 
shall not have heartily accepted Him as their 
alone salvation, shall be found to have sinned 
against the Holy Ghost, which, in His church, 
had been testifying of Him from His birth at 
Bethlehem to that hour. The testimony to 
Messiah being the Son of Man, which was 
rejected by the Jews, might be repaired by the 
individuals of that nation receiving the testi- 
mony of the Spirit, and washing out their first 
sin in his blood. But for the rejection of the 
testimony of the Spirit to him there is no 
remedy, no salvation, no way of escaping ever- 
lasting death. Then will be seen whether the 
zeal which God’s children have manifested to 
proclaim the name of Jesus as the sole means 
of salvation from eternal damnation, and to 
testify against the sinful maxims and practices 
of the world, has been the work of the Holy 
Ghost in them, or the work of the unclean 
spirits of fanaticism, and methodism, and de- 
lusion, that has ever been imputed to them; 
then will the Lord make retribution for all the 
“cruel mockings,” and all “the hard speeches — 
which ungodly sinners have spoken against

Him” in His saints. Then it is, after these are 
caught up, that those who are left will call 
upon the rocks to cover them, to hide them from 
the wrath of the Lamb.

Philalethes.—When I began this conversation, 

I had no conception that we stood upon the 
brink of so important an era as you have now 
unfolded; and I am so overawed by the idea, 
that I must entreat your pardon, if I appear 
troublesome by endeavouring to sift this matter a 
little deeper: and let me refer to one of our 


30 


former conversations in which yóu gave me an 
explanation of the vials, and enquire on what 
ground commentators have concluded, in the 
words of Mede, that the sun, moon, stars, &c. 
signified ceelum politicum, and not those material 
luminaries themselves? I should state candidly, 
that my object in putting this question is not 
with reference to any interpretation of the vials, 
but with reference to the passage where our 
Lord says, “ there shall be signs in the sun, and 
in the moon, and in the stars,” &c. 
Anastasius. — All commentators have been 
obliged to consider the heavens, and their sun, 
moon, and stars, as symbolical, ex necessitate rei. 
Mede quotes the Hebrew commentators, p. 448, 
466, 614, to shew that they understood these ex- 
pressions in that way; and Daubuz, in his 
Dictionary, gives several instances of heathen 
writers using the same figures. Thus the hea- 
then king of Babylon is represented by Isaiah, 
xiv. 13, 14, as threatening to subdue all the 
world in the following words: “I will ascend 
into heaven, I will exalt my throne above the 
stars of God, I will sit also upon the mount of 
the congregation in the sides of the north. I 
will ascend above the height of the clouds,” &c. 
But I will lay before you such passages of 
Scripture, as have convinced me that those ex- 
pressions are to be taken symbolically. The. 
first place in which we find them is in Joseph’s 
dream, Gen. xxxvii. 9, where Jacob instantly 
interprets the obeisance made by the sun, moon, 
and eleven stars, to mean himself, his wife, and 


31 


his eleven sons, clearly, therefore, establishing 
their symbolical import. The next is in Isa. v. 
being the conclusion of chap. ii. iii. and iv. 
which contain a complete prophecy in them- 
selves, relating to the Jewish church under its 
well-known emblem of the vineyard; wherein 
the judgment, by means of the Babylonian or 
Roman armies, is declared to be coming upon 
it: in the last verse it is written, “if one look 
into the land, behold darkness and sorrow, and 
the light is darkened in the heavens thereof.” 
Here also the word heavens, must mean celum 
politicum, not only as the event has shewn, but 
also because the limitation of the term to a par- 
ticular land, abundantly proves. In Isa. xiii. 
is contained the judgment upon Babylon: in 
v. 10, 13, we have the same figure, which, if 
literal, was not fulfilled at the destruction of 
ancient Babylon ; but, as by ver. 19. we seem 
to be carried forward to the mystical Babylon of 
these days, no inference can be drawn on either 
side from this passage. In Isa. xxiv. this 
figure occurs at ver. 23, “Then the moon shall 
be confounded, and the sun ashamed, when the 
Lord of Hosts shall reign in Mount Zion,” &c. 
If the expression be construed literally, it is un- 
intelligible; but if construed as the ccelum poli- 
ticum, the legal and ecclesiastical rulers of its 
former polity, the expression is full of propriety 
and force. In Isa. xxxiv. 4, it is declared, that 
“ all the host of heaven shall be dissolved, and 
the heavens shall be rolled together as a scroll ; 
and all their host shall fall down, as the leaf 


Re: 


falleth off from the vine, and as a falling fig 
from a fig tree.” Here also, if taken literally, 

it is not easy to affix a definite idea to the word 
heavens, as contra-distinguished from the hosts 
of heaven; but passing this by, it is not possible 
that the heavenly luminaries should fall down to 
this earth, which is not half their size; and yet 
to fall down can mean nothing else: whereas, if 
taken for the rulers of every kind, and systems in 
which they rule, the figure is clear and beautiful. 

But Isa. lx. is quite conclusive upon the point: 

here the Jewish church herself, at her restora- 

tion, is said to be the light to which the Gentiles. 
shall come: and in verses 19, 20, “the sun 
shall be no more thy light by day, neither for 
brightness shall the moon give light unto thee, 

but the Lord shall be unto thee an everlasting 
light, and thy God thy glory: thy sun shall no 
more go down, neither shall hy moon withdraw 
itself, for the Lord shall be thine everlasting 
light, and the days of thy mourning shall be 
ended :” expressions which it is impossible to 
interpret of any thing but of that state of the 
Jewish kingdom when the Lord Jesus shall be 

both King and Priest upon its throne: particu- 
larly the pronoun ¢hy, limiting the sun spoken 
of to be that which exclusively pertained to the 
land of Judea.

We come next to Joel ii. 10. Although | this

although the material light would be dimmed by

importance in explaining the similar terms in

-Philalethes.—Yet surely you do not think the

_Anastasius.—I think it very probable, that cir- 
cumstances belonging both to the beginning and 
to the end of the millenium, may be blended; as 
we frequently find in other parts of Scripture ; 
and ,this also may furnish a more correct ex- 
planation of the passages which I have laid 
before you from Genesis to the Apocalypse, 
than tying them down either to wholly symboli- 
cal, or wholly literal meaning. On the passage 
in 2 Pet. iii. Mede says, p. 614, We know the 
Scriptures make three heavens, 1. The air, or 
sublunary heaven. 2. The ethereal, or starry 
heaven. 3. The heaven of glory. Every one 
of these heavens have their host, or army. The 


35 
host of the heaven of glory, or third heaven, 
are the angels and blessed spirits: the host of 
the ethereal heaven are the stars and planets: 
the host of the aeriel, or sublunary heaven, are 
either visible, as the clouds ; or invisible, as the 
devil and wicked spirits. Peter’s words import 
that some or all of these heavens shall suffer a 
conflagration at the day of Christ: not all of 
them, for certainly not the heaven of glory, 
nor the ethereal heaven : there remaineth, there- 
fore, onty the sublunary heaven, with their 
saysa, to be the subject of this conflagration. 
He then proceeds to shew that the dissolving is 
for the purpose, not of annihilation, but of puri- 
fying; and that the word passing away, signi- 
fies no greater passing away than that which 
took place at the deluge, (p. 615,) and that the 
invisible host of this sublunary heaven shall, 
by this fiery judgment, be driven into some 
lower place. But I must once more request 
your particular attention to these signs, being 
said to take place before the great and terrible 
day of the Lord. In Luke xxi. 28, our Lord 
tells his followers, (after saying, “ there shall be 
_signs in the sun, and in the moon, and in the 
stars; and upon the earth distress of nations, 
with perplexity, the sea and the waves roaring,’’) 
“ when these things begin to come to pass, then 
look up, and lift up your heads for your re- 
demption draweth nigh:” plainly thereby in- 
dicating, that these phenomena in the heavenly 
powers, were to precede the re-establishment of 
the Jews, as well as the gathering together 
D2 


36 


the elect, which is spoken of by the other two 
Evangelists. There is a very masterly paper 
in the Christian Observer for October 1827, in 
which it is well observed that “the coming of 
the Lord with the clouds of heaven, announced . 
in Matt. xxiv. 30; Mark xiii. 16; and Luke 
XXi. 27, is proved to be the same event as that 
predicted in Dan. vii. 13, 14; Rev. xi. 15; 
2 Thess. ii. 8; Rev. xix. 11—21, from its being

blishment of the Jewish nation, which events 
are, by all the best interpreters of prophecy,

Aristo.—Whilst we agree to the symbolical 
import of the passage in hand, which Mede has 
unfolded with his usual sagacity, Philalethes 
must not suppose that we therefore loosely 
apply all such expressions symbolically ; for 
so far from arguing against the literal accom- 
plishment of Isaiah’s prophecy, I most assu- 
redly believe it will take place, the passage 
where it is predicted having plainly a literal | 
bearing, and forming part of prophecies which 
are not symbolical, but literal, though at times 
highly figurative. Nay more; touching this 
connection between things written both literally 
and symbolically, I believe, that as all things 
which have been literally accomplished under 
the Gospel, were symbolically represented under 
the law, so the things which are symbolically 


37 


written under the present dispensation will be 
literally accomplished under the dispensation 
of mighty power about to commence ; and that 
it is the literal arresting of the spheres, dissolu- 
tion of the host of heaven, and falling of the 
Stars, like the figs off a fig-tree, prophecied by 
Isaiah to take place at some time, which gives 
the language to the'sixth seal of the Apocalypse, 
and which was performed emblematically at - 
the beginning of the French Revolution. And 
this is a general rule, for want of attention to 
which interpreters of the symbolical prophecies 
go astray. In finding, ofttimes in the discursive 
and literal prophecies, the ground-work of the 
symbol, they are apt to suppose that the event 
contained under the symbol must take place 
at the same time with the event contained under 
the letter. Whereas, if this were the case, 
there would have been no need of the sym- 
bolical at all, which would have been merely 
to write darkly and doubtfully, what had been 
already written clearly and expressly. But real 
things being revealed in plain language as about 
to happen at a distant time, it was possible and 
profitable to set forth things more near at hand, 
and to illustrate the intervening space by a 
symbolical history, which should, at the same 
time, give a foretaste of the great events to 
come, whereof it was a shadowy picture. Thus, 
under the Patriarchs, the coming of Shiloh and 
the promised seed being well and plainly re- 
vealed, there was constructed under Moses a 
symbolical, or emblematical, exhibition of the 


38 


spiritual blessings which we now possess; and- 
when His coming in power was postponed, there 
was constructed, for our use during the interval, 
a symbolical representation of the acts and 
acquisitions of power which He should shew for 
His people at His second coming. Therefore 
I am nothing amazed to find, that the exact and 
critical interpretation of the symbolical book 
of the Apocalypse does bring forward the 
events before the time of their literal accom- 
plishment ; that in the symbol the sun becomes 
black as sackcloth of hair, and the moon is 
changed into blood, and the stars of heaven 
fall unto the earth, before it becomes so in the 
letter; and that it has become so in the symbol, 
is proof to me that it shall become so in the 
letter. 

Philalethes—There are then, if I understand 
you rightly, some passages relating to these 
celestial signs, which cannot be understood 
symbolically. 

Anastasius.—It appears to me that Isaiah li. 
6, 8, and Psalm cii. 25—27, relate to the mate- 
rial creation, because the argument, in both 
places, seems to require it. 

_Crito.—It appears to me that much of the 
difficulty which occurs to our friend Philalethes 
arises from the vagueness of ideas which the 
church in general entertains upon the meaning 
of the word first and second coming of the Lord. 
Jesus Christ ; and that she can never be brought 
to a right reception of this all-important truth, 
until she shall have more distinctness upon the 


39- 


subject. Amongst other points of view in which 
these different comings may be considered, one 
is, that the first was to enable him to perform 
his office of priest ; the second to perform that 
of king. It is true that these acts are often 
blended in prophecy; and that during the time 
he sojourned on earth in the first capacity, he 
gave some indication of his future sojourning in 
_ the second. Of this character was his riding into 

Jerusalem, when they cried, ‘‘ Blessed is he that 
cometh in the name of the Lord;” also his 
cleansing the temple of the money changers, 
thereby representing his cleansing of the Chris- 
tian church from the Papists, and all who have 
made a gain of his religion and services, and 
therefore fitly called “the merchants ;” also his 
transfiguration on Mount Tabor, when the Apos- 
tles unconsciously prophecying, like the High 
Priest, pointed to his future tabernacling on the 
earth in ‘that glorious body, which they then saw 
before them. Amongst the prophecies, which 
blend the circumstances of his two comings to- 
gether, may first be cited, that to which he him- 
self refers in Luke iv. 16—21, “The spirit of the 
Lord is upon me, because the Lord hath anointed 
me to preach good tidings unto the meek; he 
hath sent me to bind up the broken hearted, 
to proclaim liberty to the captives, and the 
opening of the prison to them that are bound ; 
to proclaim the acceptable year of the Lord, 
and the day of vengeance of our God,” &c. &c. 
but he stopped before he came to these last 
words, which belong to his second coming. 


40 


Micah v. also contains a double prophecy, 
where, in ver. 1, the Judge of Israel is spoken 
of as smitten with a rod upon the cheek, whilst. 
in ver. 4, he is mentioned as standing and feed- 
ing in the strength of Jehovah, in the majesty of 
the name of the Lord his God. You will find 
similar instances in Zech. xiii. 7—9, and xiv. 
A double coming of Elias is also plainly men- 
tioned in Mal. iv. 5,6. In like manner the mil- 
lenial and eternal state are blended, as in Isa. 
lxv. 17, 18; Dan. vii. 18. xii., which two states _ 
are however accurately distinguished in 1 Cor. 
xv. 22—28. Thus there are three distinct acts 
of judgment, all called the coming of Christ; 
Ist, his judgment on the Jews, Matt. x. 23; 
2d, his judgment on the Gentiles; and 3d, his 
judgment on Satan, and his angels, and the 
‘reprobate, at the end of the millenium. 

Philalethes—Eut are we not to consider that 
the second coming of Christ is a personal 
coming ? 

Anastasius.—If by “ the second coming” of Christ 
be not meant a coming of his person, but only a 
spiritual coming, such as that at Pentecost, or a 
providential coming, sucn as that on Jerusalem, 
it is no second coming at all; and may with 
equal propriety be called a hundredth coming, 
or any other number; so that none of the comings 
enumerated by Crito can be called the second 
coming. This is the grand point of infidelity in 
the church at the present moment; she cannot, 
totidem verbis, deny a second coming of some 
sort, but she contrives, by help of the words 


41 


“ providential” or “spiritual,” or some other 
such, effectually to undermine and destroy all 
the essentials which constitute that coming, the 
second coming of Christ. The threat given to 
Satan was that the seed of the woman should. 
bruise his head. The promise to Abraham was, 
that his seed should have the land in everlasting 
possession. The promise to David was, that 
his seed should sit upon his throne for ever; and 
the promise to Mary was, that the child of her 
womb should sit on the throne of his father 
David for ever. But no spiritual nor providential 
coming can effect this; nor anything but the 
coming again of that same body which was 
born at Bethlehem, lived in Judæa, and was 
crucified at Jerusalem ; according as the angels 
declared in Acts i. 11, this same Jesus shall so 
come in like manner as ye have seen him go; 
and whom Peter, in his first sermon, declared 
that the heavens had received only “until the 
times of the restitution of all things; Acts 
iii. 19, so that it is quite conclusive that any 
other coming than that of the glorified manhood 
of Christ is not, nor cannot be, the second 
coming to raise the bodies of the dead saints, 
which has been held up to the church in all 
ages as the great object of her hope and ex- 
pectation. Whereabouts, Crito, do you think 
we have arrived in the revealed order of these. 
events ? | 

Crito.—I am inclined to think that the sixth 
vial has nearly ceased, and that it has a two- 
fold operation, one in the Eastern and the other 


42. 


in the Western Empire. The preparations for 
final judgment have been made by the labours 
of the Bible and Continental Societies ; and the 
next event which we may expect is that general 
revolution which shall bring forth antichrist in 
a more distinct shape and activity than he has 
hitherto shewn himself. 

Thraso.—It is clearly right to bear in mind 
the distinction which Anastasius pointed out, 
namely, that the order of events varies accord- 
ing to the different objects for which Christ 
comes; and thus we may, in that sense, call 
his coming to the Jews, which the apostle 
speaks of as a “time of refreshing,” Acts ii. 19, 
to them, a different coming from that to the 
Gentiles, to punish their apostacy, although 
taking place at the same period. Thus a spirit 
of repentance in the Jews, and of attention to 
their national expectations, must be among the 
first signs exhibited in the chain of events con- 
nected with their restoration. Whilst these things 
are going on amongst themselves, the servants 
of the Lord, amongst the Gentiles, will be send- 
ing the swift messengers and preparing the 
present of a people scattered and peeled for 
Jehovah Sabaoth, Isa. xviii. ; while, at the same 
time, the kings of the whole world will be mus- 
tering together those forces which are finally to 
perish at Armageddon. As preaching repen- 
tance was the work of Elias in John the Bap- 
tist, so preaching repentance to the Jews must 
be performing that office towards them.

out this time as that in which we are now living. 
When a friend of mine first began, about six

apostacy and mystery of iniquity revealed in 
the New Testament, his preaching excited so 
much opposition in the minds of those who

There is also a striking series of Psalms, from.

Philalethes—The principal difficulty which I 
find in adjusting the events of the Apocalypse to 
the Jewish times and seasons, arises from the 
circumstance of some of those events being con- 
nected with feasts which were fixed to a day, 
(as jubilee,) and some with seasons which cannot 
be so exactly determined, (as harvest, &c.) The 
harvest and vintage of course do not take place 
till the grain is come to maturity, and the clus- 
ters- fully ripe; but this period must vary consider- 
ably in different aspects, and in cold, or in warm, 
seasons. Accordingly, I cannot find any two 
travellers exactly agreeing in the time of harvest, 
or vintage; some protracting the harvest till the 
middle of October, and speaking of clusters on 
the vine even in the middle of November. Nor 
can I find any precise determination of the times 
of harvest and vintage in Scripture. 
Thraso.—This latitude in the seasons, which 
seems to oppose considerable obstacles in our 
way, will be found, on closer examination, to be 
a great beauty in the prophecy, and a great 
mercy in the event; for it prolongs the harvest 
to allow time for the ripening of the backward 


56 


grain; and it delays the vintage of wrath till 
iniquity is fully ripe; and it leaves both seasons 
in that uncertainty which is best calculated to 
produce the watchfulness So ay enjoined 
in Scripture.

Thales.—It has been considered by some, that 
the sea of glass, like chrystal, with fire, of 
Rev. xv. 2., on which the saints are seen to 
stand, represents their situation in the trans- 
parent atmosphere, whilst the fiery judgment is 
going on upon the earth. It is scarcely possible 
for any one who is not old enough to remember 
it, to conceive the rapidity with which the 
events of the French revolution succeeded each 
other. Some idea may be gathered from the 
writings of Burke; and all -now living, who 
remember it, speak of it as a -time of fearful 
dismay. It would seem as if we ought. to 
expect a time of trial now to come on, as 
suddenly as that which came on France. 

Isecrates.—All events, other than those which 
affect the elect exclusively, and which precede 
the second coming of our Lord, must be brought 


about by natural means, so. that the statesman 
may think that they are brought about by se- 
cond causes, otherwise the nations will not be 
taken by surprise; but the first great crash to 
them will be the coming of Jesus Christ in the 
flesh, in like manner as he went away.

18. as seeming to indicate something of the sort,

County of Roscommon, Py Deposition of Mary Anne 


to wit, Burke, late of Ballymoe. 


Deponent declares to the best of her knowledge and belief 
she is twenty-two years old; that about eighteen months ago 
she procured a Bible, and from its perusal and the conversation 
of some persons who conformed to the Protestant Religion, she 
became convinced that the Church of Rome was erroneous ; 
that when her relations found that this change had taken place 
in her mind, they treated her with great severity ; that she at- 
tended some lectures given by the Rev. Mr. Welden, in Bally- 
moe, which further tended to open her mind to the errors of 
her Church and the truth of the Protestant faith. That she 
was then treated with the greatest cruelty and severity by her 
relations and the priest of her parish, the Rev. Mr. O’Connor. 
That she was locked up four weeks in a room, and that the . 
window shutters were nailed to exclude the light. That she 
‘was lovked up two days, (from Thursday till Sunday,) without 
any food, to make her consent to go to Mass, which she would 
not do. That on Saturday, September 29, she went to Castle- 
rea for the purpose of procuring medical advice, for a pain 
and spasm in her arm—the consequence, she verily believes, of 


the ill treatment she received. That being determined to 


continue no longer a Roman Catholic, she went openly to 
church in Castlerea, on Sunday, and attended Protestant worship 
in the evening. That she returned to Ballymoe on Monday, 
but was obliged to fly back that night to Castlerea, and put 
herself under the protection of the police. That on Wednes- 
day, the 3d of October, the Priest of her parish , accompanied 


63 


by some of her relations, went to Castlerea to bring her back ; 
that she refused to go with them, knowing that they would ill 
treat, confine, or send her to the country, to prevent the injury 
they feared-would be done to the Church of Rome, if the niece 
of the Roman Catholic Bishop of Elphin should publicly read 
her recantation, which she is determined to do so soon as she 
can. That she charged the Rev. Mr. O’Connor to his face 
with having beaten her, which he denied. That several times 
since, some of her relations went to Castlerea, to endeavour to 
bring her back, but she would not go, fearing they would force 
her to continue a Roman Catholic, contrary to her conscience ; 
that she is in fear and terror of her life, should her relations ob- 
tain possession of her. 

Deponent feeling herself unsafe in Castlerea, from the vio- 
lence of her enemies, fled to Boyle, where she implored the 
protection of the Magistrates, that she may be allowed to ex- 
ercise the rights of conscience and become a member of the 
Protestant Communion, which she believes to be alone agree- 
able to the Word of God. | 

Deponent is willing to earn her livelihood in any way she 
can among those who will allow her to worship God according 
to the dictates of her conscience. 7 

(Signed) | Mary ANNE BURKE, 
Boyle, October 10, 1827. 


Philalethes.—I find that amongst those who 
refuse to examine into the subject of the second 
Advent, there prevails a strong opinion that the 
church is so much more numerous now than 
ever, and that it has produced such a great effect 
upon the world, that it is impossible it can be so 
ripe for judgments, as those who have studied 
most closely the prophetic Scriptures say it is: 
how can that erroneous opinion be met? 

Anastasius.—It is rather a prejudice than an 
Opinion, and therefore idle to contend with it; 


64 


but even if the position were true, that the church 
be more evangelical, and the world become bet- 
ter, the inference that therefore it is not ripe for 
judgments would be unsound. Look to the first 
judgment on Jerusalem, in the reign of Josiah, 
who re-established the worship of Jehovah in 
such a way that nothing like it had been seen 
for 600 years: “ surely there was not holden 
such a passover from the days of the judges that 
judged Israel, nor in all the days of the kings 
of Israel, nor of the kings of Judah:” and like 
Josiah, “ there was no king before him, that 
turned to the Lord with all his heart, and with 
all his soul, and with all his might, according to 
all the law of Moses; neither after him arose 
there any like him: notwithstanding, the: Lord 
turned not from the fierceness of his great wrath, 
wherewith his anger was kindled against Judah,” 
&c. &c. 2 Kings xxiv. 22. 25, 26; and against 
this very excellent king did the Egyptians come 
up, kill him, and conquer the country, making 
it a tributary province, which it remained, until 
the inhabitants were at last carried away to Ba- 
bylon. Again, from the birth of our Lord to the 
destruction of Jerusalem by the Romans, the 
Jews were freer from idolatry than at any for- 
mer period of their history, yet they were given 
over to that judgment. In modern times, 
Louis XVI. was one of the most amiable kings 
that ever sat upon the throne of France, and. 
Pope Pius VII. one of the most truly holy men ; 
yet they were delivered over to the judgment of 
the French revolution. Thus, even if the boasted 


65 


increase. qf., religion were: true, the objectors 
would not, be. borge out in their conclusion. But 
the very idea of the world having become better 
is absurd, unless they mean to contend that they 


have bound Satan, or made him better too ; and 


whenever you hear any. one contend that the 
world has got better, you may be sure: that that 
man’s heart is in it, and he isin search of an ex- 
cuse to pacify the conscience ‘for remaining in 
and of it. A little observation will'serve to con- 
vince you that the effect of the peace which the 
church has. so long enjoyed has been to make 
her quite content with the world as it is.. She 
has not been called to partake of the sufferings 
of Christ, and she has lost all wisn t to. Share his 
crown. |

in systematically and purposely concealing’ a

of. converting more souls. than if he declared it,

religious parade, even in his day. At p. 184, 
“We must be active and bold whenever duty 
calls us to be so. My own conduct with respect 
to the religious world is too much formed on my 
own feelings... Esee itin what I deem a lament-

into my closet, and. shrink within: myself. But 
VOL. II. F 


G6 


had I more faith, and simplicity, and love, and 
self-denial, I might do all in my present sphere, 
but I should throw myself in the midst of them, 
and intreat, and argue, and remonstrate. But then 
such a man must give himself up as a sacrifice. 
He would be misrepresented and calumniated 
from many quarters.” This last sentence is 
truly prophetic; for they who have felt it their 
duty to expose the low state of religion which is 
here complained of by Mr. Cecil, and the system 
of expediency which it has generated, have 
brought upon themselves the most unjust asper- 
sions ; and scarce a meeting of an Auxiliary 
Bible Society has been held throughout England, 
where the persons who have called in question 
the conduct of the London Committee have not 
been calumniated and misrepresented. 
Anastasius.—One of the most extraordinary 
and painful parts of the state of our Socie- 
ties is, that in private, the men who have 
greatest influence, object to the misconduct 
of them as strongly as possible; yet in pub- 
lic their fear of public opinion is so great that 
the abuses are never remedied. Scott is nearly 
as strong in his opinion as Cecil; he says 
(Life, p. 339,) “I am decidedly of opinion that 
the slight unscriptural kind of Gospel, cur- 
rent in most places, especially about London, 
threatens the cause of ‘genuine Christianity 
more than the attacks of open enemies.” But 
the first decided and avowed union between the 
children of God, and the children of the devil, 
in order to effect a common object, was that 


67 


_ between the orthodox Dissenters and the So- 
cinians. The evil arising from which was 
clearly foreseen, and boldly declared by Andrew 
Fuller; the only palliation, however, which 
he could offer for it was, that the union was 
for effecting purely civil purposes; and he adds, 
“had the union been of a religious nature, it 
must indeed have been surprising.” Yet since 
his day we have seen “a union of a religious 
nature,” between Christians and Socinians, in 
the Bible Society, boasted of as the perfection 
of Christian love. ‘Until that time no orthodox 
Christian would ever give the title of Christian 
to a Socinian ; and it is impossible to conceive 
a greater abuse of terms than to do so. The 
earliest definition of the word Christian, is that 
by Pliny, in his letter to Trajan, 97, one who 
“ prays to Christ as to God.” A Socinian is no 
more a Christian than a Mahometan or a Deist, 
and ought never to be considered in any other 
light than that of a member of “ the God-denying 
apostacy,” even if we should find him in the 
highest offices in the state, or in the church. 
There is another damning fact, marking the 
actual extent of infidelity, which cannot be 
gainsaid, and that is the establishment of 
the London University. The present day has 
given rise to. the first attempt which has ever 
been made in the history of the world to esta- 
blish a system of national education without 
being founded upon the religion of the state. 
No worshipper of Jupiter, no disciple of Con- 
fucius, no follower of Mahomet, no admirer of 
F 2 | 


68 


Vishnu, no believer in Budh, would have acted 
a part so dishonest to the objects of their re- 
spective idolatries, as to have united with each 
other upon the common basis of the exclusion 
of every reference to a God. It remained for a 
Christian nation, for a Protestant people, for 
the disciples of Jesus of Nazareth, to unite with 
the Jew, who calls him “accursed ;” with the 
Papist, who denies that he is ‘the sole me- 
diator betwixt God and man,” and that “by 
his one death he hath perfected for ever them 
that are sanctified ;” with the Socinian, who 
rejects him as ‘‘ God over all blessed for ever ;” 
with the Deist, who considers the whole Word 
of God as a book full of lies and imposture ; 
upon the common principle of excluding the 
religion of Jehovah from the catalogue of sciences 
necessary to be inculcated. 

Philalėthes—But there are some men of 
genuine piety connected with it, and many very 
plausible arguments have been advanced to. 
justify it. 

Anastasius —And when did Satan ever suc- 
ceed but by plausible arguments? does he under- 
stand his trade so ill as to come forward in his 
own naked character? If that were the case, 
we had no need to be warned against ‘‘ the deceit- 
fulness of sin.” Our Lord tells us, that there 
shall be false Christs ; and if Satan is permitted 
to set up a mimic Christ, he will surely set up 
mimic Christians, and a mimic church. As to 
men of genuine piety being members of it, that is 
only another proof of what we discussed much in 


69 


a former conversation, namely, that infidelity, 
under the mask of liberality, has pervaded the 
church, in proportion as it has pervaded theworld; 
and hence the great necessity for the real servants 
of God to come out and stand aloof, both from 
the professing, as well as from the unprofessing 
world. This was the case with the church at 
_ the period immediately preceding the deluge ; 
she had mixed herself with the men of the 
earth, and that judgment came upon her as 
well as upon it, Noah and his family alone 
being saved. 

Philalethes.--I do not doubt but that if the 
study of the prophecies, and the belief which it 
inculcates of the speedy coming of our Lord, had 
led merely to an intellectual speculation, with- 
out its practical tendency being seen to draw 
the Christian to a closer walk with God, and 
a greater separation from the maxims and 
practices of the world, it would not have been 
so much opposed in the church as it has been; 
but you are aware that they call every attempt 
to rise, and still more to lead others above 
the ordinary standard, a breach of Christian 
unity and love. 

Isocrates.—In a former conversation, our truly 
beloved brother Theophilus taught us that our 
first duty was to protest clearly and boldly 
against the sins of the church. The church has 
settled upon her lees, and all she begs is, like 
the world, to be let alone. This sort of hap- 
piness consists in the conscience being asleep ; 
the Christian tries to awaken it, and they detest 


70 


him for his pains. Hence it comes to pass, that 
the children of God, who are children of peace, 
are, nevertheless, the occasion of much dis- 
turbance in the world, and much sufferings to 
themselves. “J am for peace,” said David, 
“but when I speak, they are for war.” Why 
speak then? why not exercise love, and charity, 
and forbearance, and avoid giving offence by 
disagreeable truths? “I tried,” he says, ‘to 
refrain even from good words, but it was pain 
and grief to me; as I mused the fire kindled, 
and at the last I spake with my lips.” Jere- 
miah found the same thing, see xv. 10. xx. 7—11. 
A greater than David said, even he who is the 
Prince of Peace, Matt. x. 34--36. “Think not 
that I am come to send peace on earth; I came 
not to send peace, but a sword, for I am come to 
set a man at variance against his father, and the 
daughter against her mother, and the daughter- 
in-law against the mother-in-law ; and a man’s 
foes shall be they of his own household.” When 
holiness and zeal are combined in any man, 
that man becomes a sword in a wicked world. 
If a man be holy without zeal, he will be scoffed 
at indeed by many, but despised for his insig- 
nificance. If aman be zealous without holiness, 
he exposes himself to that withering rebuke, 
“ thou hypocrite, first cast out the beam in thine 
own eye.” This would be all easy if we had 
no contention but with the unconverted world; 
but we have a multitude who belong not fully 
to either camp. It seems uncharitable to call 
them enemies; it seems unfaithful to admit that 


71 


they are friends. Hence arises the difficulty. 
It is the Lord’s special and distinctive com- 
mandment to his disciples that they love one 
another. But who is a disciple? and how far 
is our love to one whom we suppose a disciple 
to be carried? I have laid before you a state- 
ment which I believe to be the truth of God. 
There is a man whom I suppose to be a Chris- 
tian. This statement does, I see, offend that 
man. What is my duty? Should I, out of love 
to the man, suppress the statement; or should 
I, out of love to the truth, make the statement, 
though I offend the man? Suppose we adopt 
the former mode of keeping peace; then it is 
clear that this consequence would follow; the 
prejudices of our brother, and not the contents 
of the Bible, would become the measure and 
standard of our statements of truth; an evil than 
which I cannot imagine any thing more abso- 
lutely destructive. If this be admitted, the 
church, instead of growing to the measure of 
the stature of Christ, would dwindle into the 
dwarfish littleness of the most puny, timid be- 
liever in her communion. We must adopt the 
latter mode then, and speak out; and in so doing 
we must incur the consequence, viz. we shall be 
reproached for disturbing peace and harmony, 
and brotherly love; as renders of the body of 
Christ; as destitute of that meekness which 
characterized our Master, whose commandment 
we shall hear was love. 

Anastasius.—One of the most extraordinary 
instances of a charge of wanting brotherly love 


72 


has just made its appearance, in Bretschneider's 
attack on Mr. Rose’s valuable account of the 
state of infidelity in Germany, by translating 
which Mr. Evanson has performed a good ser- 
vice to the church. The author divides the Ger- 
man infidels into four classes, the last of which, 

wherein he ranks himself, he says, are called 
EvANGELIC, (p. 68, Translation ;) Mr. Rose 
very properly includes the whole four under 
one general denomination of “ a mass of 
abominations,” on which Bretschneider says, 
“no Christian would act thus who knows. 
what is.due to his brother in Christ.” This 
is the only age in which the principles of 
such men would have been defended, by those 
who know better, and yet. many of them have 
been so in our (mis- called) religious reviews, and 
have been praised in the Reports of the Bible 
Society—that very Society, whose directors and 
agents took as decided a part as they had it in 
their power to do against the persecuted Chris- 
tians in Switzerland, and loaded their oppressors 
with the most fulsome flattery. There is one. 
sentiment so plausible in the mouths of these 
German infidels, that it requires to be particu- 
larly exposed ; it is this, ‘‘ Holiness (says Bret- 
schneider) indeed is the grand object. When 
we reflect, that even among those who here and 
there differ from the creeds of the great ec- 
clesiastical establishments, we find a zeal for- 
holiness, and a Christian course of life, we may 
rest in peace, and spare the alarm of ‘ fire.’ ” 
The persons over whom this cloak is thrown are, 


73 


by the description of the author himself, men 
who deny every one of the miracles recorded in 
the Bible, and who say that our blessed Lord 
was a man who admirably accommodated himself 
to the divine will, and that Providence wonder fully 
assisted him. 

Josephus.—I have lately been into Germany, 
and can truly say that the state of infidelity there 
far exceeded any thing of which I had formed an 
idea, although you told me so much of it before I 
left England. Jena, Halle, Goettingen, and Er- 
langen are still the seats of infidelity, and those 
Protestants, who seek rest in a revealed religion, 
turn Papists. Professor Paulus, an honorary 
member of the London Bible Society, is as much 
an infidel as he was ten years ago. Some of 
the Protestants who are considered pious are 
mostly mystics, and approach to Popish idola- 
try, as you will have seen by the account which 
Dr. Tholuck gives of Hendes the missionary. 
There are excellent people at Elberfield, Barmen, 
and Dusselthal. Dr. de Veluti, from Weimar, 
was obliged to seek an asylum in the institution 
of Count Von der Reeke, for he was put in pri- 
son in the Duchy of Weimar on account of his 
evangelical views ; for which also the preacher 
Schmidt was banished. There are some good 
men also at Berlin, as Neander, formerly a Jew, 
and Jaeniche ; but they are detested by the rest, 
such as Schleyermacher, &c. The Roman Ca- 
tholics in Germany may be divided under four 
heads: 1. Strict Papists, as the followers of 
Schlegel, and the Ligorians at Vienna, who are 

VOL. II. G 


74 


called, by the other Roman Catholics themselves, 
Romanists. 2. Those of the sentiments of the 
Gallican church. 3. Those who are complete 
apes of the Protestant Neologists in Germany. 
4. Partly Jesuitical, partly Protestant, and part- 
ly pious Roman Catholics. The Bible Societies 
have promoted more mutual intercourse amongst 
those different. parties, but I doubt whether 
much real conversion has been effected hitherto. 
It is my firm conviction that Germany is ripe for 
judgment. 

Anastasius.—It is of the utmost importance 
that the characters should be clearly known, for 
whom our Societies, in their Reports, claim our 
brotherly love, at the very moment when they 
are so indifferent to the suffering servants of 
Christ in Switzerland and Germany.

Anastasius.—According to the modern cant, 
there never existed brotherly love in the world 
till now. Certainly none of the Prophets in the 
Old Testament possessed it, for the constant 
theme of their sermons was the sins of the pro- 
fessing church. Certainly the Apostles posses- 
sed none, for their perpetual subject was the su- 
perficial quality of the religion of the straitest 
sects of that day. Acts vii. 1. 51—53 ; xiii. 27, 28; 
xviii. 6; xx. 29, 30. Certainly the reformers and 
nonconformists possessed none, as we find that 
their great subject of complaint was the worldly-

mindedness of professors; and Isaiah, Elijah,

this topic. I then stated, that the position of 
this country, in the present state’ of the world, 
was one of neutrality, not only between contend- 
ing nations, but between conflicting principles ; 
and that it was by neutrality alone that we could 
maintain that balance, the preservation of which 
I believed to be essential to the welfare of man- 
kind. I then said, that I feared that the next 
war which should be kindled in Europe, would be a 
war not so much of armies as of opinions. Not 
four years have elapsed, and behold my appre- 
hensions realized. It is to be sure within nar- 
row limits, that this war of opinion is at present 
confined ; but it is a war of opinion that Spain 
(whether as government or as nation) is now 
waging against Portugal ; if into that war this 
country shall be compelled to enter, we shall 
enter into`it with a sincere and anxious desire to 
mitigate rather than exasperate; and to mingle 
only in the conflict of arms, not in the more 


78 


fatal conflict of opinions. But I much fear that 
this country, (however earnestly she may en- 
deavour to avoid it,) could not, in such case, 
avoid seeing ranked under her banners all the 
restless and dissatisfied of any nation with which 
she might come in conflict. It is the contem- 
plation of this new power in any future war, 
which excites my most anxious apprehensions. 
The situation of England, amidst the struggle 
of political opinions which agitates more or less 
sensibly different countries of the world, may be 
compared to that of the ruler of the a ae 
described by the poet: |

- Ni faciat maria ac terras cælumque profundum 
Quippe ferant rapidi secum, verrantque per auras.” 


| The consequence of letting loose the passions, at 
‘present chained and confined, would be to pro-

Philalethes. —All these passages, and parti- | 
cularly this last, reminds me very forcibly of 
the language of Mr. Canning, which you quoted 
to me in a former conversation, describing the 
probable effects of the next war that will take 
place in Europe; and which seems to be the 
best interpretation yet given, of the effects of 
the seventh vial. 

_Anastasius.—It is certainly very similar; but, 
on a question of this kind, the strength of the - 
argument rests more upon the uniform current 
and extent of the testimony, than upon any 
one passage, however clear; and for this 
reason I bring so much Scripture before you. 
In Zech. xii. 11, it is written, “In that day 
there shall be a great mourning in Jerusalem, 
as the mourning of Hadadrimmon, in the valley 
of Megiddon ;” by which we are referred to the 
histories of the battles which took place there, 
as types of what shall be hereafter at the period 
predicted. One battle fought at Megiddon, 
was that in which Josiah, the last independent 
king of Judah, was slain: for though three suc- 
cessors sat upon his throne, one was tributary 
to Egypt, and the two last to the Chaldeans ; 
so that, with the death of Josiah the political. 
sun. of Jerusalem set for ever. The Prophet 
Jeremiah, who foretold, witnessed, and mourned 
over that event, though unheeded by the people, 
transfers. it to the future and similar judgment 


86 


upon the Gentiles, saying, “the punishment of 
thine iniquity is accomplished, O daughter of 
Zion ; he will no more carry thee into captivity : 
he will visit ¢hine iniquity, O daughter of Edom, 
he will discover thy sins.” Lam. iv. 22. The 
other battle fought in the valley of Megiddon, 
was that in which the Canaanites under Sisera, 
with 900 chariots of iron, were defeated, greatly 
through the instrumentality of two women, De- 
borah and Jael: but the circumstances which 
accompanied the victory, and no doubt chiefly 
occasioned it, were supernatural, for the pro- 
phetess informs us that, “the stars in their 
courses fought against Sisera: the river of 
Kishon swept them away, that ancient river, the 
river Kishon.” Judges v. 20,21. It is said also 
by Obadiah, “ thy mighty men, O Teman, (a 
city of Idumea put for the whole country,) shall 
be dismayed, to the end that every one of the 
house of Esau shall be cut off by slaughter.” 
Hence it seems most clearly to appear, that 
PANIC and CIVIL WAR Shall prevail amongst the 
enemies of the Lord at the last great battle, as 
they have done at the former battles in which 
he has interfered on behalf of his people. But, 
with respect to Civil War, if the whole of the 
nations of Christendom be considered as one 
family, an international war between these con- 
stituent parts of one great family would, with 
equal propriety, be called a civil war in reference 
to the whole. 

Philalethes——Is there such a place as Arma- 
geddon ? or is it a figurative term ? 


37 


Thraso.Many significations have been given 
to it, which may be seen in various commenta- 
tors; but the most obvious appears to be the 
best, which is, that the name evidently relates 
to the great battles which have been fought at 
Megiddon, and which are frequently alluded to 
as types of this. One derivation that has been 
offered is from two Hebrew words, the one signi- 
fying gathering, and the other cutting off: 

Polydorus.—It is at least in favour of this de- 
finition, that those ideas seem the chief points 
in the minds of the prophets who treat of this 
place. St.John says, the unclean spirits go to 
gather the kings to this battle—and_ he gathered 
them to a place called Armageddon. Joel says, 
iii. 1., in those days when I shall bring again 
the captivity of Judah, I will gather all nations 
in the valley of Jehoshaphat: and, again, “ as- 
semble yourselves, and gather yourselves, all ye 
heathen——multitudes, multitudes in the valley 
of excision.” ii. 14. Other Scriptures are equally 
definite respecting the locality where the battle 
is to be fought: Daniel’s expression, “ the glo- 
rious land,” xi. 41, fixes it to Palestine; and 
that of “glorious holy mountain,” 45, to Jeru- 
salem. Joel fixes it to the valley of Jehosha- 
phat; by which, we know, is meant the valley 
of Megiddon ; so that the encampment will pro- 
bably stretch from thence to Jerusalem, the 
valley lying about 60 miles north of that city. 
Joel iii. 12, 13. and Rev. xiv. 18, identifies this 
battle with the vintage of wrath. The prepa- 
ration for it is made at the close of the sixth 


88 


vial, and the principal event of the seventh will 
be this battle; the last vial, answering to the 
last blast of the trumpet which brought down 
the walls of Jericho. It appears that it will 
take place in consequence of an invasion on the 
Jews in their restored state, for Zechariah says, 
xii. 1—6, “I will make Jerusalem a cup of 
trembling unto all the people round about, when 
they shall be in the siege both against Judah 
and against Jerusalem.” Again, by the ex- 
pression, “ Governor of Judah,” it appears as 
if they must have appointed a regular govern- 
ment over themselves. Daniel xi. shews us 
that one of the invaders will be the wilful king, 
while we learn from the Apocalypse, that the 
Beast and false Prophet will meet with their 
doom there also. Zech. xiv. 2, 3, says, “I 
will gather all nations against Jerusalem to 
battle——then shall the Lord go forth and fight 
against those nations, as when he fought in the 
day of battle:” and the typical history of Jo- 
shua, (the type of Christ, who gives his people 
their final possession of their land,) shews that 
in his last battle all the kings, and all their 
host confederated, and were destroyed together. 
Joshua xi. It does not appear as if any na- 
- tion would be on the side of the Jews at the 
battle, although several will have concurred in 
bringing them to the place, as is declared by 
Isaiah lx. 9, 10. The supernatural agency 
which. will be manifested, is shewn very clearly 
in Rev. xix. 14; and also, by Isa. xxiv. 21, 
that more than one king will be present. The 


89 


stake contended for seems, by Zech. xiv. 2, to 
be, on the part of the Jews, no less than their 
whole political existence; while the moving - 
cause in their enemies is hatred of them, and a 
wish to exterminate them. I do not find in the 
Scriptures, however, any light as to the ground 
of this hatred; but it is probable that it may 
be a kind of religious crusade against. their 
establishment under Christ their king. 
_ Philalethes.—The opposition of God’s enemies 
to His. people is continually at work, more or 
less, and exactly in proportion as they are dis- 
cerned to be His people; thus, in this battle, it 
may not be so much religious enthusiasm, as 
blindness to God’s judgments. | 
Polydorus.—The second Psalm seems to ap- 
ply directly to this period. “ Why do the hea- 
then tumultuously assemble (marg.). and the 
people imagine a vain thing? the kings of the 
earth set themselves, and the rulers take counsel 
together, against Jehovah and against his — 
Christ—yet have I set my king upon Zion,

I will give thee the heathen for thine inheri-
