154 


me?’.in that day, I say he shall be the Shep- 
herd, the only Shepherd and ruler of the earth 
before the Lord, all the rest being cut off and 
destroyed for their confederacy against him. 
In which day also he will be revealed as the 
stone of Israel, not to break her as heretofore, 
but to smite the toes, and limbs, and body, 
and head of the great colossus of power, which 
hath oppressed her, and to lead by the union 
of his people, and to fill the whole earth with 
their rule and government. In this sense, he is 
the Shepherd and the Stone of Israel, when he 
becomes the glory to his people Israel, against 
whom he hath been the swift witness, and their 
unknown Messiah. ` And as such, he is declared 
to proceed from Joseph, though he hath already 
come of Judah. How this can be accom- 
plished I know not, unless it should be so or- 
dained, that appearing to these ten tribes, 
surnamed Ephraim, and of whom Ephraim was, 
by right of primogeniture and blessing, the 
foremost, he should bring them along with him 
from their captivity, and, as their mighty head 
and leader, break in pieces the Assyrian who 
ruleth over Babylon, and therein holds. Judah 
and Benjamin oppressed; and break in pieces 
the powers of the world in the day of the 
battle of God Almighty, and become the Shep- 
herd of the whole earth; fulfilling the. purpose 
and end of God, which Cyrus fulfilled in the 
figure, who is also called the Shepherd of the 
Lord, “ Cyrus, my Shepherd,” perhaps that he 
might be the more eminent type of the Shep- 


155 


herd of Israel, who is to proceed from Benjamin 
in his triumph, as in his humility he proceeded 
from Judah. This I give as the interpretation 
of a text, which has always seemed to me a 
shrowded mystery, while I was led into the idea 
which I am now endeavouring to unfold, that 
in these kings, from the sun-rising, are the ten 
tribes of Israel gathered under Messiah their 
Prince. Some such high and enduring destina- 
tion of Joseph is evidently shewn in the verses 
which follow, 25, 26, “« Even by the God of thy 
father, who shall help thee, and by the Almighty, 
who shall bless thee with blessings of heaven 
above, blessings of the deep that lieth under, 
blessings of the breasts, and of the womb: the 
blessings of thy father have prevailed above the 
blessings of my progenitors, unto the utmost 
bound of the everlasting hills: they shall be on 
the head of Joseph, and on the crown of the 
head of him that was separate from his brethren.” 
Taking in the whole of this blessing upon Joseph, 
and comparing its fulness with the scantiness 
of those upon all the rest, except Judah, I can- 
not help being convinced, that he is blessed 
here as the head of the ten tribes, just as Judah 
is blessed as the head of the two; and that as 
the coming of Shiloh is the prerogative of the 
latter, so the coming of the Shepherd and the 
Stone of Israel is the prerogative of the former ; 
and. that herein consists the double blessing of 
Joseph, yea, and the whole mystery of Joseph’s 
humiliation to the dungeon, and exaltation to 
the right hand of Pharaoh king of Egypt, and

156 


his salvation of his father’s house, yea, and of 
the whole world from death. | 

Let us now come to the blessing of Moses 
upon the tribes, and hear what he saith of 
Joseph, which far surpasses all the rest, and is 
written in Deut. xxxiii. 13, 14,15. Concerning 
these verses I say nothing, although when I 
compare them with the blessings of* the rest, 
I cannot but believe, that there is a certain 
royalty, and richness, and universality of en- 
dowment contained under these symbols, “ the 
precious things of heaven, the dew, and the 
deep, that coucheth beneath,” which is a kind 
of enfeoffment over the elements; and “ from the 
precious fruits brought forth by the sun, and 
for the precious things put forth by the moon,” 
which is an investiture in all the influences of 
the day, and of the night; and “ for the chief 
things of the ancient mountains, and for the 
precious things of the lasting hills,” which is 
an investiture in the mineral and gemmed riches 
of the mines and of the mountains, as also of 
the cattle and the flock which roam there, and 
every tall and fruitful tree; “ and for the pre- 
cious things of the earth and fulness thereof,” 
which is an investiture in all that this world 
contains, the earth and all the fulness thereof, 
which. belongeth unto the Lord, is here given 
unto Joseph; “ and for the good-will of him 
that dwelt in the bush ;” when the drunkards of 
Ephraim, the proud saucy scoffers of Ephraim, 
the head of the rebellion against Judah, received 
or possessed this good-will of Jehovah, I find 


157 


no more than I do that any one part of this 
most prolific blessing came upon the head of 
Joseph, and upon the top of the head of him 
that was separated from his brethren. But 
hear what follows; ‘his glory is like the first- 
ling of his bullocks,” (i. e. sanctified unto the 
Lord,) “and his horn like the horns of unicorns,” 
(that is single and mighty,) “ with these he 
shall push the people together to the ends of 
the. earth; and they are the ten thousands of 
Ephraim, and they are the thousands of Ma- 
nasseh.” The bullock was an emblem of 
royalty, being considered the head among the 
domestic cattle, as was the lion among the 
beasts of the wood; and as the eagle among 
the fowls of heaven. The solitary horn of the 
unicorn is an emblem of single monarchy and 
sovereign power, and is thus used in Ps. xcii. 
= of Messiah’s kingdom; 10, “My horn shalt 
thou exalt like the horn of an unicorn: I shall 
be anointed with fresh oil; and what follows 
in the blessing of Joseph, so accords with the 
peculiar language told of Messiah’s kingdom, 
that I make no doubt the same words are re- 
ferred to: “ with these,” with his two horns of 
Ephraim and Manasseh, which are like the 
horn of an unicorn, “ he shall push the people 
together to the ends of the earth.” This 
glorious destiny hath not yet been fulfilled of 
those tribes of Joseph, which have been lost 
to the knowledge of men for more than 2000 
years. And before their dispersion, there was 
hever any thing done by them at any time

Polydorus.—There are two great principles of 
which we should never lose sight in considering 
unfulfilled prophecies, and which are especially 
necessary tO be borne in mind for the right ap- 
prehension of those which relate to the restora- 
tion of the Jews. The first is, that the words 
Judah, [srael, and Gentiles, are always to be 

taken jiterally, without any extension which is 
not marked expressly by the context; except 
that tsra2el ‘5 sometimes used for the whole 

ipes: Zion also sometimes includes the 
whole ye ingdom, as in modern times we use the 
phrase ? << the Court of the Thuilleries,” or “ the 
Court 21 St. James’s,” for the whole government 
of Frat lI and England. The second principle 


a aa 


159 


is, that every passage is to be taken in its whole 
signification, and that we ought not to attempt 
to reconcile discrepancies, but rather conclude 
they relate to different places, and periods. 
When deliverance from Egypt took place of 
old, the whole population was in one spot; now 
the nation is dispersed over the whole world, 
and therefore the present deliverance must be 
a more complicated business. I find five dif- 
ferent modes of restoration pao of: 1. from 
the north-east unconverted; 2, from the east; 
3, from Egypt; 4, a second portion seems to be 
signified as restored after the first, in Isaiah 
xlix. 21, “ Who hath begotten me there, seeing 
I have lost my children, and am desolate, a 
captive, and removing to and fro? and who hath 
brought up thee? Behold, I was left alone; 
these, where had they been?” 5. Another por- 
tion comes home after their brethren are in pos- 
session of the land, and their temple worship 
restored. I do not mean to point out these 
things as indicating the order of the restoration, 
but I think that an attention to the quarters from 
whence they are to.come will obviate many ap- 
parent difficulties. | 

Isocrates.—We have no very decisive clue to 
distinguish those meant by Israel from those 
meant by Judah, since Paul calls himself an 
Israelite, though he was of the tribe of Benja- 
_ min, and consequently of the kingdom of Judah. 
It is clear, therefore, that the word Israel is 
used both in an extended and in a restricted 
sense. There cannot be a better way of getting 


160 


at the true meaning of the prophets, in the use 
of these terms, than by studying the great ana- 
logies which are set forth by Aristo. - 

Philalethes—1 wish you would explain more 
fully your ideas upon the difference between the 
blessing promised to Ephraim and those pro- 
mised to Judah. 

Aristo.—The largeness, and abami mag- 
nificence, of those blessings pronounced by the 
patriarch Jacob upon the head of Joseph, and 
his son Ephraim, and which Moses afterwards 
reiterated upon the twin tribes of Ephraim and 
Manasseh, the two sons of Joseph, are best ob- 
served by comparing them with the blessings 
pronounced at the same time upon the other 
patriarchs, and upon their tribes, which, with 
the exception of Judah in Jacob’s roll, and of- 
Levi in that of Moses, are, as you will perceive 
by reading them, nothing more than local de- 
finitions of their border, and characteristic fea- 
tures of their several ways of life; but certain it 
is, there is none of that exceeding great mighti- 
ness of power, and royal function of honour and 
glory, which are assigned unto Ephraim. And 
between the blessings of Judah and Ephraim 
there is this characteristic difference, that while 
to the former is given the sceptre and the so- 
vereignty, to the latter is given the fruitfulness 
and the warlike might; while to the former is 
given the rich and exuberant enjoyment of the 
choicest blessings of the earth, to the latter is 
given the bow of abiding strength, with which 
those blessings are achieved, and the strong 


161 


right hand by which they are maintained; to 
the one, the birth of the Shiloh, to whom the 
gathering of the people should be, and to the 
other, the origin of the Shepherd who gathers 
Israel, and of the Stone which smites the na- 
tions. So that if to the one it is assigned to 
wield the sceptre, to the other it is assigned to 
wield the sword with which the crown and 
sceptre are defended: as we would say, the 
one is the sovereign caste, the other the war- 
like caste, of the children of Israel: and so 
near does Joseph run to the level of Judah’s 
blessing, that I believe to this day it is not an 
unfrequent notion amongst the Jews, that there 
is to be a double Messiah, one of Judah’s, the 
other of Joseph’s line; and certainly amongst 
the Samaritans it is held, that the Messiah 
whom they expect is to come of the tribe of 
Joseph. But the plausibleness of their false 
notions is removed, and the blessing preserved 
in its entireness,. if that idea be true, which 
I have mentioned before, that it is to Ephraim,- 
as head of the ten tribes, that Messiah, when 
he cometh as the man of war, shall appear, and 
by their means shall accomplish the redemption 
of the royal tribe of Judah, which, with Benja- 
min, has been these two thousand years and 
more under the captivity of Babylon, and her 
daughter Rome. | 

Departing now from these blessings of Jacob 
upon the twelve patriarchs, and of Moses upon 
the twelve tribes, which may be regarded as the 
two fountains of prophecy, we shall follow down 


162 


the stream, and seek to ascertain what farther 
light they cast upon this point in particular, 
and in general upon the whole mystery of the 
restoration of Israel and Judah; and let us 
take as our guide the prophet Hosea, who, of 
all the prophets, is the fullest upon the judg- 
ments of the ten tribes of Israel, or of Ephraim, 
as he denominates them, from the tribe which 
held the supremacy from the time of their sepa- 
ration, and within whose bounds the royal city 
of Samaria was situated. 

The first chapter contains their doom, written 
in the names of the prophet’s three children : 
the first called Jezreel; for saith the Lord, “ I 
will avenge the blood of Jezreel upon the house 
of Jehu, and will cause to cease the kingdom 
of the house of Israel,” i. 4. The second, called 
Lo-ruhamah ; “ for I will no more have mer¢y 
upon the house of Israel, but I will utterly take 
them away,” ver.6. The third, called “ Lo- 
ammi ; for ye are not my people, and I will not 

be your God.” These judgments, from which 
Judah is by express declaration separated, 
(ver. 7,) came accordingly to the very uttermost 
upon Israel in the days of their king Hoshea, 
at the time when Judah, with their king 
Hezekiah, were delivered from the hand of 
Sennacherib the Assyrian. But the same chap- 
ter in which is written this threefold doom of 
bloody vengeance, no mercy, and rejection from 
the Lord their God, is thus with full and gra- 
cious promise concluded: “ Yet the number of 
the children of Israel shall be as the sand of the 


1638 


sea, which cannot be measured nor numbered : 
and it shall come to pass, in the place where it 
was said unto them, ye are not my people, there 
it shall be said unto them, ye are the sons of 
the living God: then shall the children of Judah 
and the children of Israel be gathered together, 
and appoint themselves one head, and they 
shall come up out of the land; for great shall 
be the day of Jezreel,” ver. 10, 11. Now con- 
cerning the manner of this restoration of Israel, 
we have many particulars given in the conclu- 
sion of the following chapter, of which the first 
is the Lord’s turning to her with gracious and 
comfortable words. Ver. 14. “ I will allure her, 
and bring her into the wilderness, and speak to 
her heart, or friendly (margin) to her.” When 
she hath thus been delivered from her present 
dwelling-place, wherever it is, and brought into 
the wilderness beyond Jordan, and there dealt 
with according to the riches of the grace and 
loving-kindness of the Lord, he proceeds to in- 
vest her in her ancient hereditaments: “ And I 
will give her her vineyards from thence, and the 
valley of Achor” (which lay upon the edge of 
the land of promise) “ for a door of hope; and she 
shall sing there as in the days of her youth, and 
as in the day when she came up out of the land 
of Egypt.” And then follow her solemn espou- 
sals to the Lord. Ver. 16, “ And it shall be at 
that day, saith the Lord, that thou shalt call me 
Ishi,” (my husband). And to the same effect 
is verse 19: “ And I will betroth thee unto me 
for ever; yea, I will betroth thee unto me in 


164 


righteousness, and in judgment, and in loving- 
kindness, and in mercies: I will even betroth 
thee unto me in faithfulness, and thou shalt 
know the Lord.” And therewithal these outcast 
tribes of Israel do receive a special bond over 
the creatures, such as Adam had in the begin- 
ning. Ver. 18, “ And in that day will I make 
a covenant for them with the beasts of the field, 
and with the fowls of heaven, and with the 
creeping things of the ground.” And at the 
same time He promises to undo all violence, 
and bring all resistance to an end, no doubt 
by their means, as we shall elsewhere see. “I 
will break the bow and the sword, and the battle 
out of the earth, and will make them to lie down 
safely.” And then follows the serenity of peace, 
fulness of blessedness, spontaneous showers of 
the heavens, and springing of the earth beneath, 
which of old was chartered unto Joseph’s line. 
Ver. 21, “ And it shall come to pass in that day, 
I will hear, saith the Lord, I will hear the hea- 
vens, and they shall hear the earth; and the 
earth shall hear the corn, and the wine, and the 
oil, and they shall hear Jezreel.” And finally 
is added that exceeding populousness which 
shall make Ephraim a multitude of nations: 
« And I will sow her unto me in the earth.” 
And to shut up the whole of this to the pos- 
session of Israel, and to shew that of her it is 
especially spoken, and not of Judah, (though 
the like be said of Judah elsewhere,) this re- 
markable passage is thus concluded: ‘“ And I 
will have mercy upon her that had not ob- 


165 


tained mercy,” (but Judah had obtained mercy, 
ver. 1, 17;) “and I will say to them which 
were not my people, thou art my people; and 
they shall say, thou art my God.” 

Now, if any one should wonder that such a 
lavishment of blessing, that such an honourable 
_ pre-eminence of place and power should be pro- 
mised to these ten tribes, so long lost from the 
knowledge of man in the yet unknown parts of 
the earth, he will bear in mind, that the ten 
tribes of Israel had ten parts out of twelve in 
the inheritance of Messiah, and all the blessings 
promised to Abraham, of which they have not 
yet received any : whereas Judah and Benjamin 
were delivered out of their captivity, and being 
preserved within the bounds of Christendom, 
had not only in all their cities the first presen- 
tation of the Christ and his Gospel; but ever- 
since have possessed the pre-eminency of giving 
birth to, and bearing upon their stock the whole 
elect church. Now, if for Judah’s sake the 
Lord did raise up Cyrus, his shepherd, for their 
deliverance, and by means of many prophets, 
and-of Jesus, and the Apostles, shew forth upon 
them the goodness of his grace, whilst poor 
Israel is suffering far from knowledge, hope, 
or consolation; should we wonder, should we 
not rather expect, that a time will come, at 
which Israel shall be visited with her propor- 
tionate share of the promised blessing, shall 
be called upon for her testimony and diligence 
on the behalf of her Lord and God? If Judah 
received the blessing of Messiah's former ad- 


166 
vent, and was called upon to send forth the 
Apostles and first preachers of the Gospel to 
= bear the contumely of his cross in all nations ; 
should not Israel also have a revelation of his 
power, and be called upon to do him good 
service in the time to come? But, as we well 
know, that it is in power and might that he 
appears, that service which she can render 
him must be in power and might, to bear the 
brunt of all the nations, and break that huge 
confederacy of the kings against her King, 
or, at the least, to call by the way of the 
wasted Euphrates, and overthrow the Anti- 
christ, who shall then sit monarch in Babylon, 
and out of his hand to set the tribes of Judah 
and Benjamin free. I say this ought not to 
appear strange; but rather the reverse of it 
should appear strange, that Israel should not 
have a distinct manifestation of her Redeemer, 
and a distinct part to act in the establishment 
of his kingdom. And to the different charac- 
ters of these two advents and acts of Christ, I 
believe the different character of the blessings 
upon Judah and Joseph refers. The feeling of 
Surprise at the mention of so magnificent a pro- 
mise being removed, let us proceed to search 
our prophet for further information concerning 
Israel, which we find in the very next chapter, 
to the effect, that during the many days of her 
present concealment, she is looked upon by the 
Lord in the light of an adulterous woman, 
pledged unto a man after many days, during 
which he shall wait for her, and shall not play 


167 


the harlot, nor be for another man, even as he 
also will remain for her. “ For, saith the Lord,” 
interpreting his own emblem, ‘“ the children of 
Israel shall abide many days without a king, 
and without a prince, and without a sacrifice, 
and without an image, and without an ephod, 
and without teraphim,” iii. 4. It is rather diff- 
cult to know what is meant by the words trans- 
lated “ image” and ‘‘teraphim :” for it does not 
consist well with the general scope of prophecy, 
that the Israelites should be preserved from 
idolatry, wherefore I incline to understand, un- 
der these names, the furniture of the temple, 
and the temple service, as the cherubim ; and 
to take the whole passage to mean, that what 
Judah possessed, the sceptre, and the law, and 
the worship also of God, in token of her service 
to him, Israel should possess none of all these 
during the many days of her concealment and 
distance from the Lord; that she should, as 
Moses twice declares, “ serve gods, the work 
of man’s hands, wood and stone,” which Jere- 
miah also predicts of Judah, xvi. 13, and 
which Ezekiel predicts of Israel, who say among 
the heathen, “ we will be as the heathen, as 
the families of the countries, to serve wood and 
- stone.” But into whatever excesses of idolatry 
she may fall, she is not therefore relieved from 
the obligation under which God had put her of 
being his “ after many days:” which being 
expired, it is added, ‘ Afterwards shall the 
children of Israel return, and seek the Lord their | 
God, and David their king, and shall praise

the Lord and his goodness in the latter days.”

Ezek. xxxvii. 26. | 
=- Before leaving, however, the peculiar Pro- 
phet of Ephraim, I would bring under your eye 
the chief of those passages, which cast light 
upon the captivity of the ten tribes of Israel. 
The place of her seclusion is thus described, 
ix. 3, “ They shall not dwell in the Lord’s 
land; but Ephraim shall return to Egypt, and 
they shall eat unclean things in the land of 
Assyria.” And as the reason for Judah's 
being sent captive to Babylon, was that vain 
glorious trust in that power, and wealth, and 
display before her ‘ambassadors, which was 
made by Hezekiah; so also with Ephraim, 
vii. 11. “ Ephraim also is like a silly dove with- 
out heart; they call to Egypt, they go to 
Assyria:” and the manner of their abiding 
there is then described, “ for they are gone up 
to Assyria, a wild ass alone by himself ; Ephraim 
hath hired lovers; yea, though they have hired 
among the nations, now will I gather them, 
and they shall sorrow a little for the burden 
of the King of princes.” That is, against the 
time of the Lord’s gathering of them, they shall 
begin to mourn for Christ the King of the 
princes of the earth, whose royal function they 
had rejected, and whose royal function they 


169 


must now begin to acknowledge, and to bear 
the burden of his warfare with the kings of 
the earth, who are now beginning to follow 
her evil example, and grow rebellious against 
him. Where I perceive another great instance 
of God’s marvellous. grace, that as he used 
the Jews of Jerusalem, who had been present 
at, and I doubt not acted a part in, his cru- 
cifixion, to uphold the glory of his cross after 
the day of Pentecost; and especially Paul, 
who persecuted him; so does he use these ten 
tribes,{ who first rejected his royal offer, to be 
the instrument of maintaining it against the 
combined rebellion of the princes of the earth. 
And herein lies the mystery of the separation 
of the tribes into two parts, that the honour 
might be divided between the children of Israel 
of sustaining the cause of Messiah in the two 
passages of its most perilous strait; the first, 
when he was crucified and his Gospel perse- 
cuted; the second, when he shall be resisted 
by the confederacy of all the earth, and the 
setting up of his kingdom opposed. Now, if, 
as some have supposed, those wise men from 
the East, who were guided by a star to come 
from a far country, in order to offer their ho- 
mage and their gifts at the feet of the new- 
born King of the Jews, be the representatives 
from the ten tribes, (and I think it is the most 
rational account that can be given either of the 
stars appearance, or of their long journey of 
more than a year’s distance,) then it gives no 
small confirmation to the idea of Ephraim’s 
VOL. II. N 


170 


future high vocation, that they were first 
brought into the presence of Herod and his 
Court, to bear witness to them of. their King, 
“ Where is he that is born king of the Jews? 
for we have seen his star in the East, and are 
come to worship him.” And it is further to 
be observed, that when Herod, by his arts, would 
have circumvented these witnesses to the royal 
function of Christ, the Lord was pleased to guide 
them by a dream according to his will: and 
this is the only testimony which I remember, 
saving that of Jesus himself, which was made 
to him as a King, before kings. But without 
laying more stress upon this than barely to 
mention it, I proceed with the fourth examina- 
tion of the things contained in the Prophet 
Hosea concerning this mystery. 

The gathering of the tribes is thus described 
in xi. 9, “ I will not execute the fierceness of 
mine anger, I will not return to destroy 
Ephraim, for I am God and not man, the Holy 
One in the midst of thee: and I will not enter 
into the city,” (or as it is in xii. 9, “ I will yet 
make thee to dwell in tabernacles, as in the 
days of the solemn feasts ;”)—<‘ they shall walk 
after the Lord; he shall roar like a lion: when 
he shall roar the children shall tremble from 
the west: they shall tremble as a bird out 
of Egypt, and as a dove out of the land of 
Assyria; and I will place them in their own 
house, saith the Lord.” Here we have Ephraim 
visited by the Holy One in the midst of her: 
brought out of the cities and nations of her 


17} 


captivity by mighty demonstration of the divine 
power, thus described by Ezekiel; “ And I will 
bring you out from the people, and gather you 
out of the countries wherein you are scattered, 
with a mighty hand, and with an outstretched 
arm, and with fury poured out;” of which 
acts the terrible impression over all these lands 
shall be so great, and in the sight of the chil- 
dren of Israel the manifestation so glorious, 
that, (Jer. xvi. 10,) “ it shall no more be said, 
the Lord liveth that brought up the children of 
Israel out of the land of Egypt; but the Lord 
liveth that brought up the children of Israel 
from the land of the north, and from all lands 
whither he had driven them.” Being thus de- 
livered out of the cities of their captivity, and 
from the nations that sowed themselves of 
them, “ they shall walk after the Lord,” as 
heretofore they marched out of Egypt under 
the leading of the cloud and pillar of fire; for 
it is so written in Ez. xx. 35, “ And I will 
bring you into the wilderness of the land of 
Egypt, so will I plead with you face to face; 
like as I pleaded with your fathers in the wil- 
derness of the land of Egypt, so will I plead 
with you, saith the Lord God: and I will cause 
you to pass under the rod, and I will bring 
you into the bond of the covenant; and I will 
purge out from among you the rebels: and 
them that transgress against me: I will bring 
them forth out of the country where they 
sojourn, and they shall not enter into the land 
of Israel, and ye shall know that I am the

172 


Lord.” ‘There‘is revealed in these words a great 
natural reformation, and I may say, re-¢otisti- 
tution, of which, as appears to me from all ’the 
Scriptures, Elias shall return, and be the great 
instrument in God’s hand, as Moses was here- 
tofore. In all which his holy commandments, 
having walked after the Lord, it is immediately 
added, “ The Lord shall roar like a lion.” Now, 
as saith Jeremiah, “ He shall forsake his covert 
as a lion; for the land is desolate, because of 
the fierceness of the oppressor, and because of 
his fierce anger.” Now, as saith Isaiah 
xlii. 13, “ He shall go forth as a mighty man, 
he shall stir up jealousy like a man of war;” 
and, not to multiply other numerous instances 
of the same figure used of the same time and 
circumstances, I refer you to Is. xxxi., where 
the cause of this mighty putting forth of royal 
strength and indignation, by the Lion of the 
Tribe of Judah, is revealed to be the destruc-

of Isaiah, as also in Micah and Zechariah, is 
the name to denote the great head of the Anti- 
christian confederacy in the day of the Lord’s 
glorious advent; ver. 4, “ For thus hath the 
Lord spoken unto me, like as the lion and the 
-young lion roaring on his prey; when a multi- 
‘tude of shepherds is called forth against him, 
he will not be afraid of their voice, nor abase

Lord. of Hosts come down to fight for Mount 
Zion, and for the hill thereof—then shall the 
Assyrian fall with the sword, not of a mighty

‘man; and the sword not of a mean man shall 
deyour him.” At that time the great opposer 
of Christ and usurper of His kingdom, shall 
have come into His royal city of Jerusalem, 
and entered into possession of the land of His 
inheritance, and be ruling with a load of op- 
pression over His saints, and over all the earth ; 
when the fulness of the time being come, He 
putteth on righteousness as a breast-plate, 
and a helmet of salvation upon His head; 
and He putteth on garments of vengeance for 
clothing, and is clad with zeal as a cloak—For 
the enemy is come in like a flood, and the 
Spirit of the Lord doth lift up a standard 
against him. 

When he hath uttered this voice, so sublimely 
described in Ps. xviii. and xxix.; when he shall 
have blown the great trumpet of war spoken of 
by all the prophets, and set up the standard for 
his people, the effect is thus described in our 
prophet: “ He shall roar; then the children 
shall tremble from the west ; they shall tremble 
as a bird out of Egypt, and as a dove out of the 
land of Assyria; and I will place them in their 
houses, saith the Lord.” Here are three dis- 
tinct places mentioned, from which the chil- 
dren are to come to be placed in their houses: 
Ist, the west; 2d, Egypt; and 3d, the land of 
Assyria; of which the two latter, we have al- 
ready seen, are set forth in the prophet, and 
indeed in all the prophets, as the places whither 
the ten tribes were cast out in the anger of the 
Lord, and whence they are to return when the 


174 


Lord shall assemble the outcasts of Israel, Isa. 
vii. 15, 16; xxvii. 13. Wherefore I conclude, 
as we do in fact see it to be, that the children 
who tremble from the west are the dispersed 
of Judah, who are also to be gathered into one. 
Now it is remarkable, that the trembling is not 
accompanied with any immediate movement, 
but that from Egypt is followed by a flight as 
quick as a bird, and that from Assyria with 
a flight as quick as a dove, the most rapid of 
birds, which signifies, that while all. the dis- 
persion shall hear the voice of the Lord, how- 
ever given, and see his standard, however lifted 
up, those of the west shall either not be called, 
or, which is more likely, through the oppres- 
sion of the antichristian king of Babylon, shall 
not be permitted to obey the summons, which 
Ephraim from his two hiding places shall gladly 
hear, and readily obey; and being mustered by 
the Lord, and reformed into a nation, as is said 
above, their Shepherd that gathered them shall 
lead them, and break the Assyrian, when he 
shall come into our land, and tread in our 
palaces, (Amos v. 5;) which last monarch of 
Babylon being broken, the children who trem- 
bled from the west at the Lion’s roaring, shall 
come out of their prison, and be given unto 
their brethren. 7 

There is another passage of the prophet Hosea 
which casts a steady light upon the mystery 
into which we inquire, contained in chap. xiii. 
of which, and the two preceding chapters, I 
would remark, that they are a striking example 


176 


of that brief and starting style for which this 
prophet is remarkable, which contains under 
it matters very deep, beyond the apparent sur- 
face ; and all the words of which coming forth 
at long intervals from a strong and deeply 
agitated bosom, are as it were the gleam 
and growlings of the volcano, that tell the 
beholder of a fearful commotion within, and 
an imminent danger without. But though I 
have not been able yet, to my satisfaction, to 
unravel the secret and invisible link which con- 
nects these motions of the prophetic spirit with 
one another, I have no difficulty whatever in that 
which follows from verse 9 of chap. xiii. to the 
end of the prophecy, to which I do now request 
your attention. ‘‘O Israel, thou hast destroyed 
thyself; but in me is thine help. I will be thy 
king: where is any other that may save thee 
in all thy cities? and thy judges of whom thou 
saidst, Give me a king and princes? I gave 
thee a king in mine anger, and took him away 
in my wrath.” The Lord here toucheth upon 
the deep root of the controversy which he had 
with the whole house of Jacob, over whom he 
desired to be the king, but which he had espe- 
cially with the house of Israel, who stood up 
against the line of David, in which he had 
appointed that the true King of Israel should 
. come: and he says, though by that bitter revolt 
they had undone themselves, he would be their 
help, and himself become their king, and save 
`: and deliver them. Therefore, in the greatness of 
his grace he addeth, “ the iniquity of Ephraim 


176 


is bound up; his. sin is hid.” . This is the act 
of God’s: forgiveness, and the turning away of 
iniquity from Jacob, when all Israel shall be 
saved, as it is written in the prophet Jeremiah, 
l. 20: “ In those days, and in that time, saith 
the Lord, the iniquity of Israel shall be sought 
for, and there shall be none, and the sins of 
Judah, but they shall not be found; for I will 
pardon them whom I reserve.” To this mystery 
Paul makes allusion, Rom. xi. where, after the 
fulness of the Gentiles is come in, or the number 
of the elect is completed, he saith, “ and so all 
Israel shall be saved; as it is written, there 
shall come out of Zion the Deliverer, and shall 
turn away ungodliness from Jacob; for this is 
my covenant unto them, when I shall take away 
their sins.” In Jer. xxxi. this new covenant 
into which Israel shall be introduced in the wil- 
derness, Ezek. xx. 37, is written at length, and 
is. the same into the promise of which we are 
‘brought by the Spirit, and to the free inheritance 
of which we shall be raised up in the day of the 
coming of the Lord. But previously to the so- 
lemnizing of this new covenant with the house 
‘of Israel, there is mentioned, in the same chapter 
of Jeremiah, a work of great heart-searching and 
conversion in the midst of Ephraim. Ver. 18. “I 
have surely heard Ephraim bemoaning: himself 
thus: Thou hast chastised me, and I was chas» 
‘tised, as a bullock unaccustomed to the yoke:: 
turn thou me, and I shall be turned ; for thon 
art the Lord my God.” Nothing can be more 
conclusive than this, both with respect to. the 


177 


conversion and the return of Ephraim. And now, 
for the’ procuring cause’of both, observe what 
follows in the next verse. 22. “ How long wilt 
thou go about, O thou backsliding daughter? 
for the ‘Lord hath created. a new thing in the 
earth, a woman shall compass a man :” which 
is- exactly parallel with the famous prophecy 
given by Isaiah to the house of David, as the 
assurance of its establishment for ever upon 
the throne of Israel: “ Behold, a virgin shall 
concerve, and shall bear a son, and shall call his 
name Emanuel.” Now this child hath already 
been presented unto Judah, and was rejected 
by her; wherefore the tabernacle of David, and 
their beautiful temple, are fallen down. But 
unto the tribes of Israel the Son of the virgin 
hath not been presented; the new thing which 
the Lord hath created. upon the earth hath not 
been made known unto Ephraim, to whom it 
is here expressly promised, as the wonderful 
spring of that wonderful revolution which is to 
take place upon them. It is in this same re- 
markable chapter of Jeremiah, that the lamen- 
tation of Rachel for her children occurs, which 
by Matthew is applied to the cruel murder of 
the innocents in Bethlehem and Ramah, by the 
fierce wrath of Herod. For*you will perceive, 
by referring to Neh. xi., that in these cities, 
though situated in the ancient territory of 
Judah, did the children of Benjamin, the 
youngest son of Rachel, dwell, after the cap- 
tivity; and here also Rachel died, in the ex- 
treme anguish of giving birth to. Benjamin: so 


178 


that with great propriety doth the Evangelist 
apply the passage, ‘“‘ a voice was heard in Ra- 
mah, lamentation and bitter weeping ; Rachel, 
weeping for her children, refused to be com- 
forted for her children, because they were not.” 
But when the passage is studied in the pro- 
phecy itself, it hath manifestly a much larger 
application ; for it is immediately added by the 
Lord to his weeping handmaid, “ Thus saith the 
Lord, refrain thy voice from weeping, and thine 
eyes from tears, for thy work shall be rewarded, 
saith the Lord; and they shall come again from 
the land of the enemy; and there is hope in 
their end, saith the Lord, that thy children shall 
come again to their own border.” And then 
follows the large and particular prophecy quoted 
above concerning Ephraim, the son of Joseph, 
the son of Rachel, and the head of the tribes. 
Now, when any prophecy, which is applied in 
the New Testament to any event, has, in its 
own place and connexion, a manifest reference 
to another event, there are only two ways of 
reconciling the difference: the one is to say, as 
is generally the case with the present unbe- 
lieving generation, “ it is quoted by way of 
accommodation, as any writer may quote an 
appropriate passage of a poet as he passes 
along his subject.” This I utterly reject and 
detest as dishonourable to God’s word, and the 
root of that most baneful denial of the prophecy 
altogether, which has come over the Reformed 
Churches abroad, and a little, or to speak: all 
the truth, not a little darkened the light unto 


179 


us. The other, and the truer view of the mat- 
ter, is, that the one event to which the Spirit 
applieth it in the New Testament is typical of 
and intended by God for the furthering of the 
great future event to which it is primarily and 
permanently intended to refer. And this is true 
of the greater part of the prophecies respecting 
the events of our Lord’s advent in humility, 
which are almost all typical of the events which 
shall attend his advent in power and glory. For 
example, his riding into Jerusalem in triumphal 
state; his cleansing the temple of the money- 
changers; his transfiguration; his changing 
water into wine, and many more, which I need 
not enumerate, are all typical of events to come, . 
and therefore may have these prophetic Scrip- 
tures applied to them which foretel those events, 
and in them their types also. How then are 
these two events, Rachel weeping for her chil- 
dren that had been slain at Ramah, typical of 
this weeping of Rachel for her children which 
shall be comforted in the restoration of Ephraim? 
The exposition of this will cast much light upon 
the mystery into which we are inquiring. To 
take it up from the beginning: the birth of 
Emanuel, the child of the virgin, was given 
to the house of David, as the sign of the utter 
destruction and cutting off of Ephraim from 
being a nation: “ Within threescore and five 
years Ephraim shall be broken, that it be not 
a people.” It answered the end of God’s wis- 
dom to bring on the judgment of Ephraim be- 
fore he brought into manifestation the sign or 


180 


cause of it, that is, Emanuel : wherefore he 
constituted the prophet’s son into a type of him 
for this end, and pledged the fulfilment of it on 
Shear-Jashub’s head, saying, “ before the child 
shall know to refuse the evil and choose the 
good, the land which thou abhorrest shall be 
forsaken of both her kings.” This, which was 
pledged upon the head of Shear-Jashub, who 
was constituted a type for that end, was truly 
brought to pass, however, by the wrath of 
Christ, whose royal office they had scorned, in 
refusing Rehoboam’s anointed sceptre, and now 
more especially in conspiring to cut off David's. 
line. Therefore, as in Christ all the types did 
- meet, it was necessary to show forth, by some 
demonstration, that, in revenge for the injured 
honour of him, Ephraim had been broken ; and, 
to make the type good, it must be before the 
age that children know good from evil. There- 
fore, by the information of those wise men, who 
arrived at the very time of the child’s age, 
Herod is set upon the scent of royal jealousy, 
which he pursues by slaying the infants of Ben- 
jamin which dwelt in Bethlehem and Ramah. 
Now, the very inhumanity of this act doth show 
me its demonstrative and typical character. For 
you will observe, from the first passage of Hosea 
which we quoted, and which may be regarded 
as the title of the whole prophecy, it is said, 
“ I will avenge the blood of Jezreel upon the. 
house of Jehu,” which then reigned. : Now, 
what was the blood of Jezreel to be avenged , in | 
the house of Jehu? it was the blood of every 


181 


living thing that breathed of the line of the 
former race of kings. And what did Zimri, who 
introduced. that former race? he did the same 
by every living soul which had in it the blood 
of Baasha, the father of the former dynasty. 
And what did Baasha? he did the same by all 
the seed of Jeroboam, the father of the rebellion. 
Sò that royal jealousy was the capital crime of 
the house of Ephraim, through which the sceptre 
began, and by which it continued, even by inhu- 
man massacres, until the house of Jehu was cut 
off, before Shear-Jashub could know good from 
evil: and in the next reign of Hoshea, who . 
usurped the government, they were all carried 
away into Assyria. Now all this happened 
unto them, because of their revolt against the 
line of Messiah; and it was necessary to shew 
this, before the child, whom the virgin brought 
forth, who was the true avenger, should be of 
age to know good from evil; which was accord- 
ingly done by Herod’s act of royal jealousy, 
and was so ordained as to fall upon the house 
of Benjamin, and not of Judah, because Ben- 
jamin was the brother of Joseph, whom Joseph 
loved as his own soul. But the blow was struck 
at Ephraim, had indeed been struck 720 years 
before’; and thus what now occurred was only 
the signification that it was Messiah who struck 
it’ ‘then, in vindication of his own royal fune- 
tion, against which they had conspired ; which 
syitibolical event doth further shew us, that all 
which Ephraim hath suffered, she hath suffered 
in'cénsequence of that fearful revolt against his 


182 


royal office; just as all which Judah has suf- 
fered, she has suffered for her fearful rejection 
of his claim to be the Messiah. And therein 
Rachel’s weeping in Ramah for these innocents 
is an apt symbol of her weeping always, even 
until now, over those ten tribes of Ephraim and 
Manasseh, and their confederates which have 
been lost, which have been as if they were not 
these 2,500 years. And the Spirit of God doth 
signify that the event of Ramah was the act in 
which the character of the judgment of them 
long ago was exemplified: great cruelties, great 
. inhumanity, a visitation of the jealousy of kings, 
because they had been jealous of the King of 
kings. But the Lord comforteth Rachel, and 
declareth that they shall come again from 
the land of the enemy. Now, seeing that to 
Ephraim and the ten tribes there hath been 
awarded hitherto nothing but wrath and cruel 
and inhuman massacres on Messiah’s account, 
shall there not, think you, be to Ephraim a 
glorious reaping time? Shall he be the only 
nation of the earth who shall not profit from the 
promised seed of Abraham? God’s faithfulness, 
God’s grace forbid! he hath not so cast away 
his people. For Ephraim shall be great, he 
shall be blessed, he shall be mighty, he shall be 
the arrow of the Lord, and his bow; and his 
hands shall be made strong by the hands of the 
mighty God of Jacob: for Ephraim is my first 
born. For it is expressly so written in this very 
fruitful chapter of Jeremiah’s prophecy: “ Be- 
hold, I will bring them from the north country,” 


183 


whither Judah and Benjamin were never cast. 
What this means I know not, save that Ephraim, 
as a nation, shall be the first-born unto Christ, 
when he shall come to be the Shepherd and the 
Stone of Israel: for Ephraim hath sowed very 
bitterly, and he also shall have his reaping time 
of joy. 

This expression, ‘“ Ephraim is my first-born,” 
gives the key to another passage with which 
I have been much puzzled, and which is written 
in Hosea xi. 1. “ When Israel was a child, 
then I loved him, and called my son out of 
Egypt.” Now we know that this is applied by 
the Holy Spirit in the New Testament, as a 
prediction of Christ’s being called out of Egypt 
upon the death of Herod, Matt. ii. 13—20. 
But when we study the passage and its context 
in the Book of Hosea, it has a manifest refer- 
ence to Ephraim, and that to the exclusion of 
Judah; for the last verse of the chapter. says, 
“ But Judah yet ruleth with God, and is faith- 
ful with the saints.” We do right, therefore, 
in excluding Judah from this and the three 
subsequent chapters, and indeed from the whole 
of this prophecy of Hosea, except where it is 
by name expressly introduced. Now believing, 
as I do, that there are no accommodations by 
the Divine Spirit in the use of the Old Testa- 
ment Prophecy, but an exact opening of what 
himself intended in whole or in part, when He 
first inspired it, I am forced to conclude, that 
in this passage, “ out of Egypt have I called 
my Son ;” there is a prediction of that passage 


184 


in the life of Christ, when he was taken to 
Egypt and again brought out of it. The Son 
here spoken of is Christ, and therefore Israel 
in the prophecy is a name for Christ. -Then 
the prophecy proceeds to speak of Ephraim’s 
backsliding from the worshipping of God to the 
worship of Baalim, ver. 2. Then it makes men- 
tion of the Lord’s teaching him to go as a- 
child is taught by his parent; and of his 
delivering him from a yoke, and feeding him 
with his own hand. Then he is threatened 
with a sorer bondage than that of Egypt, be- 
cause of the obstinate and iron sinew of his 
neck, which would not bend itself unto the 
Lord. Then the desolation is described, and 
the following verses contain, as we have seen, 
the promise of his restoration; and in the last 


. verse, Judah is excluded, as has been said. 


Philalethes.—Notwithstanding the labours of 
Bishop Horsley and Mr. Faber, the important 
part which the house of Israel is once more to 
perform on the theatre of the world has been 
greatly overlooked, even by the majority of 
those who have paid most attention to what 
relates to their future destinies. If the stu- 
dents of prophecy are disposed to magnify the 
events of their own times, certainly those who 
despise prophecy, the statesmen and politi- 
cians of the world, are not less disposed to 
despise remote events, or they would perceive 
how mean are all the states and wealth of 
modern Europe, in comparison with the em- 
pires which have’ existed in former times: yet 


185 


the Assyrian, Babylonian, Medo Persian, 
Egyptian, Grecian, and Roman Empires have 
vanished as completely as if they had never 
had an existence. The Jews alone remain; a 
never failing miracle; the daily condemnation 
of the sceptic; and the ready prepared execu- 
tioners of Divine justice ; and while the modern 
statesmen speculate on the probable destinies 
of Russia, Turkey, America, and China, they 
never consider the Jews as worthy of the 
smallest consideration. You have excited so 
much interest in my mind concerning them, 
that I must entreat you to give me any further 
information in your power. 

Aristo.—I will do so gladly, if you are not 
weary, for I think God’s dealings with Israel 
the great key to the right understanding of 
his dealings with ourselves, as individuals, as 
a church, and as a nation. 

The doctrine, which we have already de- 
rived from the Holy Scripture, ‘concerning 
Ephraim and the ten tribes, (whom I believe 
to be signified by the kings of the East, in 
Rev. -+xvi., whose way is prepared by the drying 
up of Euphrates, as heretofore the way of 
Cyrus and his many kings was prepared ; and 
for the same end of destroying the last king 
of the mystical Babylon, and setting free the 
tribes of Judah and Benjamin, which have 
now for many centuries been locked up. in 
the captivity of that fourth monarchy over 
which she rules,) is that before the great day 
of the Lord, they shall be called by the Lord, 

VOL. II. o 


186 


and be ready to come at his call, in order 
‘to maintain the glory and the power of his 
royal prerogative, when it shall be called in 
question, and confederated against by the 
kings of the earth and their people; as here- 
tofore at his former advent, the tribes of Judah 
and of Benjamin did furnish forth the men 
and the means, whereby the testimony of his 
incarnation and resurrection was planted in 
the nations and kingdoms of the earth: and 
as these two tribes were honoured to furnish 
the foundation, yea, and the chief corner-stone 
of the church that now is, so shall these ten 
tribes furnish forth the battle-axe, and the 
weapons of war, whereby the kingdom, that is 
yet to be, shall be founded on the destruction 
of the kingdoms which now are, and of all 
who shall cleave unto their sinking State. 
Having obtained the hold of this great principle, 
by a careful and patient investigation of various 
Scriptures, I would now try its soundness, 
and enlarge its evidence, by an examination 
of other passages, which contain any notices 
concerning the future destination of Ephraim 
and his outcast brethren. | | 
First, let us consider what is said of Ephraim 
im Ps. Ix., which, by the occasion of triumph 
upon which it was composed, and by the 
language of the first five verses, manifestly 
refers to the time when God tums again unto ' 
lás scattered and outcast people; and in his 
controversy for their sakes makes the whole 
«arth to tremble, and be broken: when also 


187 | 


he lifts up that banner which is spoken of. in 
all the prophets, from the great prophecy: of 
the Shiloh, to whom the gathering of the 
people is to be, down to the sixth vial of 
Rev. xvi., when their way is prepared to the 
great battle of the Lord, (Is. xi. 10. 12; xviii. 3; 
Jer. iv. 5, 6.) In this time of the deliverance 
of his beloved, it is said, Ps. lx. 6, “ God hath 
spoken in his holiness; I will rejoice; I will 
divide Shechem; I will mete out the valley 
of Succoth :?” which allusion to these two places 
carries us back to the time of Gideon, when 
he destroyed the men of Succoth for not refresh- 
ing him and his 300 men, in the pursuit of 
Zebah and Zalmunna, and of his son Abimelech, 
who destroyed the men of Shechem who had 
rebelled against him. This allusion to these 
two events gives us a hint, that they were 
typical of the retribution which God will 
render to the false and. treacherous friends 
of his people in the day of their greatest trial 
and victory. Then it is added, in this Psalm, 
« Gilead is mine, and Manasseh is mine; 
Ephraim also is the strength of my head: 
Judah is my lawgiver.” Of Judah, it is the — 
constant attribute to give law, to guide and 
direct: “ The sceptre shall not depart from 
Judah, nor a lawgiver from between his feet, 
until Shiloh come:” and in the day of the 
- exaltation of the mountain of the Lord, “The 
law shall go forth from Zion, and the word of 
the Lord from Jerusalem,” which is the seat 
of the men of Judah. If this be the charac- 
o 2 


188 


teristic of Judah, then is strength that of 
Ephraim. “ Ephraim is the strength of my 
head.” I think the allusion is to the most 
valiant men who were chosen to keep the head 
of the king: as when David fled to Achish, 
king of Gath, and Achish was going out to 
battle against Saul, he said unto David, of 
whose prowess he had often heard, “ There- 
fore I will make thee keeper of mine head for 
ever.” 1 Sam. xxviii. 2. This same office of 
strength, valour, and trust, is given to Ephraim 
in Ps. eviii., as that of government also to 
Judah. Now, I ask, when has this been 
proved in Ephraim hitherto? It was proved 
in Judah, by the giving of the sceptre to. them, 
and shall be more so in the time referred to 
in the’ Psalm, when all nations shall go up to 
Jerusalem from year to year, to worship the 
King, the Lord of Hosts, and to keep the feast 
of tabernacles, Zech. xiv. 16. But by Ephraim, 
nothing great or noble has yet been done to 
entitle her to this high degree in the Lord’s 
confidence ; wherefore, I conclude, it remains 
to be accomplished in the time of the Psalm 
where it is mentioned, which, in Ix. and lvii. 
is ‘beyond all question that of the returning 
and triumphal deliverance of his people. I 
cannot help making one remark farther before 
leaving the Psalms, which is, that none of the 
tribes is mentioned save those of Joseph: and 
Judah ;‘for Moses, Numb. xxxii., gave’ Gilead 
to Machir, the son of Manasseh, who' took it; 
and dispossessed the Amorite that’ was in it; 


189 


and Manasseh, who is mentioned besides 
Gilead and Ephraim, was one of the sons of 
Joseph; so that we have Joseph’s family 
gathered together, and declared to be the 
strength of God’s head, while Judah is de- 
clared to be his lawgiver, besides which no 
others are mentioned. Now, so little had the 
Ephraimite attained to this. character before 
their dispersion, that they were defeated by 
Jephthah and his Gileadites in the midst of 
their saucy and proud boastings, Judges xii. 
So also by the men of Gath, chap. 1. ver. 22: 
and in general it is said of them, Ps. lxxviii. 9, 
“The children of Ephraim being armed and 
carrying bows, turned back in the day of 
battle.” Their mighty promises seem to have 
inflated their minds, and made them boastful 
against Gideon and Jephthah: they wished all 
the renown; but they had no patience nor 
courage for the fight; because the time was 
not yet come. Just as Judah, expecting the 
sovereignty and legislation of kingdoms be- 
fore the time to which the promise referred, 
was cut off in the presumption and pride of 
his heart; so Ephraim, expecting on all occa- 
sions the honour and glory of battle before the 
time against which it will come to him, was 
contumacious against God, and jealous of every 
valiant man. 

Now, very similar to this distinction between 
Ephraim and Judah, is another, which is taken 
by the Prophet Hosea x. 11, against the same 
times, “ I will make Ephraim to ride, Judah 


190 


shall plow, and Jacob shall break his clod.” — 
But this must be studied along with the con- 
text, which wholly concerneth Ephraim and 
his royal city of Samaria. In ver. 9, is stated, 
the long continuance of their backsliding and 
sinfulness, even from the days of the trans- 
gression of Gibeah, which they did not avenge 
as they ought to have done, because of their 
own relaxation of righteous duty : in the 10th 
verse, comes their servitude to the nations, 
like that of a heifer which is bound to tlie 
yoke, and drags continually between two fur- 
rows; then in ver. 11, the Lord beautifully 
represents the altered condition of Ephraim. 
= “ Ephraim is as a heifer that is taught, and 
loveth to tread out the corn; taught as it 
were from my word to know her high distinc- 
tion of treading down,.and spoiling his enemies, 
and loving to be employed in that most glorious 
work: as a heifer loves above all the works 
of husbandry to tread out the corn; in which 
work, being unmuzzled and permitted to eat 
at. their pleasure, the beasts grew fat, and 
plump, and beautiful. “ But I passed. over 
upon her fair neck,” as one that binds her 
to the yoke to plough in the furrow, and 
changes her sleek beauty into bare and galled 
nakedness: I brought her into captivity, and 
dashed to the ground all those lessons of power 
and glory which I had taught her in her 
youth; but he adds, ‘“‘ I will make Ephraim 
to ride, and Judah shall plow, and Jacob 
shall break his clods:” I will restore Ephraim 


19] 


to his promised glory, and he shall ride upon 
the high places of the earth in glory and 
triumph. Judah shall plough, or cultivate 
the nations after the other has trampled their 
grandeur and glory under foot, sending forth 
the feet of the ox and the ass, and sowing 
beside all waters the law and the testimony of 
God: in which diligent work she shall be 
assisted and helped by all the tribes of Jacob, 
whose dignity shall yet be as much inferior 
to the other two, as is his who breaks the 
clods to that of the good husbandman who 
holds the plow, or to that of the master who 
rides at his ease and overlooks the whole. 
That this is the true interpretation, is appa- 
rent from the following: “ Sow to yourselves 
in righteousness, reap in mercy; break up 
your fallow ground; for it is time to seek the 
Lord till he come and rain righteousness upon 
you.” Such, then, in the day of the great seed 
time of righteousness, and reaping time of 
_-blessedness, is the dignity of Ephraim, beside 
Judah and the rest of his brethren: and see- 
ing the rest of this prophecy fulfilled; seeing 
Ephraim bound between her two furrows; 
seeing her fair and voluptuous neck peeled 
and furrowed with the yoke, and scourged 
with the whip; I look to see when she shall 
be made to ride in liberty and power by the 
same Lord who hath brought her into bond- 
age. 

Before quitting this similitude of Ephraim, 
as a heifer treading out the corm, I cannot 


192 


deny myself the satisfaction of referring you 
to the burden of Ephraim, in Is. xxviii., where 
the Lord shews to us, first his fatness and 
lustihood, drunkenness and vain glory; then 
passes over her fair neck with such a scourge 
and desolation as makes one’s ears to tingle, 
for it passes from Ephraim to the whole earth ; 
and descends upon it as it did upon the Ca- 
naanites in the valley of Gibeon, and upon the 
Philistines in Mount Perazim; but mark how 
the chapter concludes with the description of the 
great threshing of the nations, for which Ephraim 
is prepared of old, by that word of Moses, 
‘‘His glory is like the firstling of his bullock, 
and his horns are like the horns of unicorns ; 
with them he shall push the people together 
to the ends of the earth; which ancient de- 
signation of Ephraim to the threshing of the 
nations is recognized in the passage before us ; 
“« Ephraim is a heifer that is taught, and loveth 
to. tread out the corn.” ` Not so with the judg- 
ment on Ariel or Jerusalem, which is con- 
tained in the following chapter of Isaiah, 
whose crime is ignorance of the book, and 
reading of the book, which, as the lawgivers:of 
the earth, they ought to have opened for’ the 
instruction of mankind: and the restoration of 
these is not to the treading and threshing of . 
the: nations; but to understanding, ver. 24. 
“ They also that erred in spirit shall. come ‘to 
understanding, and they that murmured. shall 
learn, doctrine.” This suggests an addition‘ 'to 
the great idea of .Ephraim’s destination, which 


193 


we are endeavouring to unfold, that Ephraim 
was originally set apart by God for valour 
and strength, as Judah for lawgiving and wis- 
dom; that it was a part of God’s purpose it 
should be so; but Ephraim having, in the 
cases of Sisera and Jephtha, those heroic de- 
liverers of Israel, shewn only contumacy and 
saucy discontent, instead of valour and prowess 
for the good of the whole nation; and having 
headed the insurrection of the ten tribes, and 
sought to enthral Judah, and cut off the line 
of- David ; because of her cowardice when there 
was need of valour ; and of her envy when there 
was need of unity; and her discontent when 
she should have rejoiced with the valiant; and 
her insurrection, and her other braggart and 
boastful qualities; because of this desertion 
and abuse of her talent and inheritance, she 
was cut off and sent into ignoble and unknown 
captivity, until the Lord, having sufficiently 
avenged himself upon her, for her disappoint- 
ment of him, shall turn to her again, and make 
her indeed the strength of his head, and the 
weapon of his right hand; and this cause for 
her. rejection is expressly given for her rejection 
in'Ps. lxxviii. “‘ That they turned back in the 
day of battle, and kept not the covenant of 
God, and refused to walk in his law; and 
forgot his works and his wonders that he had 
shewed them.” 

''Phis idea, of Ephraim’s proper and peculiar 
calling unto valour and might in the defence of 
the:‘Lord's glory against his enemies, is- much 


194 


confirmed by Ps. lxxx., to which I would next 
direct your attention. You will remember, that 
it is written in the blessing of God upon Joseph, 
“ hence is the Shepherd and the Stone of 
Israel,” which I supposed to have a literal ful- 
filment, when Christ shall become the Shepherd 
of Israel, to gather them, and to stand and feed 
them in the strength of the Lord; when he 
shall become the stone of Israel, to break the 
fourfold Colossus of their oppressors in pieces ; 
and when, as the breaker, he shall come up 
before his flock, Mic. ii. 13; indeed, I give 
the 12th and 13th verses of the second chapter 
of Micah as a full and sufficient comment upon 
the words of Jacob’s blessing upon Joseph, 
“ hence is the Shepherd and Stone of Israel.” 
Now, the Ixxxth Psalm opens with the very 
name, ‘‘ Give ear, O Shepherd of Israel,” and it 
continues, by giving him a special guardianship 
over Joseph ; yea, as if in this capacity he be- 
longed to Joseph, as regularly as in the capacity 
of the Shiloh he belongs unto Judah: “ Thou 
that leadest Joseph like a flock.” This is a 
very remarkable coincidence indeed with Ja- 
cob’s blessing, and not to be otherwise ex- 
plained than by the supposition that it pertains 
to Christ, when he appears as the Shepherd or 
King of Israel, to manifest himself first unto 
Joseph. And thus it proceeds: “ Thou that 
dwellest between the cherubims, shine forth ; 
before Ephraim, Benjamin; and Manasseh, stir 
up thy strength, and come and save us.” This 
has a reference to the order of march in which 


195 


the army of the tribes were wont to proceed 
through the desert; the camp of Ephraim, 
which consisted of Ephraim, and Benjamin, and 
Manasseh, following immediately behind the 
ark of the covenant and the tabernacle, which, 
encompassed with the Levites, moved right 
onward in their van. And the meaning of 
the supplication is, that the Lord would shine 
forth as in the times of old, and stir up his 
strength before these tribes, which, together, 
did compose the camp of Ephraim; and it is 
remarkable, that none of the other camps 
of Judah, Reuben, and Dan, are mentioned, 
Then the Psalm describes the Lord’s bringing 
up of the goodly vine out of Egypt, and plant- 
ing it in his land, its desolation and wasteness, 
by the invasion of every wild beast of the 
forest, and mightily prays him to return and 
visit this vine ; and “ let thy hand be upon the 
man of thy right hand, upon the son of man, 
whom thou hast made strong for thyself.” Now, 
when I compare these words with the words of 
Joseph’s blessing, the whole mystery opens to 
my view still more. That blessing, which the 
church here prays to be upon the son of man, 
is pronounced by Jacob to his son Joseph, and 
almost in the same words, “ the arms of his 
hands were made strong by the hands of the 
mighty God of Jacob.” This, I say, opens to 
me the whole mystery of Joseph and his chil- 
dren, that they were set to be for a type of 
Messiah in his character of the Shepherd and 
Stone of Israel; as Judah was set to be a type 


196 
of him as the lawgiver and the ruler; and that 
the words, “ from hence is the Shepherd and 
the Stone of Israel,” is as much the key to this 
purpose of Joseph, as “ until Shiloh come,” is 
the key to the purpose of Judah. For no one 
person, except Adam, may be said to have been 
a complete and perfect type of Christ: from 
that time forward the types are in fragments ; 
one presenting one part, and another another 
part; and, like a beautiful Mosaic, they do 
together compose the picture and present the 
similitude of Christ. Joseph, being to this 
chosen, had visions of royal preferment above 
his father and mother, and all his brethren, and 
for the envy. thereof is brought into the pit, an 
emblem of Ephraim’s captivity : and, to perfect 
the resemblance, we have sight of him in the 
dungeon of Egypt: so was Christ, because he 
declared himself to be a king, brought unto the 
dust of death, and the prison-house of the tomb : 
thence Joseph was raised to the right hand of 
Pharaoh, and saved both Israel and Egypt, the 
the church and the world: this came upon the 
‘heart of him that was separated from his bre- 
thren, as it also came into the great Antitype 
who was cut off for the people. To carry on 
this goodly work of typifying the power and 
glory of Messiah in the destruction of all his 
enemies, was Ephraim set apart, and blessed : 
but he failed, and for that failure was cut off, as 
Adam had been before him, and as Judah hath 
been after him. They all were set for types; 
to serve, for the forerunners of Him that was to 


197 


come: they all set up for themselves, to become 
independent, to be as gods, and for this they 
were cut off, and shall continue cut off, until 
the time of the restitution of all things. But if 
the type of Adam is to be recomposed out of 
- the pieces into which Satan broke it, and con- 
stituted glorious upon the earth in the holy city 
of the risen saints; and if the type of Judah, 
the lawgiver and sceptre-bearer, is to be recom- 
posed gloriously in the Jerusalem which is to be 
the metropolis of nations, then shall the type of 
Ephraim, the mighty man of war, and the faith- 
ful father of nations, be also recomposed glori- 
ously in the time when the Lord comes as a 
man of war, shall break all his enemies in 
pieces, and for a while compel the rebellious 
earth, with a rod of iron, to the acknowledgment 
of his sovereignty: then, indeed, shall the Shep- 
herd of Israel lead Joseph like a flock; then 
shall he shine forth from between the cheru- 
bims ; then before Ephraim, and Benjamin, and 
Manasseh, he shall stir up his strength, and 
come and save us. 

_ That the Lord is to make use of Ephraim in 
these days for his battle-axe and weapons of 
war, to break the nations, and destroy the king- 
doms, is further manifest from a very peculiar 
expression used of him in Zech. x. 8: “I will 
hiss-for them” (Ephraim.) To understand the 
meaning of this, you -must know, that in the 
south of Europe to this day, as well as in the 
East, :when: they would call the, attention of, a 
friend,’ and: draw him to them, which we are 


198 


wont to do with a—Hem! they do it with a 
hiss; so that you will hear, as you pass along 
the streets, not the ms, but the ss; and the 
people will instantly turn round to the voice, 
to see who it is that called, and for whom he 
called. Even so, with the ease with which a 
man summons with a hiss his friend close at 
hand, the Lord declares that he shall summon 
Ephraim ; and with the promptitude with which 
that friend turns to the familiar call, and flies 
to the side of his friend who called him, shall 
Ephraim turn in that day unto the Lord, and 
come to the place of his call. Now, there are 
only two other passages in which this form of 
expression is used, and in both cases it is for 
warlike and destructive ends. The first is Isa. 
v. 25: “ And he will lift up an ensign to the 
nations from afar, and will hiss unto them from 
the end of the earth; and behold, they shall 
come with speed, and swiftly :” then, to repre- 
sent the purpose for which they are so swiftly 
summoned by the hiss of the Lord of Hosts, 
‘‘ whose arrows are sharp, and all their bows 
bent, their horses hoofs shall be counted like flint, 
and their wheels like a whirlwind,” &c. Now, all 
this is descriptive of the successive and sweep- 
ing invasions with which the Lord, in his wrath, 
was to overwhelm the people of his covenant, 
when they should have broken it and trampled: 
it under foot, including the Assyrian, the Baby- 
lonian, the Roman, the Goth, the Saracen, the 
Turk, and finally Ephraim, who shall in the 
end be brought up, his swift witnesses, his true 


199 


sanctified ones, who rejoice in his highness. 
And as the former have been so swift and so 
terrible when they came, so swift and terrible 
do I judge the last will be, when the time shall 
come to hiss for Ephraim, and to gather him. 
The other passage is Isa. vii. 18: “ And it shall 
come to pass in that day, that the Lord shall 
hiss for the fly that is in the uttermost part of 
the rivers of Egypt, and for the bee that is in 
the land of Assyria ;, and they shall come, and 
shall rest all of them in the desolate valleys, 
and in the holes of the rocks, and upon all 
thorns, and upon all bushes.” That this is 
spoken of figuratively of the two great military 
powers which the Lord was to bring up from 
these two lands, is manifest, both from the verse 
which precedes and the verse which follows, in 
both of which the king of Assyria is expressly 
mentioned by name; and the desolation he 
would bring is particularly described in the 
verses that follow, to the end of the chapter. 
These two instances, the only ones in Scripture 
where the expression is used, besides this third 
time of Ephraim, would lead us to conclude, 
that for the same end of judgment are warlike 
acts in Ephraim to be hissed for by the Lord 
in the latter times; but that this conclusion 
may not rest upon analogy merely, let us study 
the context, beginning at Zech. x. 7. ‘ And 
they of Ephraim shall be like a mighty man, 
and their heart shall rejoice as through wine; 
yea, their children shall see it, and be glad; 
thine heart shall rejoice in the Lord.” These 


200 


figures of a mighty man rejoicing by means of 
wine, are said of the Lord himself, in Ps. lxxvii. ; 
“The Lord awaked as one out of sleep, like 
a mighty man that shouteth by reason of wine.” 
Then follows a splendid prophecy of their return, 
which I wonder much that any one can gainsay, 
or dare doubt: ‘I will hiss for them, and gather 
them ; for I have redeemed them, and they shall 
increase, as they have increased ; and I will sow 
them among the people, and they shall remem- 
ber me in far countries ; and they shall live with 
-their children, and turn again. I will bring them 
again also out of the land of Egypt, and gather 
them out of Assyria; and I will bring them 
into the land of Gilead and Lebanon, and place 
shall not be found for them: and he shall. pass 
through the sea with affliction, and shall smite 
the waves in the sea; and all the deeps of the 
river shall dry up: and the pride of Assyria .- 
shall be brought down, and the sceptre of Egypt 
shall depart away: and I will strengthen them 
in the Lord, and they shall walk up and down 
in his name, saith the Lord.” 

While I thus maintain the specialty of 
Ephraim’s vocation in the latter times, to do 
the Lord service as a man of war, I deny not 
the like unto Judah, when she shall have been 
delivered out of the bondage of the fourth 
monarchy by the might of Ephraim, and the 
outcast tribes. For though Ephraim no doubt 
be in captivity and cruel bondage somewhere, 
yet is it not within the bounds of the daughter 
of Babylon,. where Judah is held in sore and 


201 


ignominious thraldom. Then the Assyrian, the 
last head of Babylon, the personal: Antichrist, 
or by whatever name you call him, who shall 
rule the nations, must first be broken, before 
the tribes ‘of Benjamin can put forth their 
prowess: and of a long time he hath been or- 
dained to fall upon the holy mountain, before 
that Shepherd whom the Lord will appoint over 
Israel. After which, that Judah will be called 
upon for her arm,. as well as all the other saints 
of God, is manifest, from all the Scriptures 
which speak of these things; and from none 
more than from the first part of this very chap- 
ter, where the future deeds of Judah are cele- 
brated in no mean terms. Ver. 3. “ Mine anger 
‘was kindled against the Shepherds,” who shall 
‘then’ have the rule and government in the 
church, “and I will punish the goats,” in that 
day of judgment when the goats, or apostate 
church, shall be upon the left, and the sheep 
upon the right hand of the Judge; “ for the 
Lord of Hosts hath visited his flock, the house 
of Judah, and hath made them as his goodly 
horse in the battle,” upon which he will ride 
over all the necks of his enemies. The horse 
is the emblem of imperial and sovereign domi- 
nion, which always. pertaineth unto Judah. 
‘Out of him came forth the corner,” (i: e. 
the foundation stone, the tried stone, the pre- 
cious corner stone, the sure foundation laid 
in Zion, which the Apostle Peter applieth unto 
Christ,) “‘ out of him the nail,” as it-is written, 
Isa. xxii. 23, in a prophecy which Christ ap-

shall blow the trumpet, and shall go with 
whirlwinds of the south. The Lord of -Hosts 
shall defend them, and they: shall devour and 
subdue with sling-stones ; and they shall drink, 
and make a noise as through wine; and they 
shall be filled like bowls, and as the corners 
of the altar: and the Lerd their God -shall 
save them in that day, as the flock of his. 
people: for they shall be as the stones of. a 
crown, lifted up as an ensign upon his land: 
for how great is his goodness, and how great. 
is. his beauty! corn shall make the young man 
cheerful, and new wine the maids.” > 

Philalethes.—In our conversation on the ‘ies 
of the Gentiles, and subsequently also, you

would be a striking coincidence, . though not

Apostacy. | 

Anastasius. —You will find such an event 
noted by the Jews themselves: there is a very. 
eloquent precis of the persecutions and injuries 
which they have undergone for. sixteen cen-

ings of the Meeting of Delegates, from the
