CONVERSATION THE ELEVENTH. 


What is revealed concerning the Time-State of the Church? 


- Philalethes —IN our former conversations upon 
the times of the Gentiles, and. the times of the 
Jews, we did not enter much into the subject 
of the Catholic Church; but I am anxious to 
hear what you can produce from Scripture re- 
lating to it. 

Anastasius —Before it is possible to answer 
this inquiry, you must be somewhat more ex- 
plicit in the terms of your proposition. The 
Catholic Church is made by many writers to 
include elect angels as well as elect men, and 
it may properly be extended so as to include 
all that portion of creature, be it composed of 
matter, - spirit, or the highest intelligences, 
which is upheld by the power of God, in virtue 
of the humiliation of HisSon. Does your ques- 
tion then relate to the word Church in this 
sense, or to it in a more confined view ? 

Philalethes—1 mean only so much of the 
Church as relates to it in time; that is, from the 
days of Adam to the period when man shall be 
no more; if ever such a period should arrive. 

Aristo.— Doubtless it is necessary, in the first 


212 


place, to define the meaning of the word Church ; 
and though some of our older divines do use it 
in the sense to which Anastasius has referred, 
yet the more correct definition is, “ the body 
of Christ, with Him its head :” that is, the 
God-man, with those whom it has pleased the 
Father to give him: of these none are manifest 
till the day of redemption of the body. Eph. 
iv. 30; Rom. viii. 23; 1 John iii. 2. One of 
the necessary properties of the church is, that 
in its present state it should be invisible, which 
is the great key to a visible church ; and all- the 
ordinances of.a church are to. help us out. of 
this condition of. invisibility, and to carry. on’ 
worship. by visible. signs ;.sin having produced: 
an obscuration of the Deity, the only proper: 
and lawful object of adoration. This idea will 
therefore not only exclude’ the higher intelli- 
gences from forming a part of our present con- 
sideration, but the millennial state of the church 

also. . First, then, it is to be observed, that for ` 
a Man-Christ-Church, that 1s, a church which: 
was to manifest the mysteries of redeeming love, 
a fall was absolutely- necessary. The Paradi-. 
saical state is the perfect idea of a church set 
forth for an emblem. First, the persons: are 
‘Adam with Eve in his side, which is Christ 
with his church in him, 1 Cor. xv. 45. 47; Eph. 
v. 31, 32. Next, Adam and Eve together, re- 
present Christ and the church in their separate 
conditions, which is that in which we now are. 

Eve presented. to Adam on the morning of the 
Sabbath, is the church presented 40 Christ on 


213 


the morning of the Sabbatism.. The martiage 
of Adam and Eve, and the grant, “ let them 
have: dominion,’ Gen. i. 26, sets forth that the 
‘dominion was to be to them both, and indicates 
‘the marriage of Christ and his church, from 
-whencé there will be great fruitfulness during the 
millennium: so that we have herein represented 
.the spouse looking to her lord; and united to 
him, her consequent dominion, and increase of 
spiritual children. . : 

Anastasius—I have ies wondered at the 
loose manner in which modern divines consider 
the figures of Seripture to be used, and. espe- 
-eiaHy those of the Marriage Supper of the 
‘Lamb, the Wedding, &c.; if they had berne-in 
mind, that marriage was instituted for fruitful- 
‘ness, they would have perceived that there must 
be produce from this marriage also, and that 
therefore the next coming of the Son of Man 
could not be-to burn up this~planet, with all 
its inHabittants, in an instant of time. 

_ Aristo —All the force, propriety, and meaning 
_of divine imagéry is indeed. frittered away. 
Now, passing from the persons, consider also the 
things:in Paradise. There were under Adam all 
-ereatures, and all fruits of the earth, which 
:garden, a well cultivated place, is described in 
-Wyvek. xxviii. 13, as perfect beauty, and is an 
emblem of the: regenerate creation under the 
cohurch’s dominion.. In that garden were the 
tees of life and of death : the one represented 
dhe perfect. state of the church, and the other 
the: possibility of a fall, and thereby revealing 


214 


to : the creature its: own. fallibility : Satan. too, 
the prince of evil, was there. The. ereation 
in its. first state was liable to evil from without, 
and the tempter was: without, yet the means of 
its temptation were: partly within itself; the 
avenue to evil was .within,. the trees being part 
of that creation of which Adam himself was 
head. Man was the last and noblest of God’s 
works, and therefore he was in the highest and 
best circumstances to show the fallibility of 
every creature. He had dominion without a 
rival, and was head of a race, whilst his only 
acknowledgment of inferiority was not to eat 
of one tree; it was the least possible command ; 
the smallest possible test of his holding from 
the Head; the peppercorn rent, by which he 
held from :hiş chief in fee, corroborated by the 
threat, and consequent fear of death. When 
man fell, the church-state properly began, like 
a ship. sent forth from the haven, and reduced to 
a wreck. ‘In communicating to Adam the pro- 
mise of Christ, the church was founded, and 
from that moment the church was suspended 
upon. the hope of Christ; faith was instituted, 
and she looked forward to Him. As long as 
Adam was. -perfect in .Paradise, he was the 
typeof Christ; but when he fell, the type was 
broken, and presented one great lie: thenthe | 
only way. by which Christ could be realized by 
the church was by a promise; for when Adam 
failed of being a visible representation of what 
was to come, then faith was the only possible 
means of that hope being preserved. Adam, 


215 


instead of being: brought into a worse condition 
by the fall, was, with the promise conjoined to 
it, introduced into a higher condition, for the 
very promise, coming. from God, was a rea- 
lity. Adam then became- an heir of that of 
which. he -hàd formerly ‘been ‘only the. symbol. 
This promise was to. the race, and constituted 
the Gospel of the church; this. kept her al- 
ways looking forward, ‘and ¿when the Lord 
cannot get her to look forward, then he brings 
judgment'on her. An outward and visible ordi- 
nance was however necessary. to be superadded 
to the promise, in order to. shew out the future 
object; and this ordinance was sacrifice. ‘The 
promise was, that Christ should bruise the ser- 
pent’s head, and that he should ‘bruise Christ's 
heel; the threat had been death, and conse- 
quently death of all in him; and the reason 
why death did not take hold of him, and of all 
in him immediately, was the pre-determinate 
purpose of God, that His Son should come to 
die. Sacrifice signified that life had been lost, 
and that by the sacrifice of life, life would be 
recovered ; sacrifice, therefore, shews forth the 
redemption; and be it remembered, that the 
fruitfulness of the material earth was held, and 
lost, on precisely the same conditions as the 
ptimeval blessedness of Adam himself. The 
essence of sacrifice consisted in the fire coming 
down from heaven to consume it, in which you 
may perceive the resurrection ; for the resurrec- 
tion of the saints, and purification of the earth, 
is by fire: in this also is a memorial of the 


216 


‘church’s redemption from the grave by the 
baptism of fire, with which all visible things 
‘are to be purged. Here then are the promise 
‘and ordinances which formed the constitution ‘of 
‘the primitive church, such as she continued till 
‘the deluge. Although the principle of election 
‘was not declared till Abraham, yet it is obvious 
that God was acting on this principle, from the 
beginning: for in Seth’s line we have worship, 
prophecy, preaching, salvation, (from the flood,) 
‘and translation. In the line of Cain, we have 
murder, acquisition of property, sensual inven- 
tion, and mining in the earth, to which they took : 
the one line looked on the earth as a cursed 
thing, the other took to it with delight. These 
different objects, pursued by the two different 
lines, are remarkably signified in the meaning 
of their respective names. Confusion again 
came into this church by the children of Seth 
forgetting to look forward to the promise of 
Christ, and joining themselves to the children 
of Cain; and thus they ceased to be a race 
lookmg forward to the redemption of the earth, 
and therefore ceased to be a church: for the 
great object of a church being placed upon the 
earth, is to testify to all that it is under the 
curse, and has need of a redemption. When 
Enoch had fulfiled his testimony, he was not 
taken till Methuselah was born, whose name 
signified the thing of which his father had pro- 
phesied, and of which therefore he became the 
sign: Methuselah’s great age setting forth the 
long-suffering of God, a8 I explained in a former | 


217 


conversation. Before the deluge, there was not 
only a budding of the doctrine of election in 
the family of Seth, which was not developed 
till the time of Abraham, but also a division 
of clean and unclean; and this also was an 
intimation of election. Of every judgment 
there are two parts ; condemnation and punish- 
ment of the wicked thing, to set forth the eter- 
nal judgment; and a salvation of the church, 
to set forth her ultimate salvation. Baptism 
is aù anfitype of this, 1 Pet. iii. 21: it intro- 
duces our mortal creature, the old man, crea- 
ture substance, into the fellowship of Christ’s 
sufferings ; and the spiritual man, that is, the 
man of hope, into the life of regeneration in 
which we are risen, and sit with Him. This 
explains the design of the deluge; the earth 
came into a state of suffering; the creatures 
were brought at that time into a worse con- 
dition than they were before. The ark repre- 
sented the visible church, in which would be 
contained the saved, (as far as man can see,) 
and also the salvation hope, in which was all 
the Gospel that would be set forth. Ham being 
in the ark, shows that the intention of God was, 
that the reprobate should be in the visible 
church. : 

The deluge was a judgment on apostacy. 
Noah coming out of the ark was a new epoch, 
because there was a covenant made, and a pro- 
mise given, that the earth shall be no more 
overflowed with water; which, in the theology 
of it, signifies that the Lord would bring it no 

VOL. II. Q 


218 


lower in the state of suffering ; and the bow was. 
the ordinance of this covenant. The dispensa- 
tion from Noah to Abraham is a kind of pre- 
liminary to the Jewish dispensation ; and it is 
clear that one is included in the other, from the 
promise being made to Shem, that he should 
possess the tents of Japheth. God gave all. 
things to man, except the blood, which he re- 
served for himself. This church was punished 
by the judgment at Babel. They endeavoured 
to keep themselves together, instead of spread- 
ing themselves over the earth, to replenish and 
subdue it, which was an insurrection against. 
God’s purpose: the trespass of the church was 
their putting their trust in the hosts of heaven, 
which they worshipped on that tower, and not 
in the promise of the bow, given to their pro- 
genitor Noah. | 

The calling of Abraham, who was anal from 
the ignorance and idolatry of the nations, was- 
a third epoch in the church. The two new 
ideas set forth to Abraham were, possession of 
the earth, and election. It was necessary that 
Abraham should be unpossessed of every foot 
of ground, and live by promise ; for God shews- 
forth every principle by its contrary, so that 
before there could be a possession shewn forth, 
there must be a no-possession. This was figured 
both by his being called out of his own country, 
and from his fathers home, and also by the 
430 years’ captivity of his descendants; and 
thence the necessity for the delay, that the non- 


-possession might be made clearly out. . So of | 


219 


the election: before Noah’s time the whole 
world was allowed: to come into a state of 
idolatry ; but from the deluge to the end of 
the ages, a chosen body was always to be pre- 
served: The family to whom this possession 
of the earth and preservation from apostacy was 
promised, was obliged not only to be in no pos- 
session, but even brought into bondage, to shew 
the bondage even of these under Satan. . To 
establish election, it was nécessary that the 
whole world should worship the creature, which 
is the worship of the devil. . Abraham. being 
drawn out, set forth that the creature. must be 
brought into the condition of .devil-worship, 
before it.could -be shewn that he can be brought 
to worship the Creator. . The new ordinance 
was circumcision, a sign of righteousness by 
faith: for circumcision to be a good ordinance, : 
it must imply faith, because it was a sign and 
seal of that faith in the promise which had been 
given him; and it expresses also, in most un- 
equivocal language, the true thing signified, 
namely, the cutting off of the flesh ; the cutting 
off of all that comes by flesh, and by natural 
generation, and the transfer of Abraham into 
the church, or saved state. Thus, by every 
visible ordinance, does God shew forth his pur- 
pose to his spiritual church ; bondage in Egypt 
testifying they were slaves, that eternal neces- 
sary state for every creature to pass under the- 
bondage of sin, before it can duly offer the 
highest acts of worship to the Creator; and 
circumcision testifying its, cutting off. of this 
Q 2 


220 


state, in order to enter into another. It is an 
eternal necessary truth, that the creature, be- 
fore it can worship the Creator, must pass into 
the bondage of sin, and be delivered out of it. 
As the covenant of circumcision did not annul 
that of Noah, so that of Moses did not vitiate 
that of Abraham. A great deal of matter is 
conveyed in the history of God’s transactions 
with Moses. There are three codes of laws 
promulgated. 1. Moral; in the giving of which, 
God spake with his own lips, namely, in the 
ten commandments; which gives this the pre- 
eminent distinction. 11. Political. This was 
-given in Moses’ first interview with God, and 
‘was confirmed in the blood of bulls, &c. The 
three feasts at which the people were to appear 
at Jerusalem were part of the nationality upon 
-which they held the land; still further proved 
‘by. the Sabbaths which were given to the land. 
In. Levitical. This was given after the others, 
and after Moses had been forty days with 
God. ‘ 
_ ¥. The moral concerns persons only, and each > 
man as a living soul: it consists wholly of 
negatives, every one of which denies that man, 
in his present state, is fit for the service of God. 
‘Thus it points out the distinction between righ- 
teousness and unrighteousness, and thereby pre- 
pares the man for the Gospel of grace; for the 
doctrine of original sin is the sine gud non to that 
of redemption, and hence is a part of all re- 
ligion, as well as of the Christian. Hence is 
Christ the end of the law as the only righteous 


221 


one. It defined the righteousness of Christ 
_before-hand, and singled him out; and it tes- 
tifies the necessity of a Redeemer, for as it 
expresses the mind of God, it shews that Adam 
could not be competent to keep it. 

11. The political code was ed to shadow 
forth the government into which the church, 
under Christ her head, shall be put in the tri- 
umphant state: this will be realized in Christ, 
the Judge, and the church, the assessor of 
judgment; and that is the reason why Christ 
is oftener called Judge than King, and his 
kingdom oftener the Judgment than the King- 
dom; and hence God set up His polity in a 
Judge, and not in a King. As the judge was 
inferior to the king, although ostensibly exer- 
cismg the government, so the church, the as- 
sessor in judgment, is inferior to Christ the 
King: so that when we sit on Christ’s throne, 
we shall see him sitting upon a higher throne. 
God is not manifested in any ordinance, but 
only in Christ; Christ is represented in the 
ordinances. Slaves entered into the polity of 
Israel, and being placed beneath the freemen, 
indicated that in the blessed state of the church, 
besides the Jewish people in the flesh, over 
whom it rules, there will be the peoples of the 
earth, who were represented by the slaves; and 
hence in Isaiah the word slave is to be con- 
strued only as an inferior state. to the ruling 
people. 

111. The Levitical code was the shewing forth 
of Christ’s incarnation; the temple, his body ; 


222 


and the sacrifices, his sacrifice The holy of 
holies of the temple was evidently emblematical 
of his divinity, into the understanding of which 
the apostles could enter but seldom, while he 
was upon earth: and the vail being rent,- was 
when the Holy Spirit permitted us to enter into, 
and see, the divinity within. The holy place 
represented Christ’s humanity inhabited by the 
Spirit. The Levites represented the true spi- 
ritual church inhabited by the same Spirit; 

while the court of all Israel shewed forth the 
body of Christ visible, that is, the holy flesh, 
which they could look upon, the professing 
church. The court of the Gentiles, whence they 
saw the smoke, and heard the rumour of what 
was going on within, foreshadowed the blessings 
which have ever gone forth from the professing 
church, in the shape of civilization, justice, 
science, &c. The prophets were appointed as 
a counterbalance to the nationality which was 
necessary to their inhabitancy of the land, and 
which would have induced their relapsing into 
a settled state; and the prophets were sent to 
point them forward and upward, as a counter- 
poise to the institutions which draw backward 
and downward. The law was always tending 
to engender a ceremonial righteousness: the 
prophet, therefore, was sent to teach them the 
moral righteousness ; and a fearful office it was, 
for he went to condemn men for their. unholi- 
‘ness, who thought themselves the holiest men 
in the world. The prophets were Christ’s he- 
ralds ; and as’ when the king himself appears 


223 


the heralds fall back in his train, so when 
‘Christ comes the prophets fall back, and Christ 
becomes his own proclaimer. They had to go 
to the king, and to teach him righteousness ; to 
instruct him in the nature and design of his 
office: and though the king was superior, and 
the prophet inferior, yet he might not come into 
the province of the priest. Uzziah was a good 
king, yet was he fearfully punished for intrud- 
ing into the ministry of the altar: and this ex- 
plains also the present standing of the church 
‘In her connexion with the state, and subser- 
vience to the king, who is circum sacra, but non 
apud sacra. | 
Theologus.—The basis of an inquiry into the 
time-state of the church must be the revealed 
predestinative purpose of God. “ In the days 
of the voice of the seventh angel, when he shall 
begin to sound, the mystery of God shall be 
finished, as he hath declared to his servants the 
prophets,” Rev. x. 7. This “ mystery, which 
was kept secret since the world began, but now 
is made manifest, and by the scriptures of the 
prophets, according to the commandment of the 
everlasting God, made known to all nations for 
the obedience of faith,” Rom. xvi. 25, 26; this 
mystery, we know by Col. i. 25, 26, ii. 2, “is 
the mystery of God, ‘even of the Father, and of 
_ Christ,” and which consists in his “calling us 
according to his own purpose and grace, which 
‘was given us in Christ Jesus before the world 
began,” 2 et 1 9, and which is again detailed 
in Eph. i. 9, iii. 3—11, in order that “at the 


224 


name of Jesus every knee should bow,” Phil. 
-ii. 10, as all creation is represented as doing, 
in Rev. iv. 11, “for thou hast created all things, 
and for thy pleasure they are and were created.” 
The end, then, of God’s counsels, was the mani- 
festation of the risen God-man. Creation was 
made in the purview of his resurrection from the 
dead ; in the form of risen God-manhood all has 
been built, and all depend. He on the throne 
(Rev. v. 6,) is Christ, as the representative of 
God, while the Lamb that had been slain is in 
full possession of the seven eyes of the Spirit. 
In order to bring about this end, God’s course 
was that which became him. The means were, 
that we should have redemption through the 
blood of his Son, Col. i. 13—18: blood implies 
that there must be humanity, and that therefore 
all things were made in risen God-man, from 
which all things begin, and in which they all end. 
Mystery is God’s plan ; truth is God’s counsel of 
separation, told out in words and works ; faith is 
consent to, approbation of, and reliance on it. | 
“ God is love,” and the first principle in God’s 
acts is his goodness. Goodness comprises all ex- 
cellence; in a limited sense it is his beneficence. 
If God does an act, he has a design in it; and 
the object of that act must be drawn from him- 
self as the ultimate object. If man does a thing 
without an object, we consider him weak ; but 
God can do nothing without a plan. 
Philalethes.— It seems a strange mode of talk- 
ing of God, to say that he can do nothing with- 
out a plan. _ l 


225 


Anastasius.—You have commenced this con- 
versation by mooting a question which involves 
the consideration of points of the deepest theo- 
logy. I fear, by your expression of surprise, 
that your reading in religion has not gone be- 
yond the Reviews and Magazines of the present 
day, or that annual deluge of trash which issues 
from the press under the name “ Practical Ser- 
mons:” as if the practice of any person could 
be independent of his principles! The ideas of 
their authors are about as wise as that of a 
gardener who would expect to gather fruit from 
a tree irrespective of its roots. I beg your atten- 
tion to what a master in our Israel says on this 
point. ‘ All things that are have some opera- 
tion, not violent or casual: neither doth any 
thing ever begin to exercise the same, without 
some fore-conceived end for which it worketh. 
And the end which it worketh for is not ob- 
tained, unless the work be also fit to obtain it 
by ; for unto every end, every operation will not 
serve. That which doth assign unto each thing 
the kind, that which doth moderate the force 
and power, that which doth appoint the form and 
measure of working, the same we term a law. 
So that no certain end could ever be attained, 
unless the actions whereby it is attained were 
regular; that is to say, made suitable, fit, and 
correspondent unto their end, by some canon, 
rule, or law: which thing doth first take place 
in the works even of God himself.” And again: 
“ God worketh nothing without a cause. All 
those things which are done by him, have some 


226 


end for. which they are done; and the end for 
which they are done, is the reason of his will 
to do them.—That, and nothing else, is done 
by God, which to leave undone were not so 
good.” Hooker, vol. 1. p. 199, 201. 

T heologus. —If I had apprehended that Phi- 
lalethes was so nice a critic, I could have easily 
fortified myself with expressions from human 
authorities; but to say the truth, | am more 
anxious to convey my meaning, and leave it to 
him to receive or reject whatever parts of it he 
shall find consistent with the written word of God, 
than to defend what I advance from any ob- 
jections which may be brought. | 

The far end of God isthe communication of bles- 
sedness. It must be a great undertaking to confer 
this, because, if blessedness be conferred, the 
source of it must be made manifest; and it is 
therefore important to consider what God would 
‘have to do in order to effect it. God can only do 
this by communicating of his own being, for he 
only is good: he must therefore make manifest 
that the good is only in him, and out of the sub- 
ject of the blessing, and that all other things can 
have none; or, as your friend Hooker has ex- 
pressed it, “ the general end of God’s eternal 
working is the exercise of his most glorious 
and abundant virtue—not that any thing is 
made to be beneficial unto him, but all things 
for him, to shew beneficence and grace -in 
them.” The only way in which he can thus 
communicate of his blessedness, is. by making 
- himself. work in the substance he blesses ;. that 


`~ 


227 


is, by working in and by, and at the same time 
keeping himself distinct from that being; that 
is, by energizing in Christ; that is, by making 
himself evermore present in the substance whieh 
he blesses. This must be made manifest, or 
what proof would there be that God does not 
bless himself? This is the only escape from 
Pantheism. Pantheism is the blending of the 
creature with the Creator: man cannot go far- 
ther than to assert the eternity of the creature. 
In order to manifest that the good is from the 
Creator, it is necessary that the creature should . 
be made to undergo a change of form, but not 
of material, by a reproduction in two separate 
forms. This change would suggest that there 
was a power out of itself superior to the crea- 
ture; but the proof would be, that it is brought 
into a deteriorated state with distinction, as 
the creature would not be the willing subject of 
its own deterioration. Herein lies the principle 
of election and rejection. Thus we have the 
elements of God’s counsel in Christ, namely, 
that there must be an intermediate state of 
the creature; for if it had its blessedness at 
the first, there would be no proof of its- not 
being God. There must be a cause for the 
change, or God would be fickle ; and hence the 
necessity of sin being introduced, for there 
would be no reason for God destroying the 
works of his own hands. There must be an 
upholder, who can uphold it, notwithstanding 
its declension; and I cannot understand how 
the unchangeableness and constancy of God is — 


228 


maintained, unless there is a disruption of the 
creature. If it is necessary, for the full mani- 
festation of God, that there should be a change 
in the creature, this can only be brought about 
by the introduction of sin. Hence arises the 
necessity of such an upholder as would retain 
a part of the creation, notwithstanding its hav- 
ing changed; and this upholder must be God 
himself, in order to put aside the lie of Pan- 
theism. These are the elements of the system 
of the Gospel of Christ, and assuming the facts 
of the death and resurrection, the same truths 
would be developed in the inverse order to that 
in which I have now stated them. 

Philalethes.—The inverse order is the more 
usual way of arriving at the Divine a in 
the Incarnation of his Son. 

Anastasius.—It is so; but the most perfect 
way of attaining knowledge is, by the joint 
processes of analysis and synthesis. Nothing 
is more absurd, or marks a greater ignorance 
of the real difficulties in religion, than to con- 
sider the doctrines of election, reprobation, pre- 
destination, &c. as belonging to Christianity, 
or even to revealed religion. They belong to 
the actual and tangible condition of mankind, 
and indeed to the whole visible animal creation. 
One individual.is born to an accumulated mass. 
of suffering, which never quits him until he is 
laid in the tomb; another to every enjoyment 
which his nature is capable of receiving: the 
real sticking place is the origin of this distinc- 
tion, and specially of the evil in one, which the 


229 


writers by common consent avoid; and hence 
the weakness and inconclusiveness with which 
those other branches of the subjects are treated. 
The time-state of the church is the time of 
her sin. Sin is the transgression of the law: 
the law is a transcript of God’s requirements 
from his creatures, and is therefore unchange- 
able, as he is himself unchangeable; but as the 
creature is not unchangeable, for, if he were, he 
would be God, the creature cannot be conformed 
to the law, which is unchangeable. Sin, there- 
fore, is a condition of the creature, simply as 
creature, and nothing can prevent his changing, 
that is, departing from the law, but the almighty 
arm of Omnipotence itself: hence Christ could 
not be the Redeemer, unless he were God ; for 
creature could not uphold creature. 

= Philalethes —What do you mean by saying 
that if the creature were unchangeable he would 
be God? 

Anastasius,—It will answer your purpose much 
better to reply to this question in the words of 
one of our old standard divines, in order that 
you may make use of them to others, as more 
people will yield to an authority than will be 
at the trouble of examining a question for them- 
selves ; and so great is the historical ignorance 
of theological facts amongst the principal evan- 
gelical preachers in London, that I could men- 
tion scores between Islington and Clapham, 
who have from their pulpits, and in their Maga- 
zines, called the views of the second coming of 
our Lord, and the other subjects which we have 


230 


discussed, connected with that glorious event, 
novelties, and opinions unheard of till the pre- 
sent day, except from the Fifth-Monarchy Men. 
“ Whatsoever is immutable by nature is God; 
whatsoever is God is immutable by nature: 
some creatures are immutable by his grace and 
power. The holiness, happiness, and wisdom 
of saints and angels, as they had a beginning, 
so they are capable of increase and diminution, 
and of an end also; for their standing is not 
from themselves, or from the nature of created 
strength, holiness, or wisdom, which in them- 
selves are apt to fail, and finally to decay ; but. 
from the stability and confirmation they have by 
the gift and grace of God. There is a muta- 
bility in us as creatures, and a creature cannot 
but be mutable by nature, otherwise it were not 
a creature, but God.” You cannot do better 
than study Charnock, from whose works these 
passages are extracted, and also St. Augustine: 
but you must be prepared to hear every view or 
statement, which rises to the level of those of 
our forefathers, stigmatized as a ‘novel doc- 
trine” by the ignorant reviewers, and sermon 
writers of this generation.

have taken of the divine perfections, as revealed 
in the mode in which mankind are interested 
in them, is necessary to the growth of Chris- 
tians into the knowledge and enjoyment of God ; 
and my observation has led me to perceive, that 
our modern preachers, having departed from the 
practice of the fathers of our church, iń dis-

mystery, is revealed in order that we may study 
and know it: and the passage in the Epistle to 
the Colossians, urges us to the consideration of

clusively of the blood-shedding of Christ: the 
mystery of God in Christ is hidden, or shut up 
in him as treasure, into which we are to dig if 
we want to grow in the knowledge of God; 
“ yea, if thou criest after knowledge, and liftest 
up thy voice for understanding : if thou seekest 
her as silver, and searchest for her as for hid 
treasures, then shalt thou understand the fear 
of the Lord, and find the knowledge of God.” 
Prov. ii. 3—5.

Anastasius.—This is a point of the utmost

difficult thing in the whole range of personal

child is conceived. Original sin consists in 
every human being that comes into the world, 
being the offspring of the cursed lump, his sub-

his being from that -cursed one. The death 
threatened to Adam cannot be eternal death, 
because it was God’s design that some should 
not be destroyed by it. Death, in Hebrew, means 
dissolution. The first death consists of dissolu- 
tion, that is, of separation of soul and body, 
and is effected by the body dropping off from 
the soul. The second death consists in the 
adjunct of a body communicating torment to 
the soul; so that the loss of a soul is the re- 
ceiving of a body in which to be miserable for 
ever. Adam receiving Eve during his deep 
sleep, when she was taken out of his side, Gen. 
ii. 21, is a type of the Lord Jesus receiving his 
people through his own sufferings and death.

' Anastasius.—This mode of considering the 
truth is most important. It has been stated 
very clearly by Mr. Vaughan, in his Popular 
Essays, a work which I also recommend to 
your attentive perusal. The importance con-

that in some future age the damned will. be 
released from hell, and thus, sooner or later, 
all mankind will be ultimately saved. It is so

fancy himself more merciful than God, that it 
is the predominant feeling of every one who 
is ignorant of the Bible, or who will not believe 
God to be such an one as he has described 
himself to be, and is the most ordinary lure 
which Satan holds out to reflecting minds. The 
rationale, therefore, of the fact revealed, that 
out of the originally broken Adam two seeds

Theodorus.—The leading figure of the rela- 
tionship existing between Christ and his church 
is that of conjugal union, reflected from the 
previously existing substance before the world 
began. There are three states leading to it in 
the East, 1. The fixing of the affections; 2. 
Betrothing ; 3. Union. The church in her pre- 
sent state is as a bride taken captive, and 
brought into jeopardy, out of which she is being 
delivered by her Lord. — 

Aristo.—The expression of some divines, to 
denote the present aspect of the church is good, 
where they call it, “a church in dispersion.” 


239 


The fulfilment of God’s covenant in its pro- 
gress was in mercy building up a people; but 
when they vexed him beyond all example, his 
countenance was changed towards them, and 
his wrath began upon his own Son, and ended 
upon the whole people. Thus the present 
church, without a home, policy, or territory, is 
really a suffering church, which is a great and 
pregnant truth, as it enables us to apply to 
ourselves, in a spiritual sense, all that applies 
to the Jews in all her troubles in the literal 
sense. But above all, the present church ought 
to have learned that her true standing was as 
wild olive trees, and her members ought not so 
to have under-rated the hope set before them, 
as to say, that the present state was any thing 
like her ultimate end. The difficulty was how 
to keep the scattered church as one body, and 
this is the mystery of the Christian ritual. The 
sacrament of baptism defines the church, draws 
the outline round it, and, like circumcision, pre- 
serves the idea of transmission from generation 
to generation, and keeps its unity in time. 
Its unity in place is kept by the Supper, and 
all churches suppose, that those who partake 
of the former should partake of the latter; and 
if all the baptized were to sit down at the 
Supper, there would be a complete communion 
and community of Christ’s body.

blished by law, and so united to the state, how 
can you call it a church in dispersion ?

« damnable and heretical,” does hold such sen-

as watchmen in our Zion, and if this nation is 
to be saved from that destruction, which will 
assuredly overtake every part of the Popish 
Apostacy, it must be by all real Christians,

again and again against it. 

Philalethes—How would you distinguish the 
Apostacy from all other religious error ? 

_Anastasius—We have, as members of the 
Catholic Protestant Church, duties to perform, 
as well as what belongs to us as individual 
Christians; the sin of schism has been much 
lost sight of lately, yet there is such a sin, and 
it is our duty, as members of the Protestant 
Church, to shew that we are not guilty of it, 
which we must be, unless we can prove that 
the Popish Church is the Apostacy. In order 
to do this, I pass over the symptoms and signs 
of the Apostacy which are given in the New 
Testament, such as its “ forbidding to marry, 


243. 


and “commanding to abstain from meats;” 
“ sitting in the temple of God, shewing himself 
that he is God,” “drunk with the bloodof the mar- 
tyrs” (and if it can be shewn, that the idolatries of 
Carthage, or Juggernaut, or Moloch, are more 
deluged in human gore than the Papacy, then, 
and not till then, may we believe that the 
description is-of some other than of her ;) a de- 
scription so graphic, that he who runs may read ; 
but I pass over all these symptoms, because, 
however descriptive, they do not treat of the 
points which constitute the Apostacy, in order 
to come at once to the root of the matter. We 
shall see this most clearly, by calling to mind 
the declared purpose of God, in establishing 
a visible church amongst us Gentiles. 1. This 
we are told in Acts xv. was in order that God 
might “ take out of the Gentiles a people for 
His name :” the Jewish Church had borne faith- 
ful witness to the name of Jehovah, but when 
God, in the fulness of time, had a new name 
to record, even the name of Jah-Osea, God 
the Saviour, Jesus the only way to the Father, 
the only intercessor, the Jewish Church refused 
to bear witness to this name, and therefore a 
church was taken out of the Gentiles for that 
purpose. 1. As the Jewish Church had a 
temple-service adapted to the witness it was 
to bear, so the Gentile Church had its ordi- 
nance, adapted to the witness it was to bear, 
which ordinance consisted in “ the foolish- 
ness of preaching,” by which it was to offer 
eternal salvation by means of that name alone,

Philalethes—The Papists point to Magna 
Charta, and ask, whether it was not won by 
Popish Barons? 

Anastasius.—But they have not much need. to 
be vain of it; for although they gained great 
privileges for themselves, they gained none for, 
nor gave any to, their own vassals; and al- 
though they had no objection to rebel against 
their lawful sovereign for their own advantage, 
_ they had no objection to see him deliver up his 
crown into the hands of a Popish Priest. 

Philalethes.—Do you think there is any dan- 
ger now to be apprehended in this country 
from Popery ? 

Anastasius.—I do not suppose that Popery will 
ever again become the established religion of 
England, because, I think, that there is too 
little religious principle in the country for men 
to care about any Established Church, and 
that the probable result of what is goimg on 
will be the downfall of all churches alike, true 
and false; but I do not doubt, that if our go- 
vernment shall by any act cease to be a govern- 
ment protesting against popery, God will from 
that moment inflict sore judgments on the 
land. If we believe that not a sparrow falls 
to the ground without his especial permission, 
can we believe that God is indifferent to whether 
a true church or an apostate church is the 
national church of a people? 


247 


Philalethes.—Why do you think there is little 
religious feeling among the Papists in Britain ? 

Anastasius.—I see signs of the infidel spirit 
of the age among them in the habitual scoffing 
at, and public ridicule of, the Scriptures, which 
takes place in the Popish debates in Dublin, 
and which the priests listen to without reprehen- 
sion; a circumstance of which there is no ex- 
ample in the world, since Christianity prevailed 
in Christendom, except in the debates of the Ja- 
cobins during the worst times of the French 
Revolution. I perceive it also in noblemen and 
gentlemen of education and respectability, 
taking the part of, and making common cause 
with, those infamous priests, who have been 
lately convicted of abominable crimes in Dublin 
and Paris. I perceive it also in the cry for 
universal education which the Popish laity set 
up, and the clergy fall in with, because they 
dare not resist it, finding the world so mad 
after it, since it must cut up all their superstition 
by the roots: and also in the Popish politicians 
joining in the sentiment, that “ the people are 
the source of legitimate power;” the Bible says, 
“the powers that be are ordained of God,” so 
that the sentiment is nonsense, let it be uttered 
by whom it may; but as Shakespeare makes 
Caliban call himself, a “ thrice double ass,” 
so such a sentiment is “ thrice double” nonsense 
in the mouth of a Papist.

Apostacy. The world has never known very 
well how to hit at the people of God, because

as it thought, though without being able very well 
to define how, contained the greatest number of 
them. Hence the antipathy expressed against 
Methodism, Calvinism, Dissenters, &c., which 
has been so necessary an accompaniment to 
those who were candidates for the wealth and 
power of the Established Church. |

With respect to qualifying for office, the church

Anastasius.—There is a wide difference be-

respects outward forms of: human invention, 
which may vary ad libitum, and abandoning 
fundamental principles which are of paramount 
and equal importance in every age, and under 
every circumstance. . No King, nor Parliament, 
has the power to substitute a test for Chris- 
tianity, other than that which the Lord has ap- 
pointed, namely, Baptism and His Supper, and 
therefore all subscriptions, declarations, oaths, 
&c. are idle, or else an intrusion of Se into 
ecclesiastical jurisdiction. 

Theologus. .—The principle of these restrictive

of the State, implies the separation of the 
Church from the State, and of religious from 
political duty: by this, the false opinion is 
sanctioned, that kingly’ Government ‘is a mat-

pediency ; that the people, and not God, are 
the source of legitimate power; that power is 
delegated from the people to their rulers ; and; 
consequently, may be resumed by the people, 
whenever the people shall deem such resumption 
to be for their good:: wheréas. the true founda-

be are ordained of God;” that the king is 
Christ’s Vicegerent during the whole time-state. 
of the church, and delegate of Him who is King 
of kings, Lord of lords, the only Ruler of 
princes, the representative of Him .in His. 
kingly office, even as the church is the repre-

all distinction be abolished between Christian 
men and atheists, it is. impossible, with any.

Anastasius. —I have dready explained that no

. Anastasius.—Hence arises its danger; “when 
they are crying Peace, Peace, then sudden 
destruction overtakes them. Neither will they 
ever believe it, until some great cause arises to 
make it manifest to their senses. No human 
being would have credited it, had he been told, 
in the year 1788, that in five years the royal 
family of France would be put to death, the 
whołe priesthood extirpated, and the Christian 
religion. abolished; nor could this have’ taken 
place unless infidelity had been universally 
diffused: through the mass of the people. In- 
fidelity is in full triumph under the name’ of 
liberalism; and a more dangerous principle 
never pervaded the world than unsanctified 
benevolence. The very term “ Liberalism,” 


258 


ought to be sufficient to open men’s eyes. Re- 
ligion signifies a system of bindings; whether 
to God as Supreme, to the state as subjects, or 
to our neighbours, in all the various relationships 
of child to parent, wife to husband, servant to 
master. It inculcates controul of ourselves, and 
the submission of the will and inclination of the 
individual to the well-being of another. Liberal- 
ism is the very opposite of all this; it is a sys- 
tem of unbindings, of setting free from all ties: 
it inculcates that. religion is only an affair be- 
tween each man and his Maker; that we should 
not disturb the creed of another: it teaches its 
disciples to say, “ Let me do as I like, and you 
shall do as you like.” Self is its idol, whose 
dictates alone it is to follow. Thus it is the 
very essence of selfishness ; self its motive ; self 
its end; self, all: pursuing its own plan for its 
own solitary advantage, and drawing all its mo- 
tives of action from the confined and. narrow 
centre of its own sensuality. Yet this delusion 
of liberalism has seized upon many whom I would 
yet fain hope areGod’s servants: I earnestly pray 
that they may be delivered from the pit into 
which they are fallen: and I speak from: ex- 
perience, for I was once much entangled in the 
Same snare, when I say that nothing but.the 
study of God's. prophetic Word: can ‘save. any- 
of us from this specious error of Kania bile

promised kingdom in the west, and -denied 
Christ and his promised kingdom in the east. 
Now observe the two opposite methods of 
Christs revelation of his justice upon these 
two. The former, because it carried with it a 
certain image and resemblance of the truth, he 
endured, and doth endure unto this day; yea, 
and in many places he preseryes faithful wit-

destruction. The similitude of himself he will 
suffer long; the denial of himself he will be 
Swift to destroy. Accordingly, he brought up 
first his unconquered Ishmaelites to chastise and

Anastasius.—I entreat you: to. = the in- 
struction which is conveyed to us at the present 
moment, by the uniform dealings of God 
towards his church, both in the Jewish and 
in the Gentile times. The ten tribes not’ only 
revolted from the royal line of Judah, and 
thereby -renounced - their allegiance to. the 
Messiah, who was proniised in that line; but 
set up another object of worship, whereby they 
renounced his priesthood also: for doing this, 
God swept them with the besom of destruction 
by the hand of the Assyrian from off the face 
of the earth. Judah, although she became 
idolatrous, and committed many sins, for which | 
she suffered sore judgments, yet kept her 
foundation of temple worship, &c., and God 
preserved her until Shiloh came. 

In the Gentile Church, the heresy which 
sprung up in the East was similar to that which 
seized on Ephraim, striking at the very root 
of the true worship of God, and. he therefore 
exterminated her by the hand of the Turks. 
The apostacy which arose in the West, like 
that of Judah, covered, indeed, the pure wor- 
ship of Jehovah with many idolatries, but her 
foundations remained sound ; and in like mercy 
as he shewed to Judah, he has. preserved the 
Papacy to this day. 

England, by renouncing Christianity as an 
essential in the office-bearers of the state, has 
committed the same sin that was committed 


264 


first by Israel, and subsequently by the Eastern 
Church: for the great council of the nation, 
with her hierarchy assembled, has declared, 
that a disciple of Mahomet is as fit to be a 
ruler of the nation, and of the patronage of the 
church as a disciple of Christ. Will not God 
treat England as he treated Israel, and as he 
treated Constantinople ? | 

Philalethes:—The Dissenting Churches have 
got into such a habit of taking for granted 
that Babylon, Popery, and Establishments are 
synonymous terms, that they will have great 
difficulty in discerning thé scriptural marks of 
RPOR EEY 

Anastasius.—This arises partly from the origin | 
of dissent, for as it sprang principally from the 
Episcopal pulpits ceasing to preach the Gos- 
pel, it was a very natural conclusion for the 
Dissenters to come to, that the Church of Eng- 
land, and all Established Churches, were in- 
cluded in the term Babylon; but they are not 
very explicit in what they mean by Babylon : 
the only thing they can object to, upon scrip- 
tural grounds, in the Established Church, is 
a supposed right or power of the temporal 
authorities to interfere with the ministers and 
sacraments of the church: yet this they cannot 
mean, for such objection could not be sup- 
ported against the Church of England, but 
may be charged against many Dissenting 
Churches. I am acquainted with several, 
where the chapels are in the possession of 
laymen as trustees, who have .the power ‘of 


265 

ejecting the Minister from his congregation, 
if he preaches any thing = of which these 
laymen disapprove : this is a true interfe- 
rence of secular power in the ministration of 
the word, the very crime against which we 
are warned by the fearful example of Uzziah, 
and against which I have heard many an igno- 
rant Dissenter sneer, as if it were the cha- 
racteristic of the Church of England, merely 
because it is established by law, and as if his 
own churches were free, merely because they 
made no part or parcel of the Ecclesiastical 
Government of the land. 

But do not let us pursue this pubject: any 
farther: the Time-State of the Catholic Church 
Militant will soon close, and let us spend the 
little that remains in strengthening each other’s 
hope, in turning our own attention, and that 
of all the brethren, to the great events for 
which we are looking : it will not be enquired 
of us in what communion we were born, nor 
under what denomination we worshipped. God 
has had his hidden ones in all: He has blessed 
the labours of all in their several spheres ; 
all have been in error in some things; but 
all have had amongst them some who have 
been partakers of the Holy Ghost. Encou- 
ragement, and incessant preaching of the com- 
ing kingdom of Messiah, not recrimination, 
must now be our business. Every orthodox 
sect not only contains some of the people of 
God, and some false brethren, but each has 

VOL. II. T 


266 


been. permitted also to bear its testimony to 
some particular portion of truth which another 
has forgotten or obscured. Thus, although na- 
tional establishments have testified to the duty — 
of temporal rulers to provide for the religious in- 
struction of the people, they have been tempted 
to barter the truth itself for worldly emoluments. 
Although the non-conformists have run into a 
fearful forgetfulness of the kingly office of Christ, 
they have testified that His kingdom is not of 
this world. Although the Baptists have erred very. 
widely in their views of a visible covenant and 
church, they have testified against the heresy: of 
the opus operatum, and identity of water-baptism 
with spiritual regeneration. Although the Qua- 
kers have departed from the written directions 
to observe certain forms and ceremonies, and re- 
specting the lawfulness of oaths and war, they 
have testified to the Spirit as the only worker 
during the time-state of the church ; against the 
idolatry of the written word, and against the 
desecration of the name of God, so often pre- 
scribed by. the laws of the land. 

Philalethes—Have you any positive proof that 
dissenting churches have ever held scriptural 
views respecting the second coming of TANPE

‘CONVERSATION THE TWELFTH. 
What are the Types which prefigure the God-man in his two 
States of Humiliation and Glory? 

Philalethes.—You have frequently alluded, in 
‘the course of our conversations, to different cir- 
cumstances as types of others; but I do not 
remember that you have pointed out any one 
Person or Thing, which clearly depicted our 
Lord Jesus Christ in his two states of Humilia- 
tion and Glory. 

Anastasius.—It is impossible that any one 
person or thing should completely prefigure the 
Lord in any one of his characters, still less in 
any two of them. There are some good re- 
marks in Hartwell Horne on the subject of 
types, which he has collected from various 
authors ; but almost all writers limit themselves 
too machi in treating of types. 

Thraso.—Christ is himself the type of God, 
which we shall know when God shall be all in 
all. Since all created things have their sub- 
sistence in and through Christ, so all visible 
things do more or less typify Christ, and mani- 
fest, each in their character, some part of his 
character, and shall be all headed up in him, 
as their full and complete antitype. No one 
type can express Christ, in whom is all the 
fullness of God, nor completely shew forth any 
one part of his character; but many types must 


270 


concur, one prefiguring one part, and another 
prefiguring another part, as the finite extent of 
creation can body him forth, and our limited 
faculties comprehend. him. The only type of 
the Godhead dwelling in the humanity, with - 
which I am ‘acquainted, is the angel in the 
bush, and this appearance seems to be alluded 
to with that idea in Deut. xxxiii. 16. Adam is 
a type of Christ, as head of his first creation ; 
-Melchizedek is a type of him as ‘Priest :and 
‘King; David, as Prophet and King ; Solomon, 
‘as King of Peace: on his millennial throne ; 
Joshua, as’ the conqueror who takes possession 
of the land by the extirpation of his enemies. 
‘The Patriarchs are types of him in another 
‘series of character. Abraham, of him to whom 
the land is promised, which he never possessed.. 
Isaac, as the heir; Jacob, of -him in weakness 
and suffering; and. Israel, of ‘him :in strength, 
but not in glory. In lke manner, Christ at his 
‘first coming was frequently a type of -himself 
at his second coming, as we observed in a 
former conversation, respecting his riding into 
Jerusalem on the ass’s colt: and thus his trans- 
figuration was a type of his glory. 
Valertus.—The observations of Owen, respect- 
img types in general, are very sound; he divides 
‘them into legal, natural, and historical, -and 
these again into what relates to persons, events, 
places, and things. All commemorative types 
yelate: to time, -and therefore they. must have an 
order. and time for their accomplishment. -I 
will: not enumerate those which may be eol- 


271 


lected from books that are easily to be obtained, 
such as M‘Ewen, Goodwin, Horne, Owen, &c. ; 
but L will suggest, for your consideration, a cir- 
cumstance connected with the birth of Isaac, 
which is generally overlooked, and which is 
worthy of reflection, not only on its own ac- 
count, but also on account of its connexion with 
the meaning and type of original sin. Isaac in 
his birth was a type of being born in the like- 
ness of sinful flesh, as he was born after 
Abraham was circumcised, and Sarah being 
past conception, intimated, that he was born 
without the sign of the fal: Mary also, who was 
espoused or engaged to Joseph, had not come 
to him, whence it has been fairly concluded, 
that she was as much before the ordinary age 
of puberty as Sarah was beyond it, and there- 
fore likewise wanted the sign of the fall. Jacob 
changing his name to Israel, represents the 
church changing from humiliation and suffering 
to glory. 

Philalethes.—I have heard it often maintained, 
that we ought not to consider any thing in the 
Old Testament a type, which has not been de- 
clared to be so in the New Testament. | 

Anastasius —That is a very pernicious senti- 
ment, and, I believe, lies at the very antipodes 
of.sound doctrine on the subject. Every thing 
in the material universe tells one some great 
Spiritual truth, and if our eyes were not holden 
by sin, and consequent ignorance, we should be 
able to perceive it. In this branch of divinity, e 
the Hutchinsonian writers are, for the most 


272 


part, very. sound, as indeed is. Hervey also:in.. 
his Meditations. There is but one caution to: : 
. be observed upon the subject, and that is not to. 
make use of types as the bases of doctrines or 
arguments, butonly as illustrations of thosetruths | 
which have been derived from less disputable - 
sources. To be a type, the thing must be wor-. 
thy of its. antitype; and thus an individual 
celebrated in the olden time is not a type-in 
every circumstance of his life, but only. in that 
by which he was most remarkable. Events 
occurred to Adam, Noah, Abraham, Isaac, 
Joseph, Moses, Joshua, David, and Solomon, 
such as never occurred to any other individual : 
all these events then, and they in these events, 
are all typical of Him in whom all the-wonders 
of eternity and of time, of spirit and of matter, 
are concentrated. , : 

Aristo.—There is great necessity for ¢ caution 
in the interpretation of types, lest they should 
be strained to justify and support all kinds of 
false doctrine; we should likewise distinguish 
between subjects, which are well adapted: :for 
the edification of our own minds, and such as 
are better united for the conviction of gainsayers. 
-: -Anastasius—In this respect types resemble 
parables, in so far as they are not to be strained 
‘beyond the particular point for which they are 
used; a remark which will apply to the use of 
S also. 

Aristo.— Every thing i in Gredtion was aud 
‘whether we can see it or not, and we cannot 
tell what is to be excluded; though the. more 


273 


we enter into details, the more probability is 
there that we may differ from others in seeing 
things: in that light; yet not to use them at all, 

is to undervalue what God has provided for our 
edification. -Adam-was a three-fold type of 
Christ; Ist, in that he had supremacy over all 
the material universe, as the angels have over 
the invisible world ; 2d, because he contained a 
seed iz him, which the angels do not; 3d, as 
a federal head, that is, he stood for, and in the 
place of, others, and communicated something 
to those in him. , Adam conveyed death : Christ 
conveyed resurrection from death. ‘“ As in 
Adam all die, so in Christ shall all be made 
alive:” and the expressions in -Rom. v. 17, 
prove something greater in the antitype than in 
the type. The “ natural body” is that which 
is proper to a soul; but “a spiritual body” is 
that which is proper to a spirit. The apostle 
could not have made the difference appear but 
by the use of all the preceding metaphors, which 
he gives in 1 Cor. xv. By Rom. vi. 11, we 
learn, that possession of the Spirit gives 
us an inheritance in the resurrection, from 
whence also it is to be inferred, that Adam’s 
body was not originally corruptible. Christ’s 
power is put forth in the Holy Spirit, and there- 
fore He is termed, “ a quickening or life-giving 
Spirit.””. The expression, “ let us make man in 
our image,” is, in fact, let us make man in the 
form, or pattern, or type of him that is to come, 

that is, in the image of God-man: I do not 
offer this interpretation instead of, but in addi- 


274 


tion to, the ordinary explanation given of it. In 
his temptation Adam stood in his own creature- 
strength, or man-power, and demonstrates the 
necessary consequence of leaving the creature's 
will free to choose what its wisdom prefers ; 
he chose to believe Satan, and he chose not to 
believe God: Adam had not the Holy Spirit, 
for the Holy Spirit is the consequence and fruit 
of redemption. 

Adam was possessor of the world, but he suf 
fered it to get into the possession of the devil : 
until the devil is cast out, and another comes 
into possession of it again, it can only be held 
by heirship: hence Isaac becomes a type of 
Christ, as the heir who is not yet the possessor, 
The promise was given, but its accomplishment 
long delayed, still more to make Isaac a perfect 
type. The promise of possession of the world 
was indeed given to Abram, before the destruc- 
tion of the cities of the plain, and of the five 
kings. The power of generation to Abram, 
and of conception to Sarah, were both long 
delayed ; and the communication of that power, 
and of the destruction of Sodom, was received 
at the same time; so. now, let the church. mock 
as she will, she will never be possessed of the 
same hope of the Child of Joy, until, at the same 
time, she feels certain conviction of the coming 
destruction ; even as it is said at the same in- 
stant, “ Behold I come,” and “ Babylon: is 
fallen.” Ishmael represents man in the fleshly . 
covenant, Isaac represents him in the spiritual 
covenant. The children of Keturah all received 


275 


gifts, as the brethren of the spiritual seed, be- 
fore they were sent away, and before Abraham 
gave all that he had to Isaac. | 
` Melchizedek is the type of Christ as Priest 
and King in one person; that is, the holiness 
and power of God. If God had not given a 
type of his Son, the Jews would have fallen, 
‘without a warning, into the error into which 
they did fall, notwithstanding the warning, of 
saying, “ Have we not Abraham to our Father ?” 
and, ‘‘ Art. thou greater than our father Abra- 
ham?” Abraham is the type of the church 
now; Melchizedek is the type of what she 
-will ‘be in the coming dispensation. Abraham, 
returning from the slaughter of the five kings, 
‘acknowledged a greater than himself; and so 
will the Jews, after the slaughter of Armageddon. 
There is one type that is especially worthy of 
your consideration, namely, Eliakim, Is. xxiii. 
20, who was over the king’s “household,” 
2 Kings xviii. 18, and to which I would refer 
the parable of the Householder ; for I believe I 
shall be borne out in saying, that every parable 
has its root in the prophecies, and ‘is the fructi- 
fication of seed sown therein. There will be 
ever an usurper over the house of David, until 
the true David returns to inhabit his house, 
“whose house are we.” Heb. 11.6. The key 
was an emblem of rule over the house, “ the 
government shall be upon his shoulder,” Isa. 
1x.6; and is worn as such by the Chamberlains 
of Royal Households to this day: the marriage 
garments were under the care of. the Chamber- 


276 


lain; and such are now fastened doubtless ‘on 
nails, in a sure place, ready for those who are 
to enter into the wedding supper of the Lamb. 
Thus the parable of a candle under a ‘bushel, is 
an apt representation of Babylon hiding her 
lamp, as a church, under her traditions, her un- 
known tongue, her ceremonies, and other abomi- 
nations; whilst in her construction and ability 
to abide the flood of God’s great day of vaii, 
she is a house built on the sand. 

Justus.— Whenever Moses spake to thes peo- 
ple, his face was veiled : so Christ is now veiled, 
and we see not his face; but when he comes 
again, it is the time of his unveiling, or Apoca- 
lypse: the people too, could only be blessed 
when the priest came out of the temple, in -his 
beautiful garments ; so we shall have the pos- 
session of the promised blessing, to wit, the re- 
demption of the body, when our High Priest 
comes out of the Heavenly Temple, in His 
glorious clothing, more splendid than the god 
of Ophir. 

Theodorus. —The opinion alluded i to in Phila- 
lethes, is very general; namely, that we have 
no ground to consider any thing in the Old Tes- 
tament as typical, unless we find it alluded to, 
for that purpose, in the New. This opinion is 
so much the reverse of what I believe to be 
sound upon the subject, that we should not pass 
it by hastily. There is no allusion whatever’ in 
the Old ‘Festament to any thing being typical ; 
and the first notice we have of their being so, is 
from the mouth. of our Lord himself, who 


277 


alludes to Jonah, the temple, the brazen ser- 
pent, the destruction of Sodom, the deluge, 
&e. &c. St. Paul subsequently taught, that 
many other things were typical also: the 
proper inference to be drawn from this is, that 
our Lord and his Apostles have furnished us 
with a key, by which to unlock the hidden trea- 
sures of the former shadows, which we are to 
use according to our own sagacity and discre- 
tion. If it is to be granted that we are not to 
make use of any thing as typical in the Old 
Testament, but that which is declared to be so 
in the New, we must at once renounce the 
clearest pictures which we have of some most 
essential points of Christian doctrine; such, 
for example, as expiation, substitution, atone- 
ment, &c. &c. Types are also very valuable as 
evidences of the unity of the designs, plans, 
and operations of God, and proofs of the in- 
Spiration of the Scriptures; but if types are 
to be given up, the Old Testament may be given 
up as an obsolete book, for every thing but its 
historical minutiz ; and, therefore, unless I re- 
tain the types, I shall in vain seek in Moses for 
Him of whom Moses did write, even Jesus .of 
Nazareth. 

Among the types of the divinity ai in 
the humanity of the God-man, the Shechinah 
im the temple seems one of the most obvious. 
The Apostle seems to place the first Adam not 
onły as a type of the second, but also by way 
of contrast to him, where he speaks of the “one 
offence,” and the whole righteousness. In like 


278 


manner the first Adam admitted. the tempter. 
into the world; the second Adam casts him. out 
of the world. Part of Isaac’s typical character 
consisted in his being, at the time of his sacri- 
fice, the alone heir of his father to the posses- 
sion of the world, and the only begotten son 
also ; wherefore he was, in a figure, to be.cut-off : 
Abraham tells Isaac that ‘“ God had prepared 
himself an offering ;’ and God, in like manner, 
prepared Christ for the antitypical offering : 
“a body hast thou prepared for me.” Sufficient 
stress does not seem to be laid. upon one. circum- 
stance in the typical histories of Joseph. and 
Moses, both of whom eminently. shadowed 
forth the Lord: both were, at first, rejected of 
their brethren ; but modern divines seem to. pass 
over the faet, that both became afterwards the 
great deliverer of the very people who. had. re- 
jected them: Christ has already been rejected 
of the Jews, but he must still appear to be 
their deliverer, as Stephen argues at length in 
Acts vil. and will still be their David. and. their 
Solomon. 

Theologus.—The thing to be shewn.by all Scrip- 
ture is the Lord Jesus Christ, as the risen God- 
man, and whatever can set this forth is a type. 
The use of the word type, ought, perhaps, to be 
limited. to the legal ordinances by which the 
church was taught of old, and it.is questionable 
whether it can be applied to persons; its more. 
correct definition would. be “a substance. which 
shews out something beyond, and distinct from, 
its own proper nature.” Nothing can. be under- 


279 


stood to be a type until the antitype has ap- 
peared, or has been clearly declared. The first | 
principle to be always borne in mind, as that 
which all things are to pourtray, is a God 
existing as a Trinity of Co-equal, Co-existent, 
and Co-essential persons, willing to have crea- 
tures. God then ordering all things with a view 
to this manifestation, it is obvious that all things 
will manifest this if we can see it; since every 
event, and every substance, is brought into 
being by His will, and through His energy. 
It. pleased the Father that in Christ should all 
fulness dwell ; who is the image of the invisible 
God, the first-born of every creature; for by 
him were all things created, that are in heaven, 
and that are in earth, visible and invisible, 
whether they be thrones, or dominions, or prin- 
cipalities, or powers ; all things were created by 
him and for him, Col. i. 14. 19. God the 
Father doing all things in Christ, which is the 
second person in the man Jesus Christ in the 
Holy Ghost. The predestinative subsistence of 
the God-man runs on till his ascension, when he 
was glorified with the glory which he had before 
the world was, John xvii. 5. The design. of 
this dispensation is the irrefragable demon- 
stration of the self-existent God, and the display 
of a person necessarily connected with two 
others in Deity; the Father, the necessary 
originator of two others. The great result of 
this dispensation being the bringing out of ‘the 
God-man, it would not be probable that the 
Father should have left him without previous 


280 


exhibition, and therefore there are types of him 
every where. Adam, in his creation from the 
dust, is a type of the beautiful body produced 
out of the ground at the resurrection. Although 
all our bodies were in Adam, yet I incline to 
think that the soul of each individual is a 
separate creation, or impartation to each indi- 
vidual on creation grounds ; but existing in 
Adam, we exist as persons damned. 3 

Burnt-offering aptly represents the resur- 
rection; the offerer and the victim were one, 
and being burned by fire, rose in an altered 
state ; thus the dead sinner, after the fire of the 
Holy Ghost has changed his substance, rises a 
spiritual being, the burnt-offering being Christ. 
The burnt-offering is the ascending-one; Christ 
was under the curse till his death, and we re- 
ceive the Holy Ghost in our quasi death at 
baptism. _

instituted solely for the purpose of being types, 
and all who cannot so read them, and find the An- 
titype, are culpably ignorant of what they ought 
toknow. Iwill point out to you two, because they 
are rarely noticed, with this view, by commenta- 
tors; the first is the ceremony of washing the 
hands, in token of putting away the guilt of a 
slain man from him who performs it, which was 
enjoined in Deut. xxi. 1—9 ; and which was done 
by Pilate, when the infatuated Jews voluntarily 
took the guilt of the crucifixion of the Lord of 
Life upon their own heads, Matt. xxvii. 24. 
The second is in Numb. xxx. 1—10; the two

_ -Isocrates.—That the general principle which 
our friends have now laid down respecting types, 
is sound, may be further proved by the expres- 
sion, ‘‘in the VOLUME OF THE Book it is written 
of me,” &c. &c., Psalm xl. 17, and particularly | 
because it would be impossible to point out a 
single passage where it was said that Christ 
delighted to do the will of God, but by reference 
-to types. Likewise in Acts XXV1., where . Paul 
-makes his defence before Agrippa, he says that 
the Prophets and Moses did say that Christ 
should. suffer, and should be the first, that should 
VOL. II. U 


282 


rise from the dead, which would be very difficult 
to shew, except by the shadows or types of the 
law, for these things are no where expressly said, 
but in types. Such passages as Luke xxiv. 44,45; 
and Heb. ix. 1—8, are quite sufficient scriptural 
authority for our searching into types, and draw- 
ing thence whatever instruction we can. 

Anastasius.—Now let me, in my turn, ask you, 
Philalethes, how far have your examinations by 
the written word of God confirmed or weakened 
the effect of what we have said in these our se- 
veral conversations ? 

Philalethes.—I think I very chaise appre- 
hend that the world in which we live, was 
overcome by the devil, whose empire has been 
established in it; but that the Lord Jesus 
engaged to deliver the world from his dominion, 
for which purpose he came in humiliation to 
make a vast atonement for sin, on the ground of 
which he should deliver out of the world, in the 
age of its apostacy, a whole company of elect 
people, whom he predestinated to share his 
glory; to sit with him upon his throne, and 
exercise dominion over the renewed earth, which, 
at his second coming, shall be purified from all 
evil, diabolical as well as human; and be 
restored to a state more glorious than that of 
paradise; and so remain for ever and for ever, — 
without death, or sorrow, or sin, the whole 
nuisance of it being drained away into that great 
sink, hell. 

If it be so that the Lord Jesus, who is the heir 
of all things, is to dwell amongst us, will not this 


283 


earth be heaven? and as He is the centre and 
glory of the universe, may not the amazing 
transactions of his incarnation and grace, as 
well as that of the everlasting damnation of men 
and devils, furnish to the whole universe an ever- 
lasting display of God’s greatness, and mercy, 
and majesty, and of the tremendousness of the 
evil of sin? If this is to be the dwelling-place 
of the Lord Jesus, it must be the glory of the 
whole universe, and the transactions which will 
have taken place upon it will be the very means 
of displaying universally his holiness and his 
justice, terrible against transgressors, as well 
as the pure character of his love; and so to the 
principalities and power in heavenly places will 
be known by our church, the manifold wisdom of 
God. This is what it appears to me the Apostle 
means, when he says, that God created all things 
by Jesus Christ, to the intent that by the church 
these things should be displayed. This earth 
must, by the Lord Jesus dwelling upon it, be- 
come the great sun, light, and glory of the moral 
universe. | 

Anastasius.—You have certainly got hold of 
the cream of the whole subject: and may the 
Lord, who has granted us in this world so much 
knowledge of his truth, lead us more and more 
to walk in him, that at his second coming we 
may be found an acceptable people in his sight, 
with our lamps full of oil, not having our own 
righteousness, but the righteousness which is of 
God by faith. 

The attention of the church having been 

U2 


284 


again drawn to the long-forgotten subject of the- 
Lord’s reign upon this renovated earth, has pro- 
duced already one very good effect; which is, 
that many who had thought they were fully 
acquainted with the Holy Scriptures, have found 
that there is still much to be learned in them, 
and have begun to search them with encreased 
avidity. Messrs. Marsh, Stewart, Hawtrey, G. 
Noel, M‘Neile, in the Church of England; Mr. 
Irving, in the Scottish Church; and Mr. 
O’Bryan, in the Wesleyan connexion, have all 
lately published valuable works upon the hopes 
and expectations of the Christian. May the 
Lord own and bless the labours of these his 
servants! may His Spirit accompany the readers 
of their works! and may all, into whose hands 
they shall‘come, have their faith rendered more 
strong, their zeal more animated, and their love 
for each other more fervent, to the glory of His 
name, and to their own everlasting happiness ! 
Amen.
