CONVERSATION THE THIRTEENTH. 


Practical Duties incumbent on those who are looking for the 
Lord’s Appearance. 


Philalethes.—Tue great importance of the 
subjects which we have discussed; the pe- 
culiar circumstances .of the present time; the 
deadness which the church has felt respecting 
her Lord’s coming for fifteen hundred years; 
and the sudden call which she has lately had to 
arouse herself; all seem to indicate, that there 
must be either some peculiar duties applicable 
to us who believe these things, or at least a pecu- 
liar call to increased zeal and activity. oe 

Anastasius.—Our duties connected with this 
subject are twofold: first, those relating to our 
own hearts ; and secondly, those relating to the 
testimony we ought to bear to the church and to 
the world. The description of the wise servant, 
considered in the connexion in which it appears, 
will furnish a fit lesson to us in both respects. 
At the conclusion of that long conversation which 
our blessed Lord had with his disciples upon the 
signs of his coming, and of the end of the world, 
and in which he warns those who shall be in the 
flesh at that period, lest that coming should 
overtake them as a snare, seeing that he comes 
as a thief; he says, ‘“‘ who then is a faithful and 
wise servant, whom his Lord hath made ruler 
over his household, to give them meat in due 


286 


season? blessed is that servant whom his Lord, 
when he cometh, shall find so doing; verily 
I say unto you, that he shall make him ruler 
over all his goods: but, and if that evil servant 
shall say in his heart, my Lord delayeth his 
coming, and shall begin to smite his fellow 
servant, and to eat and drink with the drunken ; 
the Lord of that servant shall come in a day 
when he looketh not for him, and in an hour 
that he is not aware of; and shall cut him 
asunder, and appoint him his portion with the 
hypocrites,” &c. Matt. xxiv. 45, &c. In Mark it 
is added, that the Lord “ gave to every man his 
work, and commanded the porter to watch—and 
what I say unto you, I say unto all, Watcu.” 
xiii. 34, &c. In a former conversation our 
blessed Lord teaches us, that “ every scribe 
which is instructed unto the kingdom of heaven, 
is like unto a man that is a householder, which 
bringeth forth out of his treasure things new and 
old,” Matt. xiii. 52. It is not, therefore, to be 
denied, that as the good steward furnishes forth 
for the use of the household the provisions 
proper to each season, so “ the ministers and 
stewards of God’s mysteries,” ought, at this time, 
to be especially offering for the spiritual sus- 
tenance of the household of faith, the solid and 
substantial glories of the coming kindom of our 
Lord; and that we ought to press the subject 
upon the sleepy church and scoffing world, “ in 
Season and out of season.” 

Theophilus.—The coming of the Lord, the king- 
dom of the Lord at his coming, and the honour 


287 


and happiness of the suffering church then, will 
alone rouse real Christians, because it is the ap- 
pointed means: at present we all, with here and 
there an exception, slumber and sleep. 
Polydorus.—1 cannot doubt but that the right 
apprehension of the speedy coming of the Lord 
is the main point of our Christian duty at the 
present period. It is remarkable, that the order 
of doctrine and precept which is observed in all 
the epistles, the latter being invariably founded 
upon the former, is inverted in the Apocalypse ; 
from whence I gather, that a very high state of 
moral and spiritual feeling is a necessary preli- 
minary for entering into the study of the Apoca- 
lypse. We must be much on our guard against 
feeling dissatisfied at having mistaken the details 
of future events, upon which we may have been 
led to form too minute expectations; and in 
order to obviate such disappointment, we should 
not form an opinion upon any one point, until 
we have examined it with reference to all other 
points, in order to ascertain how far it is consistent 
with, or contradictory to, them. In the passage 
in Matt. xxiv, to which Anastasius referred us, 
the servant who does not expect his Lord’s 
coming, is found smiting his fellow servant; we 
should be on our guard against acting in like 
manner, and if any smite us for holding these 
sentiments, we must take care not to smite 
again, but say with Hooker, “ I pass by your 
revilings, and address myself to your arguments.” 
It will be great means of avoiding giving just 
cause of offence, not to found any argument upon 


288 


types, but only to bring them forward to illus- 
trate a doctrine. We should make it a matter 
of serious concern to be strict and rigid in the 
examination of our own personal justification 
and interest in the Lord Jesus, so that the hope 
of his appearing shall bear us out in all circum- 
stances and changes, according to the instruction 
of the Apostle Peter, 2. iii, 14. .“ Wherefore 
beloved, seeing that ye look for such things, be 
diligent that ye be found of him in peace, with- 
out spot, and blameless.” Above all, we must 
not relax in our investigations of and searchings 
into the word of God, but meditate, and read, and 
pray over it; always bearing in mind that we are 
still but at the very threshold of the: oracles of 
truth. Let us be lowly and humble before our 
fellow-christians, not forgetting at the same 
time, that the express injunction before all the 
annunciations of the coming of the Lord is. not 
to be afraid; and in this way we shall cultivate 
the present love towards the brethren, which 
is the end of the commandment. We ought not 
to omit forming and frequenting prayer meet- 
ings, especially for the coming of the Lord, 
which is the object of all scriptural prayer; and, 
perhaps, no stronger test of our election of 
God can be afforded, than the crying out day 
and night for the coming of His kingdom. 
Those Societies which bear upon the Jews 
and upon the Papacy, such as the London, Phi- 
lo-Judean, Continental, and Reformation So- 
cieties demand our especial support. The ex- 
ample of Christ ought to be ever our pole-star, 


289 


and ministers in their public preaching should 
not omit to point to the cross, as this is the ob- 
ject to which the whole world ought to be invited. 
But not the remotest idea can be formed of the 
government of God over the nations; of his in- 
terference with the power of the world; of the 
different dispensations which have taken place, 
and are yet to take place ; of the true essence of 
the Sacraments, but with reference to the com- 
ing of the Lord in glory, which has been so 
miserably lost sight of in the church. 
Theologus.—Our great practical duty is to aim 
to expect the coming of the Son of Man rightly, 
as to, 1. the Who; 11. the Why; and 111. the How. 
1. We must be clear, definite, and precise as 
to the nature and circumstances of his coming ; 
the outline of which is a personal coming, and 
not a change only, however extensive and pe- 
netrating, in the face and moral temperament 
of the world: we do expect such a change, but 
it is as the concomitant, not as the essential 
reality of the promised event. The coming will 
be first, and the change subsequent. Having 
come visibly, he will remain visible; with this- 
difference, that, at his coming, he will be visible 
to eyes of flesh; subsequently, only spiritual 
eyes will be capable of beholding him: he shall 
come to rule; of which the first exercise is to 
put down the opponents of the assumed au- 
thority: this he shall do by judging his church, 
by inflicting judgments upon all the individuals 
of those communities who profess his name and 
service falsely: having previously separated his 


290 


faithful servants out of the midst of them, to be 
his assessors in the work of judgment, and his 
partners in bliss and glory; and having thus 
purged his temple, that it be no longer a den of 
thieves, he shall proceed to establish a pure 
worship in the centre of the earth ; in the centre 
of his restored nation, in the centre of the city of 
his Holy Hill of Zion, the rebuilt Jerusalem. But 
he will not come alone; the saints will be called 
out of the grave to meet him, and those in the 
flesh will be changed into his own bodily likeness. 
When all, to which his presence in a visible 
body is necessary, shall have been effected, he 
shall take his abode in the Jerusalem above; in 
that spiritual city of which spiritual beings in 
spiritual bodies are the living stones, and himself 
the chief foundation corner stone; where visible 
to them, but invisible to all who are_not spirits, 
he shall in the midst of them, together with, and 
through them, exercise lordship as God’s heir, 
that heir which is an eternal expectant, never a 
possessor over all the earth, over all the universe. 
Christ is now sitting by the side of God on God’s 
throne, as God’s co-ordinate: then he will come 
forth as heir, and then will be subordinate: he 
is the king who is the end of rule, because God 
is now doing every thing for the recognition of 
Christ ; he is Lord of all; when he is come, being 
acknowledged Lord and God, he comes out as heir 
subordinate to God, 1 Cor. xv. There is a co- 
equality of nature, but subordination of persons. 
Whilst he rules for God, he shall worship God ; 
for the peculiarity of his made lordship (Heb. i. 


291 


4.11; vii. 9, &c.) is, that he is a Priestly King ; 
and as the royalty is not alone, so neither is the 
_ priesthood. Head of the worshippers, as well 
as head of the Vicegerents or joint heirs, he shall 
both jointly worship the God and Father, and 
with and through them, procure, maintain, and 
perpetuate the worship of the natural, national 
seed, which is abiding in flesh below. The 
priestly dominion carried on by, through, and in 
him as its head, in the Aerial City, connected with, 
and holding continual communion with the ma- 
terial Jerusalem, but essentially distinct and per- 
- fectly separate from it, shall continue for a large 
but limited period ; which being completed, and 
Satan loosed, and the nations, (but not Israel 
after the flesh, and still less the spiritual saints, ) 
having been again deceived, and another Apos- 
tacy, which involves the whole earth of living 
men, having been effected, judgment begins 
afresh ; fire breaks forth from heaven to consume 
the rebels, and the whole multitude of the un- 
saved is made to stand i-a body of resurrection, 
that is, in a body of spirit, before the throne of 
Christ and of God; that is, of God wearing the 
form, dwelling in the inwards, and exhibited in 
the person of the Lord Jesus Christ. Speechless, 
without excuse, self-condemned, and condemned 
of God, the millions who have not Christ dwelling 
in them, and walking in them, that is, energizing 
in them, are cast into the lake of fire. Thus, 
after the interval of a literal or figurative 1,000 
years from Christ’s second coming, final judg- 
ment is executed, which puts a last end to the 


292 


present form of this earth, though not to its ma-. 
terial, and gives commencement to a new era, and 
to a new scene, of which we know no particulars, 
but know assuredly that there shall be such, in 
which the saints erect for ever round the throne 
of God and of the Lamb, and the wicked pros- 
trate for ever as their footstool, in the impene- 
trable, impassable, and indissoluble enclosure of 
hell fire, a new heaven, and a new earth, 
together with a host of creatures adapted to them 
severally, shall issue forth; new heavens and 
new earth, wherein dwelleth righteousness, and 
where the sea is not any longer. 

Philalethes.—Christ is now on .God’s throne, 
and in what do you suppose the difference to 
consist between the throne on which he now sits, 
and that on which he sits during the millennium ? 

Theologus.—Christ is now reigning as God on 
his throne, at the side of God (mapa so), as his 
co-ordinate ; and subordination is not a less es- 
sential attribute of the Trinity than equality of 
substance. 

Philalethes.—Is not this question of sub-order 
rather delicate? _ 

Anastasius. —All matters of deep chesloey are 
delicate, partly from our incapacity to compre- 
hend them aright; party from our inability to 
convey by articulate sounds such conceptions as 
we have; and partly from the indolence of men, 
who find it more easy to exclaim “ Great is 
Diana,” than to give themselves the trouble of 
understanding that which is said. We may pro- 
perly apply to such would-be Theologians, the 


293 


words which Hooker applies to another class ; 
“the matter wherein ye think that ye see and 
imagine that your ways are sincere, is of far 
deeper consideration than any one amongst five 
hundred of you conceiveth. Let the vulgar sort 
among you know, that there is not the least 
branch of the cause, wherein they are so reso- 
lute, but to the trial of it, a great deal more 
appertaineth than their conceit doth reach unto. 
I write not this in disgrace of the simplest that 
way given, but I would gladly they knew the 
nature of that cause, wherein they think them- 
selves thoroughly instructed, and are not; by 
means whereof they daily run themselves, with- 
out feeling their own hazard, upon the dint of 
the Apostle’s sentence against evil-speakers, as 
touching things wherein they are ignorant.” 
Vol. i. p. 144. The idea of subordination of 
office, notwithstanding the co-equality of the 
persons in the Godhead, is well expressed by 
him in another place; and, I confess, I think 
that more good has been lost than gained by all 
attempts which have been hitherto made to 
define the mode of the subsistence of the Deity. 
“ Dangerous it were for the feeble brain of man, 
to wade far into the doings of the Most High; 
whom, although to know be life, and joy tomake 
mention of his name; yet our soundest know- 
ledge is, to know that we know him not as 

indeed he is, neither can know him; and our 
_ safest eloquence concerning him is our silence, 
when we confess without confession that his 
glory is inexplicable, his greatness above our 


294 


capacity and reach. He is above, and we upon 
earth; therefore it behoveth our words to be 
wary and few. Our God is one, or rather very 
oneness, and mere unity, having nothing but 
Itself in Itself, and not consisting (as all things 
do besides God) of many things. In which 
essential Unity of God, a Trinity personal 
nevertheless subsisteth, after a manner far 
exceding the possibility of man’s conceit. The 
works which outwardly are of God, they are in 
such sort of him being one that each person hath 
in them somewhat peculiar and proper. For being 
three, and they all subsisting in the essence of 
one Deity, from the Father, by the Son, through 
the Spirit, all things are. That which the Son 
doth hear of the Father, and which the Spirit 
doth receive of the Father and the Son, the 
same we have at the hands of the Spirit, as 
being the last ; and therefore the nearest unto us in 
order, although in power the same with the second 
and the first.” Vol. i. p. 200. Charnock ex- 
presses the same idea thus :—‘‘ He (Christ) is 
appointed heir, as he was appointed mediator, 
Heb. iii. 2. He was mediator by a voluntary 
designation, he was heir by a voluntary dona- 
tion, and all judgment was committed to him by 
a voluntary deputation ; but he was a Son by a 
natural generation; again an heir succeeds in 
the place of another; so Christ, as medi- 
ator, succeeds in the place of his Father, 
in regard of government, as his delegate and 
deputy; but what the Son hath from the 
Father as God, he hath not, as his deputy, 


295 


but by an essential, natural, and eternal com- 
munication. So that these two differ. The 
one belongs to his essence as God, the 
other to his office of mediator.——In the 
first he is one with the Father, in the other he is 
the Father’s substitute, and deputy; his Father's 
Lord Lieutenant in the world according to a 
derived authority,” &c. &c. Vol. v. p. 396. ~ If 
you want any more authorities, I beg you to 
consult Pearson on the Creed. 

Theologus.—The essence of the statement 
which I have made, lies in the coming of the 
Lord being not figurative but literal; a per- 
sonal, not an energetical manifestation of the 
great God and our Saviour in his day. Accord- 
ing to some, he might as well have been said to 
have come and to have appeared in the flood, at 
Babel, Sodom, to Pharaoh, Babylon, Constan- 
tinople, Jerusalem, Rome, or in any battles of 
our own times, as in the exterminative and 
beatific process of setting up his no longer 
resisted, his universally acknowledged kingdom. 
But we believe he shall come as he has never 
yet come, in a form of glory; the form of 
adorned manhood, so as to be seen and owned of 
men. Yet such seeing shall be transient to 
men in the flesh, and abiding only to men in the 
Spirit. This, his coming, shall be at the com- 
mencement, not at the close, of his earthly 
reign; yet he shall come while nations are 
dwelling in the earth in their ancient form, and 
shall continue to maintain their existence in 
that form during a limited period. The raising 


296 


of the blessed dead, and the changing of the 
bodies of the blessed living, shall be simul- 
taneous with the annunciation of his approach, 
and together with the vengeance then executed 
on the unbelieving members of his visible 
church, shall constitute a distinct and integral 
part of his work of judging the world ; yet shall 
there be unraised and unjudged ones still; yet 
shall the earth have a judgment still to undergo ; 
yet shall there be temples, and services, and an 
epoch to be waited for. The restoration of the 
Jews, as a whole, shall be subsequent to, and 
shall be effected in rapid succession to, the 
blessing and cursing of the instant of his 
coming; yet shall that nation have received 
a partial restoration before, and individuals of 
“ the glorified fulness” shall have been taken out 
of it; yet shall the joy of Canaan, and of the 
Jew, be distinct from that of the spiritual Israel. 
Christ’s spiritual empire shall be co-extensive 
with the earth and with the world ; but its seat 
shall not be earth, though communicating with 
it; his saints shall reign as well as serve with 
him, but they shall not justle with men in the 
flesh, and though intermixing when need be, 
individually, shall be visible only by special 
operation and appointment. A second apos- 
tacy, a second conflict, a second resurrection, 
and second judgment shall follow close on the 
sunset of the millennial day; yet even these 
events shall not finish the transactions of earth, 
which shall survive her conflagration, as she has 
survived her deluge, though the last which God 


297 


has seen good to reveal in his Bible, with a very 
brief intimation that there are others yet to 
come. 

- Having then seen Who it is that is coming, we 
have now to consider the Why. For this we have 
the distinct authority of Scripture, both parti- 
cular and general. From Acts xi. 30, we know 
that God hath sworn that he will raise up Christ 
to sit on his David’s throne; and from 1 Tim. vi. 
that he (the Father) will shew in his own (doc), 
times the “appearing of our Lord Jesus Christ.” - 
We also know the reason of his coming in glory, 
from the order, dignity, necessity, and efficacy 
of that event in the great scheme of God. As 
to the order, it is next after the session, and 
before the general judgment. With respect to 
its dignity, it is the chief step in the act of God, 
for it is the exhibition to the world of the mani- 
fester, upholder, and doer of all his works from 
beginning to end. Of its necessity it is suffi- 
cient to see that it is to be, to be sure that it is” 
necessary, and when it is said it became God, so 
certainly is that necessary which it is becoming 
of God to do; it is the reign of Christ as the 
Melchizedek with his saints upon the earth ; and 
if that state be necessary, the coming is neces- 
sary, since it must have a beginning; for like 
reasons every antecedent step is necessary ; 
his session is necessary; and it is the same thing 
whether he does this in his Father’s name, or 
whether the Father does it by him, whilst he is 
sitting beside the Father. The ascension was 
necessary, because, being invisible, he could not 

VOL. II. X 


298 


stay in the visible region; so also his resur- 
rection, death, incarnation, giving of the law, 
&e.; and here I may remark, that the cere- 
monial law is a foreshadowing of what is to take 
place during the millennial reign, and not of any 
thing in the present state of the church. In 
Rev. iv. 5, we have a representation of the 
perfected globe of the church in the millennial 
state. The saints of God are now in three 
parts; first, asleep; second, militant; and 
third, in glory. Sacrifice, during the millennial 
state, will be commemorative of the death and 
consequent exaltation. The kingdom of Israel 
was necessary, as well as all that preceded it; 
the patriarchal dispensation, the flood, the fall, 
the creation, all just such as they severally were, 
with all the superability, fallibility, destruc- 
tibility, beneficence belonging to this last ; and 
thus we rise to the predestinative council of 
Jehovah, and his will to have creatures; all 
these steps, of which the second coming of 
Christ in glory is one, were necessary to the 
end which God intended to achieve, even the 
universal resurrection, judgment, new heaven, 
and earth, &c. With respect to its efficacy, it, 
like all the links in the chain of God, implies 
this and every other mystery of God ; the mys- 
tery consisting in God joining himself to the 
creature, in order that he may communicate his 
being to it, which the name Jesus, Joshua, the 
saved one, or saved man, means. Swrno is from 
cecwcat, that is one who has been saved; in like 
manner, to justify is to save through ruin. Itis 


299 


equally easy to begin at one end of the chain of 
God's work as at the other, and only let Jesus, 
coming as a thief, be a point granted, and the 
predestinate counsel of God, and all its revealed 
results, may be clearly demonstrated. 

It remains only to consider How we are to 
expect him. This must be in God; we being in 
God must expect, as those who have realized 
God to themselves, according to that exhibition 
which he shall have made of himself through this 
means, which is the consummation of his whole 
counsel. The coming is all one day, the judg- 
ment on the church and the appearing being 
in the morning, and the judgment on the wicked 
in the evening of it. This is the mani- 
festation of Christ as the life, the self-existent 
one, the steps to which ultimate disclosure 
involve every conceivable perfection, and declare 
an infinite of which we have no conception. In 
order that there should be adaptedness to wor- 
ship, there must be resemblance between the 
object of worship and the worshipper; and the 
‘man who has the Holy Ghost is the image of 
God, even in a body of flesh, for the Holy Ghost 
does form that resemblance in us. As the second 
person in the Godhead in the flesh is the primary 
idea of Jehovah, so the central principle on which 
the Almighty counsel turns, is grace manifested 
in mercy, and mercy in grace; grace produces 
his conferring the highest favour; mercy pro- 
duces its being shewn to those who deserve to 
be damned. To expect in God, is to have a con- 
tinual regard to God ; many fail in not taking in

300 


the scheme of God ; what we read of Christ and 
God we are to realize in a person, according to 
our relation to which he has exalted us in Christ. 
We must learn move habitually to refer all to 
God; for the great point in this stupendous 
event is to bring us through the Mediator to the 
knowledge of God, and unless these matters 
bring us to God, they fall very short of their 
object. We owe a debt to the Lord Jesus who 
has redeemed us, to the church of which we are 
members, to the brethren with whom we sympa- 
thize, and to the world as sons of Adam, to set 
the whole of this truth before them. 

Philalethes.—Do not think that I mean to 
cavil, but I wish you would give me some further 
light upon an expression you have used, which 
is not clear, I mean that Christ is “always an 
expectant, never a possessor.” 

Theologus.—I mean that the propriety of all 
things will ever be in the God and Father, and 
that the possession which Christ has, and ever 
shall have, is a conceded one which always re-. 
cognizes the giver, and that a giver who is 
always somehow the holder ; retaining, whilst he 
parts with the possession ; that is, retaining it in 
another form, such as to shew that the very gift 
has himself for its object. Thus it is, in fact, a 
vice-royalty which Christ possesses, and he 
may be more truly said, in one sense, to reign 
now rather than hereafter, because he is now the 
avowed end of rule, whereas hereafter God ne 
be that avowed end. 

Anastasius,—In all these points the dismeton 


301 


are subtle and nice; and no expression taken 
from present things can fully convey the ideas ; 
above all, Philalethes, you must be upon your 
guard not to carry the figures cited for illus- 
tration one single hair’s breadth beyond the point 
for which they are used, because they will then 
cease to represent the intended idea. | 

Theologus.—Nothing can more exactly meet 
my idea that the relation of an heir to a living 
proprietor and possessor, especially if you restrict 
this form to that of an heir, who by the proprie- 
tor’s appointment exercises all the powers and 
privileges of a possessor; yet the property, it is 
obvious, still is in, and owned to be in, the 
person who makes the appointment, and for 
whose pleasure the heir manages and uses it. 
Only adhere to the idea of an heir, properly so 
called, and modify it by the peculiar constitution 
of the proprietor during his life time and pos- 
session calling him into the disposal and govern- 
ment of it, and you obtain the very view I take, 
and seek to give. The mistake has been in 
‘having run away from the propriety of the 
figure, and from having construed it with a lati- 
tude as denoting proprietor and possessor, with- 
out recognition of its relative to another, and 
that other a superior. I say this is the root of 
the mistake; for do but adhere to Scripture 
terms, and maintain the characteristics of the 
relation ascribed by those terms, and you cannot 
evade the conclusion I have expressed, or wish 
any other representation of the reality which is 
to be apprehended. 


302 


You will find, 1. That “heir” is the term 
expressly used in those passages which are most 
emphatic and distinctive ; “ whom he hath made 
heir of all things,” Heb. i. 2; ‘this is the heir,” 
Matt. xxi. 38; “heirs of God, and joint heirs 
with Christ,” Rom. viii. 17; and that the com- 
monest description of the hereafter state is that 
of an inheritance, which is properly the state of 
an heir, not the substance which is to fall to him. 
2. That this distinction alone meets the actual 
event; for in 1 Cor. xv. 24—28, it is expressly 
said, that “ he shall deliver up the kingdom to 
the God and Father, that God may be all in all,” 
meaning that he shall cease to be the immediate 
end of rule, when the purpose for which he has 
been made such is accomplished ; that purpose 
being the subjugation of all things to himself, . 
as God’s Christ, that the being and relations of | 
that particular person to the First Person, the ` 
- God and Father, may be exhibited and recog- 
nized; which being done, all falls of course to. 
him, and the second takes his proper place of 
heir, which is that of an expectant, or, at the 
highest, a Pro, or Viee-holder, that is, occupier, 
of possession and authority. I have no doubt, 
that if the Scriptures be carefully examined 
throughout, they will be found reducible to this, 
as the essence and centre of the truth which 
they unfold. The types also will be found all 
to run this way. Abraham is expressly called 
“the heir of the world;” and it is distinctly 
said, God gave him no inheritance in it, no, not 
so much as to set his foot on, yet he promised 


303 


that he would give it to him; and, in fact, 

Abraham ceases to be the type, when Isaac 
is born, who is clearly the heir; and Isaac 
ceases to be the type when Jacob is born, who 
again is clearly the heir; and Jacob, when the 
twelve heads of the tribes are born, who, toge- 
ther, constitute the Son; “Israel is my Son, 
eyen my first-born,” (which is the heir,) and “I 
say unto thee, let my son go that he may serve 
me,” Ex. iv. 22,23. It is remarkable that when 
St. Paul would prove Christ a priest, he quotes 
the 2d Psalm, “thou art my Son, to day have I 
begotten thee.” It seems therefore, that “Son” 
and “heir” are the dominant relations of the 
Christ in his glory; he that is strictly corre- 
spondent as the Son is to his Father, being alone 
worthy and able to render the service which is 
due and acceptable to God, (whence comes, “ Be 
ye holy, tor I am holy; the same term applied to 
the worshipper as to the worshipped,) and the 
dominion exercised being that of an expectant 
and natural delegate, not that of one who owns 
no superior, and author of rule. Schleusner’s 
account of zpwroroxoc is, that it means “ heir ;” 
but this is by consequence of being the first-bom. 
3. Considering the whole scheme in Christ to be 
but as machinery for exhibiting God, that men 
may, in some future state of the creature, wor- 
ship him according to his real nature and form, 
i count that this machinery would not be true to 
its object, if it did not set out the essential subor- 
dination.of the persons in Jehovah ; subordination, 
not inequality. Now what would be the effect,

if in the Christ system, the second person came 
out as supreme head, without derivation, and 
higher end of rule? where would be * the head 
of Christ is God,” even as the head of every man 
is Christ, and ‘the head of the woman is the 
man?” It is essential, therefore, to the very 
design of God, that the issue should be such as 
we have seen that the Scriptures declare at 
large, and the term implies, “ heir:” with all the 
peculiarities of a human heir, save this, which 
is impossible im this case, viz. that he ever 
should be the possessor. I throw out these ideas, 
which you may modify and improve upon, but I 
feel confident as to the soundness of the prin- 
ciple. You may derive further light from consi- 
dering Israel ; what is he now? is he any thing 
but the heir? has he ever been any thing but the 
heir? can you call his temporary possession, 
during 1500 years, any thing but a putting in 
of his claim to the heirship, which he will at last 
exhibit in holding the land in Christ as God’s 
representative! not in his own name; but Christ's, 
and this Christ, God’s glory or illustrator. 
Philalethes.—1 am much obliged to you for the

self, which makes your meaning quite clear ; 
but I am not sure that I agree with the inter-

enjoy. But as this subject is rather foreign to

Valerius. — Although ministers must study 
errors as well as truth, in order to be able to be 
of use to every class of minds; yet laymen may 
confine themselves with more profit to the study 
of truth alone. Heresies are a fruit of the flesh ; 
our Lord distinguishes between friends and 
servants, from whence I infer, that the way to 
obtain close communion and accurate acquaint- 
ance with him is to cultivate a very scrupulous 
and delicate holiness. It is said in Amos iil. 
7—15, “ surely the Lord God will do nothing, 
but he revealeth his secret unto his servants the 
prophets. The lion hath roared, who will not 
hear? The Lord God hath spoken, who can 
but prophecy? publish ye in the palaces of 
Ashdod, &c. Hear ye, and testify in the house 
of Jacob, saith the Lord God, the God of Hosts, 
that in the day that I shall visit the transgres- 
sions of Israel upon him, I will also visit the 
‘altars of Bethel, &c.—and I will smite the 
winter house and the summer house, and the 
houses of ivory shall perish, and the great houses 
shall have an end, saith the Lord.” It is clearly 
the duty for us who believe these things, to tes- 
tify of them every where, and to testify the more 
assiduously that the church is ill-inclined to 
receive our testimony. For ourselves, however, 
let us remember that the understanding of pro- 
phecy is specially a gi/t of the Spirit rather than 


306 


a grace; and that we shall find great advan-

Theodorus.—The great importance of living a 
life of faith, is strongly exemplified in these 
subjects, in as much as we are to expect events, for 
which there is no analogy. The expectation of 
the mighty power of the Holy Spirit, is not suffi- 
ciently realized in the church, and therefore few 
lead lives of entire consecration to the service of 
God. E 
Aristo.—The best evidence which I can offer, 
in opposition to all who gainsay the practical 
result of meditation on the coming glories of the 
Lord, is that I never feel myself so much over- 
awed by a sense of the character of God, and 
of my own wickedness, and of the unworthiness 
of the church, as when I contemplate this great 
act in the counsels of God, which stands so near : 
and in confirmation of what Theologus has said, 
that every link in the chain supposes every other 
link, the whole mystery of God in Christ was 
more clearly developed to me from the study of 
the Apocalypse, than from any other part of the 
sacred record. I never before could understand 
the reality of those things, which are contained 
in the barren orthodoxy of the church; and in 
my Own ministry, more men have been converted 
by the doctrine of the second Advent, than by 
any- other on which I have discoursed, except the 
Trinity. If I consider the prospect of the pre- 
sent period, and if I have any discernment of 
times and seasons, I am convinced that this doc- 


307 


trine is most necessary to be brought before the 
church, which is eaten up by infidelity in all its 
parts, particularly by an easy spirit towards the 
Apostacy, and by a blindness to the distinction 
between that and the true church. It requires 
to be much meditated on, beholding it with a 
view to the glory of God, and perceiving how it 
sets forth the purpose of the Father, the work of 
the Son, and of the Holy Ghost. It is not suf- 
ficiently recognized that the Holy Ghost pro- 
ceeds from the risen God-man, and that thus 
his official subordination is to very man. The 
church is out of joint, looking to spiritualities 
and not to the Spirit. It is the act of rege- 
neration, and not the Holy Ghost, which is before 
the church; preaching spiritualities, and not 
the preaching of the Spirit energizing in the 
heart, having taken the place of Satan in the 
soul. The influence of the Holy Spirit is 
preached as single, and not as having subordi- 
nated himself to.the risen God-man; the Holy 
Ghost subordinated to man is the life and com- 
fort of the doctrine. There are only two pos- 
sible modes of substituting false doctrine upon 
this point; the first is, that the Spirit comes “of 
himself,” John xv. 16; which is essentially 
casting out Christ and his written word, as some 
modern Calvinists and Quakers do, founding 
their erroneous view on a perversion of John 
iii. 8; the second is, taking the word and nothing 
beyond it, maintaining that all is to be done by 
your own mind being brought into contact with 
the truth, and not from faith to faith. The 


308 


word of God.does not operate upon the mind in 
the same way as other books, by a common 
process of cause and effect; and the want of 
seeing this has induced a great deal of the cum- 
bering with much serving about the Bible; as 
if the mere distribution of so many sheets of 
printed paper was, of itself, to produce the con- 
version of mankind. Then, in order to distin- 
guish the believer from the unbeliever, there 
must be tokens, evidences, &c. which are the 
fruits of the Holy Ghost, and are presented in 
definition and description. The doctrine of the 
risen God-man having received the gift of the 
Spirit for the use of the church, is that, for. the 
want of which, in a great degree, this.error in the 
church has arisen; this doctrine, therefore, of 
the risen God-man must be set forth in preach- 
ing before the doctrine of the Spirit. The fruits | 
of the Spirit are presented by our preachers in 
a set of definitions, instead of the real fruit being 
shewn to be the holding of communion with the 
Father in the risen God-man, and thus forms of 
the Holy Spirit are set forth =e niet and 
not love in the heart. 

In meditating upon it, with a view to the 
church in general, that is, the whole visible 
church of Christ, we must look at it not only in 
the light of the visible church, but also in that 
of the apostacy, for it is God’s plan to set out 
every thing by its contrary; and therefore we 
must examine it under that aspect of apostacy, 
both papal, and infidel antichristian. I do not 
separate the invisible from the visible. church, 


309 


for it never was the purpose of Christ to separate 
one from the other; but he speaks of all baptized 
persons as the church. As the former language, 
derived from the Old Testament, would only have 
set forth the mystery of God in it, so it was ne- 
cessary to have another language to set forth 
the mystery of Satan in it; this last is set forth 
as a visible apostacy, for he is the Lord of this 
visible age, and therefore makes his works 
visible ; the Scripture sets it forth as a thing to 
be revealed and manifested in its time before the 
millennium, which is to be the manifestation of 
Christ; the mystery of Satan, therefore, is not 
so much within us, as that which is visible in 
the church, and which he did bring the whole 
church to, till God brought out this kingdom ; 
-and when the kingdom takes up the church and 

establishes it, the kingdom takes it up, and not 
the church the kingdom. The church, which is 
not the apostacy, is to be spoken of as the 
Apostles spake to it in old times, as drawn out 
of the Apostacy. Our national safety consists 
in our being Protestant; the moment we forget 
the essence of that name, we symbolize with the 
Apostacy ; our standing is in resistance to the 
Apostacy ;. and so complete is our separation — 
from it, that no servant of the crown dare hold 
the smallest correspondence with the Pope. 
But how long this impediment may last in these 
days it is impossible to tell, 

Theodorus.—There is truly an awful respon- 
sibility upon us, whom the Lord has led to 
believe that portion of His long-forgotten word, 
if we do not act in a manner consistent with this 


310 


light. It is a wilful calumny to assert that the 
students of prophecy do not preach Christ ; 
the preaching of Christ is not the preaching of 
a half Christ, of Christ in some of his offices, 
to the exclusion of others ; but the preaching of 
a whole Christ, in all his offices, to the exclusion 
ofnone. This is the dispensation of the unseal- 
ing of mysteries, and we ought to be very earnest 
in our prayers for this, and also to be saved from 
error of every kind. Having sought for the truth 
of God, we ought not to conceal it, for it is not 
given to us for our private edification, but that 
we may edify the whole mystical body of Christ. 
This truth will make most winged speed, by 
connecting all other branches of truth with it; 
and it is important, therefore, to observe the 
various duties which are mentioned in Scripture 
in connexion with it. In 1 Thess. v. 1—11, it is 
connected with watchfulness, sobriety, faith, 
love, and hope; in the Evangelists, with warning 
our relations, after the example of Lot; in 
Tit. ii. 12, with denying ungodliness and worldly 
gratifications ; in 1 Tim. vi. 17, with warning 
the rich to be ready to distribute ; in 2 Tim. iv. 
1—5, with preaching in season and out of 
season, reproving, rebuking, exhorting those 
with itching ears who will not endure sound doc- 
trine. We must cultivate not only a peaceful 
but a patient spirit, lest the glorious appearance 
should be delayed longer than we expect; and 
because more irritation is excited upon this 
subject than upon any other, which it has ever 
fallen within my knowledge to hear discussed by

- Philalethes.—How can you account for this? 

Anastasius.—Without attempting to account 
for it upon general principles, I will only men- 
tion what has occurred to myself; in endea- 
vouring, by private conversation, to draw 
attention to the subject, I have had occasion 
to refer to the Old Testament, and my asto- 
nishment has been great at finding the most 
profound ignorance prevailing of many parts of 
the sacred volume, even of the histories, but 
especially of the prophets; this ignorance was 
not confined to laymen, but equally prevalent 
amongst ministers of high reputation for preach- 
ing and orthodoxy ; accompanied by a sneering 
hint that they had no time to consider such trifles, 
and that they were employed in much more im- 
portant matters than making themselves ac- 
quainted with those parts of God’s word. I 
must, however, admit, that this ignorance of the 
Scriptures was found more frequently amongst 
Dissenters, than amongst the members of the 
Established Church. In fact, the sum of -their 
theology seems to consist in the five points, and 
a few texts of Scripture, torn from their con- 
text, by which these are usually supported in 
controversial writings.

Anastasius.—I trust you will not imagine that 
I take pleasure in analyzing the operations, of 
Christians, in order to detect motives of con- 
duct unknown to and unacknowledged by them- 
selves. Lord Bacon once gave, as a definition 
of ingratitude, “a keen insight into the. motives 
of benevolence.” It does not require, however, 
a very keen insight into the motives. of prosely- 
tizing, to perceive that many other ingredients 
enter into its composition, besides a love for the 
truth of the Lord Jesus. While we do. not 
judge individuals, it is absurd to shut our eyes 
to the effects of systems, and the actions. of 
Committees; he who finds the cap fit. his own 
head, and chooses to put it on, may wear. it; 
but he must not blame us if be also find it gall 
his brow. Are we to be scared by a charge of 
wanting brotherly love, coming from men who 
testify so little themselves, that scarcely an evan- 
gelical clergyman in the Church of England 
would hold out the right hand of fellowship to 
the most useful minister of that communion, -and 
greatest light in one department: of religious 
truth, which has appeared in our time, I mean 
the late Dr. Hawker; and that not above one 
church or chapel in London was open for him to 
| preach in? Do they not.treat in a similar 
manner, the deepest theologian of the present 
day? ? Is it not notorious that some great leaders 
in ‘the religious world give or. withhold. their 
pompous patronage according as this. or. that 
clergyman is to preach its anniversary sermon?

nN 


313 


I say not this to yindicate any want of brotherly 
love in myself, but only to request that he who 
is without sin in this matter, will take up the 
first stone. Again I say, I judge not indivi- 
duals but systems. We must not be deterred from 
speaking truth by the fear of being charged with 
censoriousness, pride, and want of genuine love. 
The Christian who, in the silent watches of mid- 
night, is accustomed to trace the current of 
thought as it flows through his mind undisturbed; 
who, in the moments of pain, of sickness, or of 
alarm, realizes the hour in which he must pass 
the tremendous boundary of doubt and time into 
the unfathomable region of certainty and eter- 
nity ; who is wont every night, when he lays his 
head on his pillow, to anticipate that closing 
scene when he must lay it there for the last time ; 
when the solicitude of affection, the skill of the 
physician, and the fascination of this world will 
all be as unimpressive as the waves of the sea 
on the rocks which bound them; who, when the 
whole spirit is thus wrapt within itself, feels, as 
if there were but two beings in existence, his own 
soul and God; such an one (and who is a Chris- 
tian that is not such a one?) can do no otherwise 
than take to himself the very lowest place at the 
foot of the cross, know himself to be the chief 
of sinners, and say of every other individual, 
“ to his own master he stands or falls, I judge 
him not;” but while so knowing and feeling, 
let him not be deterred from speaking of public 
conduct as it is exhibited.

the faults of religious Societies excites contzo-

Anastasius.—Why desirable to ayoid it? What 
is the whole life of a Christian but the unin- 
terrupted controversy between the good and evil 
principles within him? What was the cause of 
the Protestant Reformation? what of Christi- 
anity itself, but controversy? Was not the life 
of our Lord a life of controversy with the Jews ? 
What are the lives of the Apostles? the Epistles 
to the Romans, Galatians, Hebrews, Corin- 
thians, but acts and works of controversy? and 
though last and least, what Society ever pro- 
duced more controversy than the Bible Society 
amongst Bisheps, Cabinet Ministers, Metho- 
dists, Popes, and Turks? There is something 
very suspicious in this pretended dislike of con- 
troversy ; and I entirely agree with the following 
passage from the North American Review of 
Milton’s Prose Works. “ Controversy i is not a 
favourite kind of reading; it requires more 
thought and a more severe and constant exercise 
_ of judgment, candour, patience, and equanimity, 
than most people are willing or able to bestow, 
and apply ; ; it is rarely conducted by the dispu- 
tants without more or less asperity ; it has ac-. 
quired a. bad name; it is called, by distinction, 
a thorny path; and many think they cannot. 
walk in it without danger to their faces and 
their clothes, and, therefore, they. decline. 
it with terror and aversion. Now, we pro- 
fess ourselves to be great friends of contro- 
versy. We regard it with respect and favour, if 


315 


not absolutely with love, If it is not a pleasant 
and flowery way, it is the direct road to light and 
knowledge; and if so, why do we talk about 
thorns, as if we expected to reach any of the 
supreme and permanent blessings of life, tread- 
ing all the distance on turf and roses? We are 
glad that the glorious bard made it his adopted 
path, and that he pursued it with sountired,sofor- 
ward, and so firm a step. We would not have had 
him write on any other subjects but those which 
not only singularly involved the destinies of Eng- 
land at the period of his writing, but are insepa- 
rably connected with the present, future, lasting 
welfare of the world. He was peculiarly fitted to 
elicit and establish truth by controversy ; which, 
we are persuaded, is its proper and almost its 
pee office.”

sand German varieties,) and professedly worldly

the essentials, and vital doctrines of Chris- 
tianity, in. order to please those who reject

not been given, there can be no doubt at all that

Philalethes.—Yet surely the annual meetings.

Justus.—They might be if they were differently

Anastasius.—The system of the annual meet- 
ings is one of pure religious excitement, and 
therefore requires to be powerfully controlled, or 
they must generate what is mischievous. The 
parties who attend come neither for the purpose 
of hearing truth, nor do they love it when they 
hear it. If any person states his views of the 
objects of the Society, or of the mode in which 
they should be promoted in a different way from 
that which flatters the Committee, they hiss, and:

cry, “ off, off,” as the audience at a playhouse 
do. aii dnpoptlar. mountebank ; thereby fully:

that these meetings were like “ theological rope- 
dancing.” There is enough of truth, and of 
devotion in some speakers, of benevolence, and 
of real good effected, to lead men away if 
they will take their own feelings and judgments. 
for their guides instead of the word of God. 
Some of the most spiritual men with whom I 
am acquainted never will attend the meetings of 
the Societies, from perceiving the worldly- 
mindedness which pervades them; and from 
being disgusted with the cant of religion which 
is mixed up withit. Indeed it must strike every 
attentive observer as an alarming characteristic 
of these meetings, that the poor in this world’s 
goods who are rich in faith, never attend them ;. 
nor did I ever hear of their attendance being 
requested ; while every exertion is made to coax: 
the attendance of a rich man let his faith be 
what it may. |

. Anastasius:—The meetings must hear pro-

that from them, the knowledge of which we be- 
lieve to be important to the well-being of their 
souls: and to hold it back shews that we prefer 
the applause of a platform to our duty to baie 
or to love of our brethren.

~ Anastasius.—The object which we propose 
in doing any thing is that alone which makes 
our exertions to bring it to pass, wise or

only of an automaton, or. of an idiot. Every 
one of these Societies has an object, which it 
dees not deny: they have got hold of two texts;

the waters cover the sea:’” whence they draw 
the unwarrantable inference that this knowledge 
is to bé produced. by their exertions. The logic 
is bad; but the theology is worse; and in order

fsocrates.—The greatest evil ar amongst

They are proud too of the title of Religious

Eugenius.—I am quite convinced that the re-

 Aristo.—The great object which we ought to

all the demonstration of Christ himself. The

vealed. Another great point is to guard heragainst. 
heresies which arise out of this subject, and espe-

appears to be so like it, that Satan leads her in 
_ her present ignorance into this error; and there-

tance of the views set forth by Theologus, which 
shew the necessary relations between Creator 
and creature, as the only views which can deliver

the creation being in misery, and the other in 
blessedness, proving that it has been in the 
power, and at the disposal of a being above and 
out of itself. This is thé key of the whole doc- 
trine of election, and of the refutation of the 
Universalists. Satan is particularly busy upon 
this point of Universalism, and therefore it is 
of great importance wherever the millennial 
doctrines are preached, to preach this. 

Apostacy is twofold ; Papal and Infidel. So- 
dom is Popery, (Rev. xi. 8.) and we should

fore we ought to pray for its destruction, whilst

With respect to the infidel apostacy, of which 
expediency is the covert, we ought always to 
contend that nothing is expedient but what is 
lawful. This expediency comes under the 
name of usefulness, and when usefulness is 
propounded to us, we ought to ask, “is it 
duty?” Whenever any thing has been pro- 
posed to me to do, or to omit doing, it has 
always been on the score of increasing or dimi- 
nishing my usefulness, never on the score of 
my duty. I cannot express what my feeling 
is. of the infidelity of these times; the whole 
church is poisoned by it; it is bringing an 
influence to bear upon the state, and upon the 
church, which absolutely incapacitates them 
from living a life of faith. In the soul of man 
there is a mixture of barrenness and fertility, 
and seeing a judgment of a famine of faith 
upon the church, we must not attempt im- 
possible things. We must not wonder that 
we do not attain to the stature of our fathers 
because there is a barrenness in the soil, and 
seeing the judgment of infidelity on the church 
we must acquiesce; and I do not say this to 
discourage hope, but to direct the spirit into 
the right channel for staying the plague. Is 
not this infidelity a judgment of God? Our 
newspapers, reviews, and all our periodicals 
carry this poison and’ infuse it into every pore. 
I speak this to Christians who know and feel

 Anastasius:—As in the time of Luther the 
great point of duty for every Christian was to 
emancipate himself from the chains of super- 
stition, so the great point of duty now. is. to 
free ourselves from the more subtle, and there- 
fore more delusive dangers of infidelity. This 
is the last form of Satan’s device, and his 
Strongest: it is by the exercise of this that 
he is now destroying the church, and it is for 
the exercise of it that he himself is bound at 
last.. There cannot be a greater curse upon a 
highly civilized nation than unsanctified bene- 
volence in the church, leading Christian men 
to unite with infidels for any purpose whatever ; 
and unsanctified learning in the people puffing 
them up with intellectual pride and incapaci- 
tating them from the humility of faith. - We 
have an instance of the combination of both 
these in the London University, of which I was 
fequested to become a subscriber, in order, as 
the person who asked me said, to help to coun- 
teract the infidel spirit. Another. person. says 
he remains in it in order to be a drag upon 
its infidel wheels. But the Word of God does 
Rot say, “mix with infidels to drag their 
wheels,” but’ “come out from among them and 
be ye separate,- and touch not the unclean 
thing.” 

Aristo. —When the Lord perceives any of his 
saints. suffering for the whole, as Daniel. did, 
and above all as the Lord Jesus did, it is- to 


333 


such a one that he will give the faith, know- 
ledge, power, and unction by which the refresh- 
ment will be communicated to the whole, if 
decreed, or if not, he will be taken away from 
the evil to come. This is the attitude of the 
spirit in which God will confer his gifts; God’s 
face is now turned from us, but it will be turned 
to us again ; nor do I press upon you what I do 
not perform myself, for I have been in earnest 
prayer upon this matter for above a year, and 
doubt not but many others have been so like- 
wise ; but these are times in which God is not 
easily found, for he is angry with his Church. 
With respect to our duty towards individuals, 
it is a sore trial to the flesh to have more 
light on any point than your brethren: “ This 
is the condemnation, that light is come into 
the world, and men love darkness rather than 
light because their deeds are evil:” and it has 
been uniformly found that the lowest forms 
in the school of theology do persecute the 
higher more than they who aré not in the 
school at all; who being full of forms and 
substitutions for the substance as yet have not 
full knowledge of God. To have more light 
in divine things than his fellows is the cruci- 
fixion of a man if he be meek, and the driving 
him mad if he be without meeknéss; to break 
his heart if he is tender, and make him dis- 
tracted if he his bold. Your friends, your con- 
gregations, your brethren will all act against 
you if you testify to what they do not believe: 


334 


but God whom you serve, will after you have 
suffered awhile for his truth, give deliverance. 
I find great comfort in considering the ways 
of God, and in this city of London, which is 
the great antagonist of Babylon, God has some 
enlightened men. Popery could make no head 
but by liberalism, which is destroying all things : 
our anathema of popery is the palladium of 
our country, like the standard in a battle. 

The Lord has delivered his church of old by 
a woman, and often by the most unlikely 
means, and he probably may again; there 
wants only the gift of faith. It is not in the 
little activity, but in the large and capacious 
heart that the work before God is to be done. 
A heart rightly feeling out of love, and rightly 
seeing the present condition of the church and 
nation, that is service to God; and also not 
in despair nor fear, but full of devout acknow- 
ledgment of his mercy and grace, is far more 
serviceable than the outward service of a king. 
The Arminian spirit of the time which cries 
work, work, work, stole from us all that faith 
and fulness of union with God, and close com- 
munion with Christ, which is the true offer- 
ing of the church, and the great act of use- 
fulness: and this is what I recommend to every 
one, firstly, secondly, and thirdly. Then we 
would work with effect. When a man or 
woman is thus occupied with God, when the 
‘seed is watered by faith, and fulness of faith, 
then our. actions tell; then our activity is 


336 


valuable to the church; then it is that words, 
lay or clerical, are spirit and life, and are 
mighty; then it is God uses ‘us as his instru- 
ment; then every offering is acceptable, like 
the widow’s mite, because it is so exceedingly 
precious. But again I repeat, it is the Arminian 
spirit of the times which has been the secret 
agency of these societies. 

I caution you against want of boldness; my 
own conscience reproves me not for shrinking 
from fear of an enemy, but from love of man: 
and I must again declare, that the views of the 
Creator and creature, on which we have often 
conversed, are the life of my soul, and all my 
grief is that I do not more unreservedly see 
my sufficiency in God. The capacity of re- 
ceiving faith in any matter is a great rarity; 
and the greatest service which laymen can do 
to a minister is to testify that what we are 
Saying is not a delusion, and that they are 
ready to go to the stake for it.

whatever. If they are, they wilfully disobey

Anastasius.—The most awful proof is con- 
tained in the petitions which the whole ẹvan- 
gelical body, whether in the Church of England 
or amongst the Dissenters, sent to Parliament 
for the repeal of the Test Act. In those peti- 
tions they broadly maintain the position that 
a Christian, in whom God the Holy Ghost 
dwells, whose will and affections are conformed 
to the mind of God, is no more worthy of trust 
in a Christian land, than one in whom Satan 
dwells, whose will and affections are wholly 
opposed to the mind of God, and under the 
guidance of the infernal spirit. This tre- 
mendous avowal consisting not only of a denial 
of the Holy Ghost, but actually reducing Him 
to a level with Satan, runs through nearly 
every one of the petitions, with more or less 
clearness ; a few samples of which you may 
here see. “That in the judgment of the peti- 
tioners conscience cannot be called free—so 
long as any opinion whatever on matter of re- 
ligion, subjects its professors to—privations

and disabilities ; that whether freedom of con- 
science be violated i in the persons of Protestant 
Dissenters, Roman Catholics, or unbelievers— 
such violation is inconsistent with the spirit 
of the Gospel.” — Parl. Votes, No. 66. | 

“That no man in any country ought to be 
excluded from any public situation for which 
he is qualified merely on account of the reli- 
gious opinions he may entertain or express,” 
(i.e. an avowed Atheist). HD, 78. 95. 184. 
195. 229. 293. 350. 

«That it is repugnant to the dictates of

purely religious.”—Dztfo, 600. 

If avowed infidels, who believe neither in the 
existence of the Holy Ghost, nor of the devil, 
had put forth such sentiments, there would 
have been no inconsistency in their so 
doing: but these petitioners call themselves 
orthodox Christians ; they therefore believe that 
they themselves are the temples of the Holy 
Ghost; that all who are not Christians have 
the devil ruling in them; and yet they deli- 
berately declare in the face of Christendom, 
that one is no more fit than another to perform 
the duties of citizenship in a Christian state ! 

Philalethes—I am truly amazed: if this be

account of his opinions, but that they do not:

why should any one not be chosen to ‘fill an 
office? because he is a blasphemer ; therefore 
not eligible: to confer eligibility, and at the

Philalethes.—You seem to form a low esti- 
mate of the religion of Dissenters. 

Anastasius.—As I am conscious of feeling no 
partiality whatever for any Christian on account 
of the sect to which- he belongs, but love all 
equally in which I find uncompromising love of 
Christ, however small the attainments in theo- 
logical science may be, and however unattrac- 
tive the manners, and exterior deportment, I 
have no hesitation in answering your question 
plainly and fully. The term, “ Dissenter,” is 
no longer properly applicable to denote a reli- 
gious, but a political body. Do not, however, 
mistake my meaning. I admit that the most 
Spiritually minded Christians, and those who 
nearest resemble the character of our blessed 
Lord, are to be found in country cottages, and 
in the cellars and garrets of the obscurest courts 
and alleys in this metropolis; and that these 
received their first impressions of religious truth 
at the hands of Dissenters, although they have 
advanced subsequently far beyond their teachers. 


339 


But these persons are Dissenters from neces- 
sity, rather than from choice, and are quite 
distinct from the influential heads of the dis- 
senting body. Speaking of this body as a 
whole, acting through its head, or many heads, 
it is undeniable that it is infidel in religion, and 
radical in politics. Nor are these easily sepa- 
rated, although there are some exceptions, such 
as Hume; and although among the absurdities © 
which are every day newly broached, Dissenters 
are very fond of denying that religious opinions 
have any influence on political conduct ; to refute 
which folly, it might be sufficient to appeal to the 
unanimous voice of history ; and there we should 
find that religious opinions have invariably 
been the greatest exciters to political conduct. 
“ In truth,” says Lord Redesdale, “ religious 
opinions, and political opinions, are on many 
points so intimately united, that they cannot 
be separated : they have at all times, and in all 
countries, acted upon each other.” p. 68. Fora 
proof of the infidel character of the Dissenting 
body, observe its zeal for the London Uni- 
versity. See, too, the whole tenor of the lan- 
guage and proceedings of the Society, absurdly 
calling itself, “ for the Protection of Civil and 
Religious Liberty :” which liberty is in far greater 
danger of being destroyed by radicals and in- 
fidels, than by any efforts on the part of the 
rulers of the land. This Society hunts out any 
trifling occurrence, exaggerates and distorts it, 
adds its own comments and glosses, until it 
becomes sufficiently pungent to suit the de- 


340 


praved appetite for evil speaking, lying and 
slandering, of the market to which it panders: it 
limits its venom to those cases which can be 
tortured into the means of attack on the ma- 
gistracy or hierarchy, while no persecution, how- 
ever gross, inflicted by ‘“ the Board,” no viola- 
tion of Religious Liberty, by one Dissenter over 
another, is noticed. You may judge also by - 
the style which any journalist adopts, who is 
to gain his livelihood by selling his paper 
amongst Dissenters: such people know well 
what fits the taste of those for whom they write, 
for a journal is not an instructor of the people, 
but an index of the sentiments of the party in 
which it circulates. Observe, likewise, that at 
the midnight orgies with which they celebrated 
the repeal of what they styled, “ the Sacramental 
Test,” they themselves selected a well known 
Socinian to return thanks in the name of all the 
Protestant Dissenting Ministers of England 
as their most appropriate representative; and 
moreover, chose as their Vice-President on the 
- occasion another Socinian. Their pious ances- 
tors would have stood aghast at such an exhibi- 
tion on such an occasion. Had they obtained 
some great public benefit, a day of thanksgiving 
would have been appointed in acknowledgment 
of the kindness of their God. But their chil- 
dren, grown wiser than their fathers, have caught 
the liberal spirit of the age, and seem almost 
to avow, that God has no claim on their services, 
except at the meeting-house, or on the platform. 
The Three Denominations now meet as a body 


341 


composed of Socinians, Baptist, and Independent 
ministers. The Bible, says he who denies the 
Divinity of our Lord, is a minister of Satan ; 
and shall the ministers of Jesus Christ be found 
recognizing such as “ Brethren,” speaking of 
them as united in the same cause, and publicly 
avowing their union before the world? What 
political object, what pitiful advantage can they 
gain by these means, to compensate for the 
awful dereliction of principles which it involves ? 
“ What fellowship hath righteousness with un- 
righteousness? and what union hath light with 
darkness? and what concord hath Christ with 
Belial? or.what part hath he that believeth. with 
an infidel?” Can such passages as these have 
ever suggested themselves to the attention of 
those who. are connected: with the London Uni- 
versity, or with the late eating and drinking 
bout in honour of the pen of the Sacramental 
Test? 

. Justus.—The principles now. avowedly acted 
on by the Dissenters, are substantially the same 
as those which actuated the Heathen Romans. 
Gibbon . says, “ the various modes of worship 
which prevailed in the Roman World, were all 
considered by the people as equally true, by 
the philosopher as equally false, and by the 
magistrate as equally useful.” Their maxims 
are diametrically opposed to those of the 
Non-conformists ; and whatever may be averred

modern Dissenters. have yielded to the influence

VOL. II. AA 


346 


Wesley. Both these eminent Christians were men _ 
of warm affections, fervent imaginations, and,

addressed to the passions. Their aim was sud- 
den and violent excitement, the very. opposite 
of the aim in the discourses of our Lord and. 
the Apostles, and of our old divines, whose 
plan was to open up and explain difficult texts. 
and large portions of Scripture. Whitefield, 
and Wesley also probably knew that it was 
more easy to convince the judgment than to 
turn the heart; and hence they possibly fell 
into the extreme of addressing the latter ex- 
clusively. The tone thus given by them has 
more or less pervaded their successors in every. 
denomination, and hence Christians are exceed- 
ingly ill versed in all the great fundamental 
doctrines contained in our formularies, such as 
the Assembly’s Catechism, the Westminster 
Confession, Lambeth Articles, &c. In proof of

of the magazines, whenever a sermon is preached.

of theology: the novelty of the doctrine is- ‘a.

Anastasius.—I cannot help calling your atten- 
tion. once more to that form of error which is. 
rising out of the Protestant, and especially out 
of the Dissenting Churches. If we could induce 
pious men to remember that in the same way 
in which every individual has some peculiar 
propensity running, - unless strictly guarded. 
' against, into sin, every community has likewise 
a bias towards some particular evil, they would 
be on the watch, and thence earlier perceive 
the sin which has been generated by an excess 
of. Protestant: principles. As an undue. defer. 
rence. for, human authority led to. priestcraft.: 
and, various. other abuses in Popery, so; has the. . 
unlimited, exercise of the right of private judgr — 
ment led to. an undue contempt for. authority. - 
in Protestantism : and as Popery as a system — 
buried the. truth of God under ceremonies and- 


traditions, so has Protestantism as a system rë- 
nounced the truth of God, in neglecting the or- 
dinanees by which that truth was to be pre- 
served. Thus Bigotry and Superstition were 
the handmaids of Popery ; while self-conceit and 
scepticism have accompanied Protestantism. 
Thus will God permit the Protestant Churches: 
to-bring to its full growth and: completeness this 
last monster of infidel apostacy, that it may be 
irrefragably proved to demonstration, that no 
form of human society, as at present constituted, 
can do otherwise than rush into some form of 
rebellion against Him. The most unbounded 
toleration from temporal rulers; the amplest 
possession of the written word of God in every 
one’s hands; large bodies of preachers of all de- 
grees of talent, and rank in society; extreme 
circulation of books, tracts, and journals ;. uni- 
versal cultivation of the intellect; increased and 
increasing knowledge of all arts and sciences ; 
‘in short, exactly such a state as Utopian Chris- 
‘tran philosophers and statesmen have feigned 
in their imaginations to be the most favourable 
for the advancement of religion and piety, shall 
be shewn incapable of producing a reignof righte- 
ousness and peace; while, on the contrary, it shall 
bring out the most open, and unblushing denial 
‘of God’s ruling amongst the children of men 
which any nation ever declared; nay, even all 
that Evangelicalism could produce out of this 
state, most favourable to its exercise, is a kingly 
government without Christ as its acknowledged’ 
head ; a church in which apostacy and infidelity

against a people and nation that is heedless of 
the glory of Christ; and giveth to another that 
worship and service which is due to him alone. 
Let the page of history which is the record of 
God’s providences, instruct Christian Britain, 
and instruct Protestant Britain what she hath 
to expect if she should in any way countenance 
or favour the apostacies with which we are sur- 
rounded. I am jealous for the honour of our 
Fathers, who built up with the sweat. of their 
long suffering toil, and watered with their blood 
this constitution of church and state, for that 
they are indissolubly one, under which we have 
enjoyed such blessings and achieved such won- 
ders, and under whose divine canopy such a 
multitude of our fathers have been gathered into 
glory. Iam jealous for the supineness, for the 
ignorance, for the indifference, to which we are 
grown, and the dishonourable reproaches which 
are heaped upon the work of our fathers. I am 
fearful for my children and for your children, 
and for the children of the daughters of my peéo- 
ple, lest the Lord’s judgments should overtake 
them with near fury and destroying haste; and 
VOL. ll. BB 


362 


being appointed a watchman in Zion, I am pre- 
pared, the Lord helping me, toclear my own soul, 
and free myself fromthe blood ofall men. Fłook 
back into the annals of our church, and I find 
our fathers standing in the battle order and 
sleepless jealousy against Rome’s diabolical 
cursing and intrigue. I read the discourses 
of my predecessors in the church, and } 
find them interpreting the course of pro- 
vidence, instructing the church to behold the 
habitation of the throne of righteousness in the 
midst of the clouds and darkness with which 
our King is coming on. And, therefore, I am 
encouraged and emboldened and compelled to 
lift though it were a solitary voice to this city 
and to the inhabitants of it, and to you the chil- 
dren of a much tried church, because I am. as- 
sured the Lord hath not forsaken us, but will 
draw out good men and true to lift up the 
trampled banner of the faith, and display it 
against the liberality which now cometh sweep- 
ing over our land and hath overwhelmed us to 
the very neck. It is very sweet to me to open 
the works of Christ in the gospel, and the words 
of the Spirit in the Epistles, and any and every 
part of the testimony of Jesus: and you will 
acquit me of wandering from the proper object, 
while I slip out into a wider field to do some 
service to the Protestant Church, and warn the 
Protestant nation by shewing them the judg- 
_ ments which God ever brought upon an apostate 
or lukewarm church. I pray you therefore to 
be faithful, to embrace the truth, and to stand 


363 


for it against all hazards, and to have no fellow- 
ship with the unfruitful works of darkness. . 
Anastasius.—Infidelity has so over-run the 
church, and pious men are so active in joining 
with, and seconding the plansof infidels, that they 
do more evil by scattering infidelity with the right. 
hand, than they do good by the clouded and mi- 
tigated gospel which they encourage with the left. 
So much closer is the alliance of some of the 
Lord’s children to infidels than to the essential 
peculiarities of the gospel, that if the time were 
to come wherein the infidels should have power 
to drag Christians to the stake, I should not be 
surprised to see some of their brethren carrying 
the faggots which were to destroy them. From 
this lamentable state nothing but the preaching 
of the coming judgments can emancipate the 
churches, and it is subject of unfeigned joy, 
and praise, and thanksgiving to Almighty God, 
that he has been graciously pleased to hear and 
answer the prayer of his servants, that he would 
open the eyes -of some of their fellows to see 
the signs of the times in which we live. But we 
must remember, that to see and to believe in the 
coming kingdom of Jesus, and in the judgments 
which are to introduce it, is a special gift: it is 
faith in his coming which the Lord signifies to 
us is that which is to be exceeding rare. Chris- 
tians of all degrees of knowledge and light in other 
branches of theology receive and reject it: thus 
the Lord shews his sovereignty in this as in 
every other of his gifts. Furthermore let us 
bear in mind, that the appearance of the Lord 


364 


will be in a manner perfectly unexpected,’ by 
those .who shall be most on the watch for him; 
and that we shall receive only day by day this 


most precious manna of the signs of the coming 
kingdom, and not enough for two days together. 
A very great enquiry is now excited: the works. 
of Mr. Marsh, Mr. Noel, and Mr. Irving have- 
been blessed to many. Still, however, some _ 
hold out, and refuse even to enquire: the Lord.. 
pardon them and give them repentance to the. 


acknowledgment of his will. In the mean time, 


No hope have they, nor fear of aught to come, 
As well for them had Prophecy been dumb, 
They could have held the conduct they pursue, 
Had Saul of Tarsus lived and died a Jew. 

And Truth proposed to reasoners wise as they, 
Is a pearl cast——completely cast away. 


END OF VOL. II.
